\section{The question and the contribution}\label{ste:mx:sec:intro}

A matrix can be close to complete averaging and still retain an exact arithmetic
signal. Closeness controls its magnitude; it need not decide whether that
magnitude is exactly zero. This note makes that distinction quantitative,
constructive, and dimension-optimal for a natural polynomial realization
problem.

The central research question is the following. Suppose all transition matrices
are rational, strictly positive, doubly stochastic, commuting, invertible, and
uniformly close to the same averaging matrix. Does this combination make finite
exact reachability of the stationary value decidable? The answer is no. More
precisely, these constraints can all be imposed on a \emph{fixed} alphabet whose
initial distribution varies on a very simple rational curve, with an
undecidable exact-hit problem. The proof also shows exactly where the arithmetic
lives: in small nilpotent parts of the matrices after their common exponential
decay has been removed.

\subsection{Results at a glance}

The main realization theorem, Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift}, preserves the entire
polynomial value up to a known positive exponential factor. Its matching lower
bound is Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:optimal}. In particular, positivity and arbitrary
prescribed mixing speed cost just one state beyond the polynomial's minimal
linear realization. The scalar readout is an actual coordinate probability,
not a signed linear functional disguised as acceptance.

Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:quartic} translates a derivative-space bound into a sharp state
budget for all quartics, including nonhomogeneous ones. Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:curve}
then proves universality with a quadratic input curve and fourth-order joint
nilpotence. Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:line} gives a different tradeoff: a straight-line
input family, fifth-order joint nilpotence, and a quadratic state bound in the
number of Diophantine variables. The latter construction uses a positive-value
filter to keep the external input affine rather than quadratic.

The reverse construction in Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:converse} recovers an ordinary
polynomial directly from promised matrix data. Thus the degree restriction is
not just a feature of one encoding. Finally, Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:robust} separates
exact critical-target reachability from effective finite-search problems at
positive distance from that target.

\subsection{Relationship to existing work}

The selected ProveIt sources were reviewed at repository snapshot
\texttt{44983ed7ebfd} (the full hash appears in Appendix B). The
Hilbert-tenth-problem README records its separate complete-certificate and universal-polynomial
arithmetic bounds as 75 and 87 operations, respectively~\cite{repoH10}.
Those ledgers are not state counts. No comparison in this article lowers either
bound.

\wtag\ Both figures are still those of the project README at this write (\path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/README.md}: ``75 operations for a complete certificate and 87 for one universal polynomial''); the source's pin is kept as provenance.

The six-substrate review includes an ``Exact Erasure'' construction using
strictly positive stochastic matrices and mortality, and carefully separates
bounded certificates from universal interfaces~\cite{review1977}. Our selected
route is different: every generator and every finite product is invertible, so
full erasure is impossible. The hard event is equality of \emph{one coordinate}
with its equilibrium probability. Commutativity, a common uniform stationary
law, and minimal translation-module dimension are built into the construction.
This is a review of the cited files, not a claim to have audited every report in
the repository.

\wtag\ The ``Exact Erasure'' construction of that review is the manuscript printed as Part~\ref{ste:part:ee} of this report (source~02); the review is~\cite{review1977}. Inside this report the comparison is direct: Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift} below uses Part~I's mass-zero embedding and contraction argument (Section~\ref{ste:mx:sec:pointers}), and the exact events differ. Part~I's predicate is rank one of a product, so that all distinction is erased; here no product erases (Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:noerasure}) and the predicate is one coordinate at its equilibrium value.

Undecidability for commuting probabilistic finite automata is already known.
Bell proves strict and non-strict emptiness undecidable even for polynomially
ambiguous commutative automata~\cite{bell}. Bell and Semukhin study exact
cutpoint reachability and related small-alphabet restrictions~\cite{bell-semukhin}.
Our strictly positive automata have exponentially many accepting paths and do
\emph{not} preserve polynomial ambiguity. The older conversion of weighted
representations to stochastic ones is associated with
Turakainen~\cite{turakainen}; the present proof instead prints the specific
mass-zero embedding needed for our dimension, readout, and mixing claims.

\wtag\ The same embedding, in column convention, is Part~I's positive affine lift (Theorem~\ref{ste:ee:thm:lift}, with its doubly stochastic case Corollary~\ref{ste:ee:cor:double}); see Section~\ref{ste:mx:sec:pointers}.

Hankel rank is the classical measure of minimal weighted realization dimension;
Balle and Mohri give the standard theorem and reconstruction
perspective~\cite{balle-mohri}. Polynomial derivative spaces also belong to
Macaulay inverse-system and apolar-algebra theory~\cite{jelisiejew,masuti-rossi}.
We use elementary proofs of the specialized facts needed here. The quartic
Diophantine reduction relies on the classical MRDP theorem; Davis gives a
primary account~\cite{davis}. None of these foundational ingredients is
presented as newly discovered.

\subsection{What kind of computational substrate is this?}

The substrate is a \emph{finite controlled stochastic system with exact
acceptance}. A finite word chooses which Markov matrix is applied at each
step. The existential question asks whether \emph{some} finite control word
has a designated exact output. It is a Diophantine recognizer for an unbounded
existential search, not an autonomous finite Markov chain simulating a Turing
machine step by step.

For every fixed input and every supplied finite word, its acceptance probability
is computable by rational arithmetic. Undecidability concerns the unbounded
existence of a suitable word as the input varies. In particular, no claim is
made that membership of a supplied word in a fixed rational automaton's exact
cutpoint language is undecidable.

\subsection{Second routes and pointers}\label{ste:mx:sec:pointers}

\wtag\ This subsection was written when source~11 was added to the report. Four arguments of this Part are results already in the collection. They are printed in place with the source's own proofs, as second routes, and no novelty is claimed for them.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The lift.} Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift} is the doubly stochastic lift of Corollary~\ref{ste:ee:cor:double} (Part~I) applied to the commuting unipotent translation operators $A_i$ of \eqref{ste:mx:eq:linrep}. Part~I works with column-stochastic matrices acting on column distributions and writes $S_w=J_{d+1}+K^{-T}EA_wF$ with an integer scale $K$; this Part works with row distributions and writes $M_w=J_s+q^LFA_wE$ in \eqref{ste:mx:eq:wordproduct} with a rational scale $q$, so the roles of $E$ and $F$ are transposed. Both contraction proofs split off a multiple of the averaging matrix and use a nonnegative stochastic remainder (Proposition~\ref{ste:ee:prop:contraction}). New here are the choice of a translation module as the represented space, the readout of one actual coordinate through $E\e_1=b$, the matching lower bound $s\geq r(P)+1$ (Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:optimal}), the quartic and quintic budgets, the fixed-alphabet input curves and the converse.
\item \emph{The gate front end.} Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:gate} is \texttt{cdc:wf:lem:quadratization} of~\cite{cdc}, the unique-extension quadratization, which Parts~I--III also use.
\item \emph{The binomial step.} The expansion \eqref{ste:mx:eq:recover} in the proof of Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:converse} is the commuting multi-letter form of \texttt{cdc:pt:prop:unipotent} of~\cite{cdc}.
\item \emph{No horizon.} Corollary~\ref{ste:mx:cor:nohorizon} is the bounded-search argument of \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound}, which every Part of this report applies (Section~\ref{ste:sec:spine}).
\end{itemize}
Two comparisons with Part~I are new. First, the generators here commute, as in Theorem~\ref{ste:ee:thm:commuting}, where commutation makes \emph{erasure} decidable with horizon $n-1$. Here erasure never occurs at all (Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:noerasure}), yet the single-coordinate equilibrium hit stays computably enumerable complete on a fixed alphabet (Theorems~\ref{ste:mx:thm:curve} and~\ref{ste:mx:thm:line}): commutation controls the rank of products, not the value of one coordinate. Second, Part~I's research question of Section~\ref{ste:ee:q:fixedalphabet} asked for one fixed stochastic alphabet with an ordinary input interface. This Part supplies one for the exact-hit predicate, at MRDP existence level and without a printed numerical alphabet (dated note under that question). Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:robust} turns Part~I's remark that $\eta^T\leq\varepsilon$ works for every instance into a decision procedure for separated targets.

Within the collection, Part~V of~\cite{pqc} (source~17 there, \emph{Arithmetic of Rapidly Mixing Reversible Computation}) proves an exact stationary equality undecidable for reversible chains with a uniform spectral gap on a countably infinite state space. Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:nonnormal} concerns finite chains with a single transient eigenvalue; the two statements do not conflict.

\section{Conventions and the exact-hit model}\label{ste:mx:sec:model}

Throughout, $\N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. Vectors representing distributions are rows.
An $s\times s$ matrix $M$ is row-stochastic when $M\one_s=\one_s$ and all its
entries are nonnegative. It is doubly stochastic when also
$\one_s^TM=\one_s^T$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:uniform}
 u_s=\frac1s\one_s^T,\qquad J_s=\frac1s\one_s\one_s^T,
 \qquad H_s=\{v\in\Q^{1\times s}:v\one_s=0\}.
\end{equation}
Then $u_s$ is stationary for every doubly stochastic matrix. The matrix $J_s$
is complete averaging, and $I_s-J_s$ is the projection onto mass-zero
information.

Fix letters $a_1,\ldots,a_k$ with transition matrices $M_1,\ldots,M_k$.
For a word $w=a_{i_1}\cdots a_{i_L}$, set
$M_w=M_{i_1}\cdots M_{i_L}$, and set $M_{\varnothing}=I_s$. The accepting
state will always be state 1:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:accdef}
 \acc_p(w)=pM_w\e_1,\qquad \lambda_* =u_s\e_1=\frac1s.
\end{equation}
When the matrices commute, only the count vector
$n=(n_1,\ldots,n_k)$ matters. We use
$M^n=M_1^{n_1}\cdots M_k^{n_k}$ and $|n|=\sum_i n_i$.
Every count vector is represented by the canonical word
$a_1^{n_1}\cdots a_k^{n_k}$, including the all-zero vector.

The exact equilibrium-hit question is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:hit}
 \exists n\in\N^k\quad pM^n\e_1=\lambda_*.
\end{equation}
The matrices, initial distribution, and target are exact rational objects.
Replacing equality by numerical closeness changes this problem fundamentally.

For two probability rows, total variation is
$\TV(p,p')=\frac12\norm{p-p'}_1$. We will impose the stronger, common-minorization
condition
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin}
 \frac{1-\theta}{s} < (M_i)_{ab} < \frac{1+\theta}{s}
 \qquad(0<\theta<1)
\end{equation}
for every entry of every matrix. This both gives strict positivity and provides
an explicit contraction estimate for arbitrary switching words.

\section{Translation spaces, finite differences, and minimal rank}

\begin{definition}
For a nonzero polynomial $P\in\Q[x_1,\ldots,x_k]$, its translation space and
translation rank are
\[
 \calV(P)=\Span_{\Q}\{P(x+m):m\in\N^k\},\qquad
 r(P)=\dim_{\Q}\calV(P).
\]
For a collection $\mathcal P$ of bounded-degree polynomials, define
$\calV(\mathcal P)$ using all translates of all members and write
$r(\mathcal P)=\dim\calV(\mathcal P)$.
\end{definition}

The coefficients may be negative; positivity enters only after the linear
representation has been constructed. All spaces in this section are over
$\Q$. For a finite supplied family, the bases needed below can be computed by
exact Gaussian elimination; the parameter families used later have explicit
finite generating sets.

\begin{lemma}\label{ste:mx:lem:derivatives}
If $P$ has total degree $d$, then
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:spans}
 \calV(P)=\Span\{\Delta^\alpha P:|\alpha|\leq d\}
          =\Span\{\partial^\alpha P:|\alpha|\leq d\},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta_i f(x)=f(x+\e_i)-f(x)$. The same statement holds for a family.
Every nonzero translation space contains the constant polynomial 1.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Taylor expansion shows that every translate lies in the derivative span.
Conversely, on the space of polynomials of degree at most $d$, differentiation
and finite difference satisfy the finite identities
\[
 1+\Delta_i=\exp(\partial_i),\qquad
 \partial_i=\log(1+\Delta_i)
   =\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}j\Delta_i^j.
\]
These are identities of nilpotent operators, so convergence is irrelevant.
Each difference of a polynomial is a linear combination of its nonnegative
integer translates, and repeated differences remain such combinations. The
logarithm identity therefore puts every derivative in the translation span.
Products of the commuting operators give the multivariable assertion.
Finally, differentiating a suitable top-degree monomial of a nonzero member
produces a nonzero constant. Rescaling gives 1.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{ste:mx:lem:grid}
Evaluation on $\N^k$ is injective on $\Q[x_1,\ldots,x_k]$. Consequently the
infinite matrix
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:hankel}
 \mathcal H_P(m,n)=P(m+n),\qquad m,n\in\N^k,
\end{equation}
has rank $r(P)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A univariate polynomial vanishing at every natural number is zero. For several
variables, regard the polynomial as a polynomial in the last variable; fixing
the others at natural values forces every coefficient to vanish there, and
induction completes the argument. The rows of $\mathcal H_P$ are precisely the
evaluations of the translates of $P$. An injective linear evaluation map
preserves their span dimension.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The row-oriented linear representation}

Choose a basis $f_1,\ldots,f_r$ of $V=\calV(P)$, or of a joint family space.
For each coordinate direction, let $T_i$ be defined by
\[
 (f_1(x+\e_i),\ldots,f_r(x+\e_i))
       =(f_1(x),\ldots,f_r(x))T_i.
\]
Set
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:linrep}
 \begin{gathered}
 A_i=T_i^T,\qquad
 b=(f_1(0),\ldots,f_r(0))^T,\qquad
 P=\sum_j (a_P)_j f_j,\\
 a_P A_1^{n_1}\cdots A_k^{n_k}b=P(n).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The last displayed identity uses $a_P$ as a row. The matrices $A_i$ commute.
They are unipotent: every eigenvalue is 1. Indeed, $A_i-I_r$ represents a finite
difference operator, up to the harmless transpose convention.

\begin{proposition}\label{ste:mx:prop:depth}
Let $d$ be the largest degree occurring in the nonzero joint family space.
For $N_i=A_i-I_r$, every product $N^\alpha$ with $|\alpha|=d+1$ is zero,
whereas some product with $|\alpha|=d$ is nonzero. In particular, every $A_i$
is invertible and $\det A_i=1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every finite difference lowers total degree by at least one. This proves
vanishing after $d+1$ factors. Choose a member of degree $d$ and a multiindex
of a nonzero monomial of that degree. The corresponding $d$ differences give
a nonzero constant: the coefficient is multiplied by the product of the
factorials of the exponents. Thus a product of length $d$ survives. An
identity plus a nilpotent matrix is invertible with finite geometric inverse,
and all its eigenvalues are 1.
\end{proof}

This is a \emph{joint} depth statement. It does not say that every individual
letter has a Jordan block of size $d+1$. Mixed products of different nilpotent
parts may be needed to detect the full degree.

\subsection{An apolar interpretation}

Let $D=\Q[\partial_1,\ldots,\partial_k]$ act by differentiation. The map
$D\to\calV(P)$ sending $L$ to $LP$ has kernel $\Ann(P)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:apolar}
 r(P)=\dim_{\Q}D/\Ann(P).
\end{equation}
This quotient is an Artinian local algebra. Its maximal ideal is generated by
the images of the derivatives, which are nilpotent. Its socle consists exactly
of the elements whose images under $L\mapsto LP$ are constants, hence is
one-dimensional. This is the familiar Gorenstein inverse-system setting
\cite{jelisiejew,masuti-rossi}. Our stochastic dimension will be the length of
this algebra plus one mass coordinate. A joint family is a module version of
this construction and need not correspond to a one-dimensional socle.

\section{A positive doubly stochastic lift with one extra state}\label{ste:mx:sec:lift}

The next theorem is the main algebraic construction. It is useful to state it
for an already-computed linear representation, because the same transition
matrices can then serve many input polynomials.

\begin{theorem}[Explicit optimal-size lift]\label{ste:mx:thm:lift}
Let $k,r\geq1$, let $A_1,\ldots,A_k\in\Q^{r\times r}$ be commuting
unipotent matrices, let $b\in\Q^{r\times1}$ be nonzero, and $0<\theta<1$ rational. Put $s=r+1$.
There are effectively computable commuting rational matrices $M_i$ satisfying
\eqref{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin}, a rational $q\in(0,\theta)$, and, for every rational
row $a$, a strictly positive probability row $p_a$ and rational
$\varepsilon_a>0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:master}
 p_a M^n\e_1=\frac1s+\varepsilon_a q^{|n|}aA^n b
 \qquad(n\in\N^k).
\end{equation}
Every $M_i$ is doubly stochastic and invertible, with characteristic polynomial
$(X-1)(X-q)^r$. Every switching word of length $L$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:contraction}
 \TV(pM_w,p'M_w)\leq\theta^L\TV(p,p')
\end{equation}
for all probability rows $p,p'$. In particular,
$\TV(pM_w,u_s)\leq\theta^L$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Complete $b$ to an invertible rational $r\times r$ matrix $C$ with first
column $b$. This can be done by adjoining suitable standard basis columns.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:EF}
 E=\begin{bmatrix}C&-C\one_r\end{bmatrix},\qquad
 F=E^T(EE^T)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The matrix $E$ has full row rank and kernel $\Q\one_s$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:EFidentities}
 E\one_s=0,\quad \one_s^TF=0,\quad EF=I_r,\quad
 FE=I_s-J_s,\quad E\e_1=b.
\end{equation}
For completeness, $FE$ is symmetric and idempotent, with image the orthogonal
complement of $\one_s$ and kernel $\Q\one_s$. Hence it is exactly $I_s-J_s$.
The inverse in \eqref{ste:mx:eq:EF} exists because $EE^T$ is positive definite over
$\R$; it has rational entries by ordinary matrix inversion.

Write $K_i=FA_iE$ and let
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:qchoice}
 K_{\max}=\max_{i,a,b}|(K_i)_{ab}|,\qquad
 q=\frac{\theta}{1+sK_{\max}},\qquad M_i=J_s+qK_i.
\end{equation}
Each $K_i$ has zero row sums and zero column sums. Thus each $M_i$ is doubly
stochastic. Moreover,
\[
 |(M_i)_{ab}-1/s|\leq qK_{\max}<\theta/s,
\]
which proves the strict entry bounds. Here $K_{\max}>0$, because $A_i$ is
invertible and $EF=I_r$, so $0<q<\theta$.

The identities $J_sF=0$, $EJ_s=0$, and $EF=I_r$ give
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:wordproduct}
 M_w=J_s+q^L F A_w E
\end{equation}
for every nonempty word of length $L$. The formula also holds for the empty
word: its right side is $J_s+FE=I_s$. Commutation of the $A_i$ implies
commutation of the $M_i$.

For a rational row $a$, put $w=aE$ and choose
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:initial}
 \varepsilon_a=\frac{1}{s(1+\norm{w}_\infty)},\qquad
 p_a=u_s+\varepsilon_a w.
\end{equation}
The entries of $w$ sum to zero, and
$|\varepsilon_a w_j|<1/s$, so $p_a$ is a strictly positive probability row.
Now multiply \eqref{ste:mx:eq:wordproduct} by $p_a$ and $\e_1$. The uniform part gives
$1/s$, and the centered part gives
\[
 \varepsilon_a q^L aEF A_wE\e_1
   =\varepsilon_a q^L aA_wb,
\]
which is \eqref{ste:mx:eq:master}. Any other positive rational $\varepsilon$ for which
$u_s+\varepsilon aE$ is strictly positive is equally valid.

The row map $a\mapsto aE$ is an isomorphism $\Q^{1\times r}\to H_s$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:conjugacy}
 (aE)M_i=q(aA_i)E.
\end{equation}
Thus $M_i$ acts as 1 on the uniform direction and as $qA_i$ on $H_s$. This
proves its stated characteristic polynomial and $\det M_i=q^r\ne0$.

Finally, define
$R_i=(M_i-(1-\theta)J_s)/\theta$. It is nonnegative and doubly stochastic.
For a mass-zero row $v$,
\[
 vM_i=\theta vR_i,\qquad
 \norm{vR_i}_1
 \leq\sum_{a,b}|v_a|(R_i)_{ab}=\norm v_1.
\]
Iterating proves \eqref{ste:mx:eq:contraction}. Since $u_s$ is stationary and total
variation between probability distributions is at most 1, the final assertion
follows.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ This is the lift of Corollary~\ref{ste:ee:cor:double}, transposed to rows and applied to unipotent operators (Section~\ref{ste:mx:sec:pointers}).


\begin{corollary}\label{ste:mx:cor:polynomial}
Every nonzero $P\in\Q[x_1,\ldots,x_k]$ has a realization satisfying all the
conditions of Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift} with $s=r(P)+1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:Psignal}
 \acc_P(n)-1/s=\varepsilon q^{|n|}P(n).
\end{equation}
The same fixed matrices realize every member of a joint translation space,
using its own initial row. In particular, $P(n)=0$ if and only if the accepting
coordinate is exactly at equilibrium.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use \eqref{ste:mx:eq:linrep} and Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift}. Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:derivatives}
ensures $b\ne0$, since evaluation at zero is nonzero on a space containing 1.
\end{proof}

The zero polynomial is a harmless exceptional input: it can be represented
by a one-state chain with constant acceptance 1, or loaded with $p=u_s$ in any
nonzero compiled module. The reference compiler requires its \emph{joint
family} to be nonzero, but allows a zero member to be loaded into that family.

\subsection{Why one extra state is necessary}

\begin{theorem}[Exact-series lower bound]\label{ste:mx:thm:optimal}
Suppose commuting rational $s\times s$ row-stochastic matrices $B_i$ have a common
stationary probability row $u$, and suppose a probability row $p$, a fixed
column $f$, and constants $c\ne0$, $q\ne0$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:competitor}
 pB^nf-uf=cq^{|n|}P(n)\qquad(n\in\N^k).
\end{equation}
Then $s\geq r(P)+1$. For a family realized by the same $B_i,u,f,q$, with
possibly different $p$ and nonzero $c$, one has $s\geq r(\mathcal P)+1$.
Thus Corollary~\ref{ste:mx:cor:polynomial} is dimension-optimal in this exact-series
category.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For every $m\in\N^k$, commutativity gives
\[
 P(m+n)=c^{-1}q^{-|m|}q^{-|n|}(p-u)B^mB^nf.
\]
Every row $(p-u)B^m$ belongs to the mass-zero space, of dimension $s-1$.
The linear map sending such a row $v$ to the function
$n\mapsto q^{-|n|}vB^nf$ therefore has image dimension at most $s-1$.
All translates of $P$ lie in that image. Injectivity of polynomial evaluation
now gives $r(P)\leq s-1$. Taking the span over all initial rows proves the
family version.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This is not a lower bound for representing \emph{only the predicate}
$P(n)=0$. Different polynomials may have the same natural zero set and very
different translation ranks. Nor does it cover arbitrary encodings of the
variables, or noncommuting competitors specified only on canonical words.
The invariant concerns the precise numerical series \eqref{ste:mx:eq:competitor}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Exact coordinate hits without any erasure}

\begin{proposition}\label{ste:mx:prop:noerasure}
In the construction of Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift}, no finite word sends a
nonuniform initial distribution to $u_s$. Nevertheless, its first coordinate
can equal $1/s$ after a finite word, exactly when the encoded polynomial
vanishes there.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every finite product is invertible and fixes $u_s$. If $pM_w=u_s$, then
$p=u_sM_w^{-1}=u_s$. The assertion about one coordinate is
\eqref{ste:mx:eq:Psignal}; a vector can be nonzero while a chosen coordinate is zero.
\end{proof}

This is the precise distinction from stochastic mortality. The information
vector survives algebraically at every finite time while its norm becomes
arbitrarily small.

\section{Sharp quartic budgets and a quintic bound}

A full monomial basis of degree at most four has $\binom{k+4}{4}$ members.
That is unnecessarily large for a single quartic's translation space. The
following sharper estimate comes directly from the derivative orders.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp quartic state bound]\label{ste:mx:thm:quartic}
For every nonzero polynomial $P$ of total degree at most four in $k\geq1$
variables,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:quarticbound}
 r(P)\leq 1+k+\binom{k+2}{2}=\binom{k+3}{2}-1.
\end{equation}
Consequently the positive mixing realization uses at most
$\binom{k+3}{2}$ states. This bound is attained, in the exact-series category,
by
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:radial}
 P(x)=(x_1^2+\cdots+x_k^2)^2.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The derivative span is generated by $P$ itself, its $k$ first derivatives,
and its derivatives of order at least two. The last group lies in the space
of polynomials of degree at most two, of dimension $\binom{k+2}{2}$. This
proves \eqref{ste:mx:eq:quarticbound} without assuming homogeneity.

For sharpness, put $R=x_1^2+\cdots+x_k^2$ and $P=R^2$. Its degree-four
part spans one dimension. Its first derivatives $4x_iR$ are independent,
so the cubic part has dimension $k$. For $i\ne j$ and for each $i$,
\[
 \partial_i\partial_jP=8x_ix_j,\qquad
 \partial_i^2P=4R+8x_i^2.
\]
The diagonal coefficient matrix is $8I_k+4\one_k\one_k^T$, which is
invertible: its eigenvalues are 8 on the orthogonal complement of $\one_k$
and $8+4k$ on that line. Hence the second derivatives span all homogeneous
quadratics, of dimension $\binom{k+1}{2}$. The third derivatives include
$\partial_i^3P=24x_i$, and a fourth derivative gives a nonzero constant.
The different homogeneous degrees are independent, giving
\[
 r(P)=1+k+\binom{k+1}{2}+k+1=\binom{k+3}{2}-1.
\]
The lower bound in Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:optimal} completes sharpness for states.
\end{proof}

The radial example has a very simple zero set. Its role is to saturate a
numerical realization bound, not to furnish a hard Diophantine predicate.
The derivative dimensions $(1,k,\binom{k+1}{2},k,1)$ are consistent with the
standard compressed inverse-system picture~\cite{masuti-rossi}; it is their
translation into an optimal positive stochastic state budget that is used here.

\begin{proposition}[General split bound]\label{ste:mx:prop:split}
If $P$ has degree at most $d\geq1$, then for $1\leq m\leq d+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:split}
 r(P)\leq\binom{k+m-1}{k}+\binom{k+d-m}{k},
\end{equation}
where the second term is taken as zero when $m=d+1$. In particular, a
quintic admits a mixing realization with
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:quinticbound}
 s\leq 2\binom{k+2}{2}+1=(k+1)(k+2)+1.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
There are $\binom{k+m-1}{k}$ differential monomials of order less than $m$.
All derivatives of order at least $m$ have degree at most $d-m$, whose
ambient polynomial space has dimension $\binom{k+d-m}{k}$. Add the two
bounds. For $d=5$, take $m=3$ and then add one state.
\end{proof}

No general sharpness assertion for \eqref{ste:mx:eq:split} is needed below. The
state-minimal construction uses the computed rank, not the upper bound.

\section{Fixed alphabets and simple input geometry}\label{ste:mx:sec:universal}

The transitions depend on a joint translation module, not on a particular
coefficient row. This permits the entire computational input to be moved into
the initial distribution. We first record the elementary parameter-loading
mechanism, then apply it to a fixed universal Diophantine predicate.

\subsection{Polynomial input curves}

Suppose $P_t(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{h}t^jP_j(x)$ and let $V$ contain all translates
of the coefficient polynomials $P_j$. Construct $E,F,M_i$ for $V$ as above.
Write
\[
 a(t)E=w_0+tw_1+\cdots+t^h w_h.
\]
Choose a positive rational $C$ strictly larger than
$\max_\ell\sum_{j=0}^{h}|(w_j)_\ell|$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:paramload}
 p(t)=u_s+\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{h}t^j w_j}{sC(1+t)^h}
 \quad(t\in\N)
\end{equation}
is strictly positive and has total mass one. Indeed, $t^j\leq(1+t)^h$ and
all $w_j$ have zero coordinate sum. With
$\varepsilon(t)=1/(sC(1+t)^h)$, the master identity becomes
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:paramsignal}
 p(t)M^n\e_1-1/s=\varepsilon(t)q^{|n|}P_t(n).
\end{equation}
This is an explicit rational map, not a noncomputable encoding into a real
constant.

When $h=2$, let $v=t/(1+t)$. The same curve is a quadratic B\'ezier curve:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:bezier}
 p(t)=(1-v)^2b_0+2v(1-v)b_1+v^2b_2,
\end{equation}
where
$b_0=u_s+w_0/(sC)$, $b_1=u_s+w_1/(2sC)$, and
$b_2=u_s+w_2/(sC)$. All three control points are strictly positive rational
probability rows. Thus the curve lies entirely in the interior of the
probability simplex.

\subsection{A quartic front end with one input-copy equation}

We use MRDP in its standard form: for every computably enumerable set
$K\subseteq\N$, there is an integer polynomial $U(t,x)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:mrdp}
 t\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists x\in\N^m\ U(t,x)=0.
\end{equation}
This external theorem is classical~\cite{davis}. For a universal halting set,
$U$ is one fixed polynomial. The following degree reduction is written out to
make both the input contract and witness correspondence explicit.

\begin{lemma}[Natural quadratic gate front end]\label{ste:mx:lem:gate}
From a fixed integer polynomial $U(t,x)$ one can effectively construct natural
variables $z=(z_0,\ldots,z_{k-1})$, with $z_0$ a copy of the input, and fixed
integer polynomials $q_j(z)$ of degree at most two such that
\[
 \exists x\ U(t,x)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists z\in\N^k\ [z_0=t\ \hbox{and all }q_j(z)=0].
\]
For each original witness $x$, the extra gate variables have a unique completion.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $U=U_+-U_-$ with $U_\pm$ having nonnegative integer coefficients.
Evaluate each by a fixed acyclic circuit using natural constants, addition,
and multiplication, replacing its input $t$ by $z_0$. Give every materialized
gate a fresh natural output variable. A gate $v=a+b$ contributes
$v-a-b=0$, and a gate $v=ab$ contributes $v-ab=0$. Source variables and
constants are allowed at the inputs. Finally equate the outputs evaluating
$U_+$ and $U_-$. Every residual has degree at most two. Topological induction
forces each gate value uniquely from $z_0$ and the original witnesses; all
these values are natural. The final equality is exactly $U=0$.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ This is \texttt{cdc:wf:lem:quadratization} of~\cite{cdc} (Section~\ref{ste:mx:sec:pointers}).


Set
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:StQt}
 S(z)=\sum_j q_j(z)^2,\qquad
 Q_t(z)=(z_0-t)^2+S(z)=Q_0(z)-2tz_0+t^2.
\end{equation}
These are integer polynomials. The polynomial $S$ is nonnegative on all real
arguments, has degree at most four, and takes natural values on $\N^k$.
For each $t$, the natural roots of $Q_t$ are exactly the complete valid
witness tuples for input $t$.

\begin{theorem}[Fourth-order universality on a quadratic curve]\label{ste:mx:thm:curve}
For every fixed computably enumerable $K\subseteq\N$ and every prescribed
rational $0<\theta<1$, one can effectively obtain, from a Diophantine
representation of $K$, a fixed finite commuting alphabet of strictly positive
rational doubly stochastic invertible matrices and a rational curve $p(t)$
whose coordinates have degree at most two over $(1+t)^2$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:Kcurve}
 t\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists n\in\N^k\ p(t)M^n\e_1=1/s.
\end{equation}
The matrices satisfy \eqref{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin}; after division by their common
transient eigenvalue, their joint nilpotence depth is at most four. The number
of states satisfies $s\leq\binom{k+3}{2}$. The initial distributions lie on a
quadratic B\'ezier curve with strictly positive rational control points.
For a computably enumerable complete $K$, this exact-hit problem is
computably enumerable complete.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply the joint-module construction to the family $Q_t$ in
\eqref{ste:mx:eq:StQt}, equivalently to $Q_0,z_0,1$. Its derivative span is contained
in the sum of the span of $Q_0$, the span of its $k$ first derivatives, and
the full space of degree-at-most-two polynomials. The two extra generators
$z_0$ and 1, and all their translates, are already in the last ambient space.
Thus the quartic rank bound remains valid for this family, even if those two
polynomials were not already in $\calV(Q_0)$.

Use \eqref{ste:mx:eq:paramload} with $h=2$. Identity \eqref{ste:mx:eq:paramsignal} and the
positivity of $\varepsilon(t)q^{|n|}$ prove \eqref{ste:mx:eq:Kcurve}. The curve and
control-point claims are \eqref{ste:mx:eq:bezier}; the depth claim follows from
Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:depth}. All data are obtained by finite rational
operations after a polynomial $U$ is supplied.

The map from $t$ to the finite rational initial row is computable. The
right-hand side of \eqref{ste:mx:eq:Kcurve} is computably enumerable by enumerating
all count vectors and performing exact rational tests. If $K$ is complete,
\eqref{ste:mx:eq:Kcurve} is a many-one reduction from that complete set, proving the
last assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Straight-line universality at fifth order}

A square is convenient for forcing the input copy, but it makes the loader
quadratic in $t$. The next construction replaces that square by a sign barrier.
It is important that the target $t+1$ is always strictly positive.

\begin{lemma}[Positive-value filter]\label{ste:mx:lem:positivefilter}
With $S$ from Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:gate}, define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:filter}
 F(z)=(z_0+1)(1-S(z)),\qquad
 P_t(z)=t+1-F(z)=t-z_0+(z_0+1)S(z).
\end{equation}
Then $P_t$ has degree at most five in $z$, is affine in $t$, and its natural
roots are exactly the roots of $Q_t$ in \eqref{ste:mx:eq:StQt}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $S(z)=0$, then $F(z)=z_0+1$, so $P_t(z)=0$ exactly when $z_0=t$.
If $S(z)\ne0$, integrality and nonnegativity imply $S(z)\geq1$, hence
$F(z)\leq0$. It cannot then equal $t+1\geq1$. This proves the full tuple-wise
root correspondence. The degree and affine-dependence assertions are immediate
from \eqref{ste:mx:eq:filter}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Fifth-order universality on a line segment]\label{ste:mx:thm:line}
For every computably enumerable complete set $K$ and prescribed rational
$0<\theta<1$, there are fixed commuting, invertible, strictly positive rational
doubly stochastic matrices $M_1,\ldots,M_k$ and two fixed strictly positive
rational probability rows $b_0,b_1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:line}
 p(t)=\frac{b_0+t b_1}{1+t},\qquad
 t\in K\ \Longleftrightarrow\
 \exists n\in\N^k\ p(t)M^n\e_1=1/s.
\end{equation}
The matrices satisfy \eqref{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin}, have only the eigenvalues 1 and
$q\in(0,\theta)$, and have normalized joint nilpotence depth at most five.
One may take $s\leq2\binom{k+2}{2}+1$. No coefficient in the transition alphabet
or the two endpoint distributions depends on $t$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take $P_t=P_0+t$ from \eqref{ste:mx:eq:filter}. The polynomial $P_0$ is nonconstant:
a constant $P_0$ could accept at most one natural input, whereas $K$ is complete.
It generates a translation space containing 1, by Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:derivatives}.
Thus all $P_t$ belong to the same space $V=\calV(P_0)$, and the fifth-degree
bound of Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:split} applies without any family enlargement.

In the lift, put $w_0=a_{P_0}E$ and $w_1=a_1E$.
Choose rational $C>\max(\norm{w_0}_\infty,\norm{w_1}_\infty)$ and define
\[
 b_0=u_s+\frac{w_0}{sC},\qquad
 b_1=u_s+\frac{w_1}{sC}.
\]
Both are strictly positive probability rows. Their interpolation is
\[
 \frac{b_0+tb_1}{1+t}
   =u_s+\frac{w_0+tw_1}{sC(1+t)}.
\]
The master identity therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:linesignal}
 p(t)M^n\e_1-1/s
  =\frac{q^{|n|}}{sC(1+t)}P_t(n).
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:positivefilter} proves the equivalence in \eqref{ste:mx:eq:line}.
All matrix, depth, and state assertions follow from the lift and the degree
bound. Choosing a complete $K$ gives completeness as in
Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:curve}.
\end{proof}

The endpoint $b_1$, approached as $t\to\infty$, represents the constant
polynomial 1. Its centered acceptance is $q^{|n|}/(sC)>0$ for every finite
word. Thus this endpoint itself has no exact equilibrium hit. The hard inputs
are a simple computable sequence of rational points converging to that endpoint,
not an exotic or uncomputable initial real.

For fixed construction data, each coordinate of $p(t)$ in
Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:line} has a rational numerator and denominator with bit length
$O(\log(t+2))$. The same estimate holds for the quadratic curve. Computing
these input rows requires only fixed-degree rational polynomial evaluation.
This is an exact symbolic input convention, not a claim that physical
preparation or measurement can use unbounded precision at constant cost.

\subsection{Strict cutpoints and a fixed initial distribution}

For the quartic front end, $Q_t(n)$ is a nonnegative integer. Consequently
\[
 1-2Q_t(n)>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad Q_t(n)=0.
\]
Loading the polynomial $1-2Q_t$ into the same joint module therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:strict}
 t\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists n\ p_{\mathrm{strict}}(t)M^n\e_1>1/s.
\end{equation}
This is an exact strict comparison with no promised positive gap. The argument
uses the nonnegativity of $Q_t$; it does not assert an analogous root-to-sign
conversion for the generally signed quintic $P_t$ without further changes.

There is also a fixed-initial-state formulation. Treat the external $t$ in
$Q_t(z)$ as an additional count variable and compile the single quartic
$Q(t,z)$. Read the input in unary as $a_0^t$, and restrict the subsequent
existential word to the other letters. Then
\[
 t\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists n\ pM_0^tM_1^{n_1}\cdots M_k^{n_k}\e_1=1/s.
\]
The initial distribution and all matrices are fixed, with
$s\leq\binom{k+4}{2}$. This pays for an additional letter and an explicit
unary input prefix. Allowing the witness suffix to contain $a_0$ would alter
the input and is not part of this protocol.

\section{The converse: polynomial degree is nilpotence depth}

The forward construction suggests an intrinsic matrix class. Its defining
conditions can be checked by rational arithmetic when $q$ and the depth bound
are supplied.

\begin{definition}
A rational doubly stochastic alphabet belongs to $\mathcal C_d$ if there is a
specified rational $q>0$ such that the matrices
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:nilparts}
 R_i=q^{-1}(M_i-J_s)-(I_s-J_s)
\end{equation}
commute, annihilate the uniform direction on both sides, and satisfy
$R^\alpha=0$ for every $|\alpha|=d+1$. Equivalently, on $H_s$,
$q^{-1}M_i=I+R_i$ and the nilpotent parts have joint depth at most $d$.
Strict positivity and a prescribed bound \eqref{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin} may be imposed
as additional conditions.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Effective degree--depth equivalence]\label{ste:mx:thm:converse}
For fixed $d\geq0$, exact equilibrium-coordinate reachability for rational
initial distributions and alphabets in $\mathcal C_d$ effectively reduces to
solvability over $\N$ of a single rational polynomial of degree at most $d$.
Conversely, every such polynomial reduces to this reachability problem, even
with strict positivity, arbitrary prescribed rational mixing bound, commuting
invertible matrices, and one accepting state. Matrix dimension and alphabet
size are allowed to vary in this equivalence.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $v=p-u_s$. On the mass-zero subspace, the binomial theorem and joint
nilpotence give
\begin{align}
 pM^n\e_1-1/s
 &=q^{|n|}v\prod_{i=1}^k(I_s+R_i)^{n_i}\e_1\notag\\
 &=q^{|n|}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq d}
       \left(vR_1^{\alpha_1}\cdots R_k^{\alpha_k}\e_1\right)
       \prod_{i=1}^k\binom{n_i}{\alpha_i}.\label{ste:mx:eq:recover}
\end{align}
The right-hand sum defines a rational polynomial $g(n)$ of total degree at
most $d$. The term with $\alpha=0$ uses the identity matrix; the formula is
therefore valid at $n=0$ as well. Since $q>0$, exact equilibrium is equivalent
to $g(n)=0$. Clearing a common positive denominator gives an integer polynomial
with the same natural zero set and no degree increase.

In the other direction, apply the polynomial lift. Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:depth}
and \eqref{ste:mx:eq:conjugacy} put the result in $\mathcal C_d$. A nonzero constant
polynomial can be realized in two states; a zero polynomial is handled by the
trivial equilibrium initialization. All additional restrictions follow from
Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:lift}.
\end{proof}

The matrix promise is finite and explicit. One checks stochasticity,
commutation, and each product of total degree $d+1$ in \eqref{ste:mx:eq:nilparts}.
There are $\binom{k+d}{d+1}$ such multiindices. Their vanishing implies the
vanishing of all longer products because the matrices commute. This is a
validity check for the promised class, not a test for reachability itself.

\begin{corollary}\label{ste:mx:cor:depth4}
The variable-dimension equilibrium-hit problem in $\mathcal C_4$ is computably
enumerable complete. This remains true under the positivity and mixing
restrictions above. At depth three the corresponding problem is exactly the
natural solvability problem for a single polynomial of degree at most three.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The fourth-degree statement follows from the quadratic-gate sum-of-squares
reduction and Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:converse}. The third-degree statement is the
same equivalence with $d=3$.
\end{proof}

The degree-three equivalence is a precise reduction, not a decision or
undecidability claim for that Diophantine class. The article does not settle it.
At depth zero the recovered polynomial is constant. At depth one it is linear,
so the matrix question reduces to an ordinary natural linear equation; no
higher-order matrix behavior is hidden there.

\subsection{Why nonnormality is doing real work}

\begin{proposition}\label{ste:mx:prop:nonnormal}
Suppose a doubly stochastic $s$-state matrix has a simple eigenvalue 1 and all
its other eigenvalues equal the same $q\in(0,1)$. If it is diagonalizable, then
\[
 M=J_s+q(I_s-J_s).
\]
Consequently, in a commuting family with this common two-point spectrum,
diagonalizability of every generator makes the normalized centered scalar
signal constant in the count vector.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The invariant mass-zero subspace contains all nonstationary eigenvectors.
The restriction of a diagonalizable operator to this invariant spectral
subspace is diagonalizable with its only eigenvalue $q$, hence equals $qI$.
The action on the uniform direction is 1. This gives the formula. Products
then act by $q^{|n|}$ on the mass-zero subspace, leaving only a constant after
that factor is divided out.
\end{proof}

A normal matrix is diagonalizable, so nonconstant polynomial signals in this
specific class require nonnormality. In particular, all the generators cannot
be reversible with respect to the common uniform distribution, because that
would make them symmetric. This observation concerns the stated two-point
spectrum; it is not a general theorem deciding reachability for reversible
chains with several transient eigenvalues.

\section{Exact critical targets versus effective robust questions}\label{ste:mx:sec:robust}

The common contraction estimate makes the limiting acceptance easy to compute:
it is always $\lambda_*=1/s$. The discontinuity lies in the predicate of
\emph{ever attaining} that value, not in a noncomputable limiting real.

\begin{theorem}[Finite search away from equilibrium]\label{ste:mx:thm:robust}
Suppose every generator satisfies \eqref{ste:mx:eq:entrymargin}. Let $p$ be rational.
For a rational target $c\ne1/s$, choose any integer $H$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:cutoff}
 \theta^H<|c-1/s|.
\end{equation}
No word of length at least $H$ has acceptance $c$. Thus exact reachability of
$c$ is decidable by testing the finitely many words of length less than $H$.
If the matrices commute, it suffices to test count vectors with $|n|<H$.

More generally, a rational interval at positive distance from $1/s$ has the
same finite-search property. For every rational $\eta>0$, all sufficiently
long words satisfy $|\acc_p(w)-1/s|<\eta$, irrespective of whether an exact
hit ever occurs.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The contraction estimate gives
\[
 |\acc_p(w)-1/s|\leq\TV(pM_w,u_s)\leq\theta^{|w|}.
\]
For $|w|\geq H$, this is strictly smaller than the distance to $c$, or to
the specified interval. All shorter products are finite rational computations.
For the last assertion, choose $H$ with $\theta^H<\eta$.
\end{proof}

There is no need to use floating-point logarithms to find $H$. Starting at
$H=0$, multiply a rational accumulator by $\theta$ until it is strictly below
the rational distance. This terminates and correctly handles equality at the
previous step. For example, $\theta=1/2$ and distance $1/8$ give $H=4$, not 3.
The implementation includes this boundary check.

For commuting $k$-letter alphabets and $H\geq1$, the number of count vectors
with $|n|<H$ is $\binom{H+k-1}{k}$. This gives a useful explicit search-size
bound, although rational numerator and denominator sizes must still be paid
for in an implementation.

\wtag\ This is Part~I's remark that a horizon with $\eta^T\leq\varepsilon$ works for every instance (after Proposition~\ref{ste:ee:prop:contraction}), turned into a decision procedure for targets at positive distance. It settles one case of Part~I's question of Section~\ref{ste:ee:q:robust} and bears on Question \texttt{pqc:q:promise} of~\cite{pqc}.

\begin{corollary}\label{ste:mx:cor:nohorizon}
For a fixed complete input family in Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:curve} or
Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:line}, there is no total computable function $B(t)$ which
bounds the length of some exact-hit word whenever one exists.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Such a function would decide membership in $K$: compute $B(t)$ and test all
count vectors of total size at most $B(t)$. This would terminate on all inputs
and be correct in both the positive and negative cases, contradicting
completeness of $K$.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ This is the bounded-search argument of \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound} in~\cite{cdc}; Part~I proves the same for erasure (Theorem~\ref{ste:ee:thm:nobound}).


\subsection{Why approximate measurement is not an exact-zero oracle}

For an integer polynomial and the basic lift,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:gap}
 P(n)\ne0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |\acc_P(n)-1/s|\geq\varepsilon q^{|n|}.
\end{equation}
The lower bound is positive for each supplied tuple, but shrinks exponentially
with its length. For a known finite word, rational arithmetic still decides
equality exactly. What is absent is a computable universal horizon for the
search and a fixed positive observation margin valid at all lengths.

Every symmetric tolerance band around equilibrium is eventually reached by
\emph{every} word, so that approximate event cannot retain the Diophantine
predicate. Conversely, a rational target separated from equilibrium is settled
by finite search. A one-sided interval with equilibrium on its boundary has
zero separation and is not covered by that argument. Exact strict cutpoints
such as \eqref{ste:mx:eq:strict} may therefore remain hard. ``Robust'' must include an
actual positive margin; merely replacing equality by a strict inequality does
not supply one.

Nothing here proposes an algorithm for the halting problem using physical
noise, sampling, thermalization, or finite-precision measurement. Fast mixing
hides the signal numerically without deleting its exact algebraic zero set.

\section{Witnesses, input cost, and representation boundaries}\label{ste:mx:sec:witnesses}

\subsection{Natural roots and canonical words}

For a fixed polynomial $P$, the map
\[
 n\longmapsto a_1^{n_1}\cdots a_k^{n_k}
\]
is a bijection between natural root tuples and canonical exact-hit words.
No additional existential variable is introduced by the stochastic lift.
Among all words, a root tuple of total length $L$ has
$L!/(n_1!\cdots n_k!)$ orderings, and all give the same acceptance. Keeping the
canonical word removes this combinatorial multiplicity.

The gate front end also preserves witnesses in its stated sense. Over each
original root of $U(t,x)$, the input copy and the acyclic gate outputs are
unique. Lemma~\ref{ste:mx:lem:positivefilter} leaves the entire tuple unchanged.
Neither statement makes the original MRDP representation single-fold or
finite-fold. Its pre-existing witness multiplicity is retained, not solved.

\subsection{What is fixed, and what is counted}

For each chosen universal representation, $k$, $s$, $q$, $\theta$, all matrix
entries, the accepting state, and the control points of the initial curve are
fixed. The parameter $t$ changes only the rational initial row. The witness
count tuple has fixed arity $k$ but unbounded entries. This is why its search
can be unbounded without adding a state per simulated time step.

It is not a free compression theorem for arbitrary chronological histories.
We begin with an actual Diophantine polynomial, whose representation theorem
already carries the computational semantics. The stochastic system then
realizes that polynomial's counts. The proof does not infer a unique trace,
a halting-time bound, or a smaller arithmetic circuit from the matrix state
count.

The reference implementation starts from explicit rational polynomials. It
computes their joint derivative module, bases, exact shifts, and the lift.
The displayed universal-existence corollaries start one stage earlier, from
classical MRDP and a gate normalization proved on paper. The package does not
supply numerical matrices for a universal Jones polynomial, nor a counted
machine-to-polynomial frontend. This keeps the deliverable's implemented scope
separate from its mathematical existence statements.

\section{Explicit rational examples}\label{ste:mx:sec:examples}

\subsection{A four-state exact difference test}

Take $P(x,y)=(x-y)^2-1$. Its translation space is spanned by
$1,x-y,(x-y)^2$, so $r(P)=3$. For $\theta=1/2$, the supplied compiler gives
$s=4$, $q=1/18$, $\varepsilon=1/8$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:explicitp}
 p=\frac18(1,3,2,2).
\end{equation}
Its two transition matrices are
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:explicitM}
 M_a=\frac1{72}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 22&17&18&15\\22&21&14&15\\10&17&22&23\\18&17&18&19
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 M_b=\frac1{72}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 18&17&16&21\\18&21&20&13\\22&17&20&13\\14&17&16&25
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Every row and every column of each integer numerator sums to 72. Every entry
is positive. Direct rational multiplication verifies commutation and gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:explicitidentity}
 pM_a^xM_b^y\e_1
 =\frac14+\frac{(x-y)^2-1}{8\cdot18^{x+y}}.
\end{equation}
Thus the accepting coordinate is exactly uniform precisely when $|x-y|=1$.
For $(x,y)=(2,0)$ the probability is $217/864$, slightly above $1/4$.
The empty word gives $1/8$, correctly matching \eqref{ste:mx:eq:explicitp}.

\wtag\ The values $q=1/18$ and $\varepsilon=1/8$ and the matrices \eqref{ste:mx:eq:explicitM} belong to the basis chosen by the shipped compiler. Another rational basis of the same three-dimensional translation space gives another valid lift with the same four states; an independent lift computed at placement had $q=3/56$. The state count and the zero set do not depend on the basis; $q$, $\varepsilon$ and the entries do. The same holds for the values of $q$ and $\varepsilon$ quoted in the rest of this section and in Table~\ref{ste:mx:tab:examples}.

Each matrix has characteristic polynomial $(X-1)(X-1/18)^3$ and determinant
$18^{-3}$. The initial row is not uniform, so no word makes the entire
distribution uniform. Four states are necessary for the numerical series by
Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:optimal}, even though the predicate $|x-y|=1$ can be studied
by other encodings.

Recompiling the same polynomial with $\theta=1/100$ keeps four states and gives
$q=1/900$. It changes the transition entries, not the zero set or the
translation rank. This provides a concrete check of the arbitrary-mixing-speed
claim without invoking a universal representation.

\subsection{Multiplication and a maximal quartic rank}

For $P(x,y,z)=xy-z$, the derivative space is spanned by
$xy-z,x,y,1$. The compiler therefore uses five states. Its exact signal has
$q=1/14$ and $\varepsilon=1/10$ when $\theta=1/2$.

For $P(x,y)=(x^2+y^2)^2$, the rank is 9 and the number of states is 10,
attaining $\binom{2+3}{2}=10$. The exported lift has $q=1/390$ and
$\varepsilon=1/20$. Its unique natural root is $(0,0)$, so its first coordinate
is at equilibrium initially, while its full initial distribution remains
nonuniform. This is another useful check that coordinate equality does not
mean vector equality.

\subsection{Quadratic and linear loaders for factorization}

The small family
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:factorQ}
 Q_t(x,y,z)=(z-t)^2+(z-xy)^2
\end{equation}
has natural roots precisely when $z=t=xy$. Its joint translation rank is 10.
The supplied alphabet is fixed at 11 states with $q=1/80$, for every exported
input $t\in\{0,1,2,6,11\}$. The quadratic loader uses
\[
 \varepsilon(t)=\frac1{44(1+t)^2},
\]
with the three control distributions determined by \eqref{ste:mx:eq:bezier}.
For $t=6$, the roots in the tested box $\{0,\ldots,6\}^3$ are exactly
$(1,6,6),(2,3,6),(3,2,6),(6,1,6)$.

Its positive-filter counterpart is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:mx:eq:factorP}
 P_t(x,y,z)=t-z+(z+1)(z-xy)^2.
\end{equation}
It has exactly the same natural roots, now with a straight-line loader.
The rank of $\calV(P_0)$ is 18, so this example uses 19 states, with
$q=3/1613$ and
\[
 \varepsilon(t)=\frac6{247(1+t)}.
\]
The construction uses $C=13/6$ in Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:line}. The independent
checker reconstructs both endpoint distributions from the exports for $t=0,1$
and verifies the affine interpolation identity for all five supplied inputs.

These are arithmetic demonstrations, not universal alphabets. They make the
input-geometry tradeoff visible on completely specified rational data and
exercise root equivalence in a case with several witnesses.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Polynomial or family & Degree & Rank & States & $q$\\
\midrule
$(x-y)^2-1$ &2&3&4&$1/18$\\
$xy-z$ &2&4&5&$1/14$\\
$(x^2+y^2)^2$ &4&9&10&$1/390$\\
$Q_t$ of \eqref{ste:mx:eq:factorQ} &4&10&11&$1/80$\\
$P_t$ of \eqref{ste:mx:eq:factorP} &5&18&19&$3/1613$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Exact exported dimensions at $\theta=1/2$. Family rows use one fixed

\section{Implementation, independent checking, and reproducibility}\label{ste:mx:sec:implementation}

\subsection{Compiler contract}

The file \texttt{code/mixing\_compiler.py} is a rational reference implementation
using SymPy. It accepts a nonzero joint family of polynomials over $\Q$, a
nonempty tuple of distinct variables, and a rational $\theta\in(0,1)$. It
constructs a derivative basis, computes shift matrices by exact coefficient
comparison, builds $E,F$, and emits the matrices from \eqref{ste:mx:eq:qchoice}.

It rejects floating-point coefficients, undeclared polynomial variables,
negative or nonintegral counts, Boolean values in numerical input positions,
and polynomials outside the compiled module. Loaded initial scales must be
positive and must produce strictly positive distributions. Output matrices
and the compiled object are immutable. Monomials are enumerated by graded
weak compositions rather than by a full Cartesian grid of exponent bounds.
No complexity optimality is claimed for symbolic elimination.

The optional scale argument permits the explicit quadratic and linear input
curves used above. Without it, the compiler selects the safe pointwise scale
\eqref{ste:mx:eq:initial}. Exported JSON stores every rational as an exact string and
includes the input polynomial, basis, shifts, $E,F$, transitions, initial row,
$q$, $\theta$, and $\varepsilon$.

A minimal use is:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from fractions import Fraction
import sympy as sp
from mixing_compiler import compile_family

x, y = sp.symbols("x y")
P = (x-y)**2 - 1
model = compile_family([P], (x, y), Fraction(1, 2))
assert model.states == 4
assert model.acceptance(P, (2, 0)) == sp.Rational(217, 864)
model.export(P, "example.json", "difference test")
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{What was checked}

The shipped execution used Python 3.13.5 and SymPy 1.14.0. The producer
\texttt{build\_examples.py} generates fourteen exports. It symbolically
reconstructs the polynomial from the transition matrices by \eqref{ste:mx:eq:recover}
for every export, tests radial quartic ranks for one through four variables,
and rejects nineteen deliberately malformed inputs. It writes
\texttt{verification/compiler\_report.json}.

The independent \texttt{verify\_exports.py} imports neither the compiler nor
SymPy. It implements matrix multiplication, powers, and Gaussian elimination
with Python's \texttt{fractions.Fraction}. It checks the identities $EF=I$ and
$FE=I-J$, reconstructs each transition matrix, checks strict entry margins,
row and column sums, commutation, invertibility, and the required nilpotent
products. It also computes finite Hankel ranks directly from polynomial
values, independently of the derivative-basis code.

Across the fourteen files, the recorded checks cover 1,310 count vectors,
1,424 arbitrary-order words including empty words, and 269 degree-plus-one
nilpotent products. They check both scalar identities and full-vector
non-erasure, the fixed-matrix property of each input family, the recovered
linear and quadratic input curves, and both four-root factorization lists at
$t=6$. All checks pass. The exact counts and per-file results are in
\texttt{verification/independent\_report.json}.

The two test populations overlap; their totals are not a count of independent
mathematical theorems. Finite success cannot prove an identity for all inputs.
The general proofs in Sections 3--9 (\wtag\ Sections~44--50 here) supply that justification, and the
independent arithmetic checks guard against transcription and implementation
errors in the delivered examples.

\subsection{Reproduction}

From the package root, run:
\begin{lstlisting}
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt
python code/build_examples.py
python code/verify_exports.py
latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The independent checker needs only the Python standard library and can be run
without installing SymPy when checking the existing JSON files. Building new
examples requires SymPy. The article has no external image or font-file
dependencies beyond the installed LaTeX packages. No network access is needed
after the software dependencies are installed.

\wtag\ These commands use the delivered layout. In this report the three programs are \path{code/11-mixing-arithmetic-mixing_compiler.py}, \path{code/11-mixing-arithmetic-build_examples.py} and \path{code/11-mixing-arithmetic-verify_exports.py}, and the delivered \path{reproduce.sh} is \path{code/11-mixing-arithmetic-reproduce.sh}. The fourteen examples, the three files of \path{verification/}, \path{provenance.json} and \path{requirements.txt} are \path{data/11-mixing-arithmetic-*}. The source's \path{article.tex} is printed as this Part; its PDF, its README and its \path{SHA256SUMS.txt} (26 of 26 verified at placement) are not shipped. \path{build_examples.py} imports \texttt{mixing\_compiler} by its delivered name and rewrites \path{examples/} and \path{verification/compiler_report.json}, and \path{verify_exports.py} rewrites \path{verification/independent_report.json}, both under their parent directory and, on Windows, with CRLF line endings; the README of this report gives a recipe that runs on a copy. At placement both programs ran (about 24~s and 2~min) and reproduced every shipped export and both reports after line-ending normalization.

\section{Further research questions and concrete next steps}\label{ste:mx:sec:questions}

\subsection{Priority A: finish a numerical universal instance}\label{ste:mx:q:instantiate}

The most direct repository-facing task is to select a frozen universal
polynomial or a fully specified arithmetic circuit from the existing H10
pipeline, carry out the natural gate normalization, and compile its \emph{actual}
joint translation module. The deliverable should print or export every matrix,
the complete input map, its exact rank, and coefficient bit lengths. This would
replace the existence-level universal alphabet in this note by a concrete one.
It must retain the source polynomial's full domain contract and cannot infer
an arithmetic-operation improvement merely from fewer states.

\wtag\ This instantiation is what the batch-79 answer to Part~I's question of Section~\ref{ste:ee:q:fixedalphabet} still lacks (dated note there).

A useful optimization target is the module rank after gate normalization.
Different circuits for the same polynomial can give different quartic sums of
squares, and hence different derivative spaces. Gate count alone is not the
correct objective. An experimentally searchable criterion is the rank of the
coefficient matrix of all derivatives through order four, together with the
height of the resulting stochastic lift.

\subsection{Priority A: line inputs with fourth-order depth}\label{ste:mx:q:line}

Our quadratic loader uses depth four, while the straight-line loader uses
depth five. Can one achieve a universal exact-hit predicate on a fixed rational
line segment while keeping depth at most four and comparable state size?
The present positive-value filter raises the degree through the factor $z_0+1$.
A construction avoiding that degree increase would improve a specific
input-geometry/depth tradeoff. This note neither proves it impossible nor
asserts that this formulation is an established literature-wide open problem.

A related restricted target is an affine input family whose normalized
polynomial has the form $A(n)+tB(n)$ with $B$ constant. Understanding which
Diophantine predicates admit such degree-bounded representations could separate
the cost of loading an input from the cost of enforcing a witness.

\subsection{Priority A: mechanically check the algebraic interface}\label{ste:mx:q:formal}

A manageable formalization begins with finite-dimensional rational vector
spaces and the identities $EF=I$, $FE=I-J$. The stochastic lift, empty-word
case, exact readout, and total-variation contraction can then be proved without
formalizing MRDP again. A second stage formalizes the polynomial translation
space and its degree filtration. Only after these interfaces are checked is
it necessary to connect an existing formal Diophantine representation theorem.

This decomposition separates rational algebra, finite combinatorics, and the
external computability theorem. It also gives a precise specification against
which the Python producer can emit certificates rather than merely trusted
matrices. No Lean or Rocq formalization is included in the present package.

\subsection{Priority B: zero-set state complexity}\label{ste:mx:q:zeroset}

The numerical lower bound $r(P)+1$ is exact, but can greatly overestimate the
cost of representing the predicate $P(n)=0$. Define a zero-set realization
complexity by minimizing over all polynomials with the same natural zero set,
and then over admissible exact-cutpoint encodings. Can useful lower bounds be
proved for this invariant? Even showing a gap between value complexity and
zero-set complexity for structured families would clarify what the rank theorem
does and does not measure.

A computational first step is to compare a polynomial, its square, a
sum-of-squares presentation, and an equivalent presentation with unique gate
variables. Variables and states must be reported separately; adding witness
variables can change the optimization problem rather than solve it.

\subsection{Priority B: coefficient height and prescribed decay}\label{ste:mx:q:height}

The construction makes $q$ small enough to enforce positivity. It does not
allow an arbitrary preassigned $q$ close to 1 at no cost. What is the optimal
tradeoff among state count, rational coefficient height, a requested contraction
factor, and the size of the transient eigenvalue? Can a different rational basis
of the same translation module dramatically improve the denominators without
adding states?

A concrete optimization problem is to minimize
$\max_i\norm{FA_iE}_{\max}$ over rational choices of the embedding and basis,
subject to the fixed coordinate readout. The theorem supplies a feasible point,
not an optimum. Large-denominator input distributions may be as important as
large matrix denominators in any end-to-end bit-cost study.

\subsection{Priority B: third-order and reversible boundaries}\label{ste:mx:q:reversible}

Theorem~\ref{ste:mx:thm:converse} identifies the third-order matrix problem with
single-cubic natural solvability. Progress on either side transfers to the
other. Restrictions such as bounded alphabet size, special sign patterns, or
small translation rank may isolate tractable subproblems without deciding the
unrestricted class.

The obstruction in Proposition~\ref{ste:mx:prop:nonnormal} disappears when several
transient eigenvalues are allowed. It is therefore worth studying commuting
reversible alphabets with a common stationary law and multiple positive
transient eigenvalues. Their centered signals are exponential polynomials
rather than just one exponential times a polynomial. The present proof does
not classify their exact-hit problem.

\subsection{Priority C: families, partial commutation, and margins}\label{ste:mx:q:families}

For a parameter family, the joint translation rank can exceed the rank of each
individual member. A systematic theory of the minimal fixed alphabet should
track this joint invariant, including exceptional parameter values. The affine
family $P_0+t$ is especially economical because 1 already belongs to the
translation space; the quadratic family need not have this property for its
input-coordinate term.

Partial commutation is another natural extension. One could replace count
vectors by traces in a partially commutative monoid and seek a comparable
mass-zero realization bound. Any such extension must pay for the retained
ordering information rather than silently replace chronological histories by
counts.

Finally, the exact/robust boundary suggests a parameterized verification
program. Given a rational target set, a certified distance from equilibrium
makes the problem finite. When that distance is zero, sign information and
nilpotent coefficients matter. A classification by target geometry and input
curve, rather than by mixing speed alone, may yield sharper verification
algorithms and more informative impossibility results.

\section{Conclusion}

A rapidly mixing stochastic system can retain arbitrary Diophantine zero tests
in an exponentially small exact signal. The translation space provides the
right finite-dimensional carrier, and an explicit mass-zero embedding converts
it into a strictly positive, doubly stochastic, commuting, invertible alphabet
with a single accepting state. The cost is exactly one state beyond translation
rank for the numerical series.

The resulting universal refinements are structurally restrictive: fixed
matrices, a common uniform stationary law, a common transient eigenvalue,
bounded joint nilpotence depth, and initial inputs lying on a quadratic curve
or even a straight line. At the same time, separated targets have effective
finite search bounds, and all positive-tolerance neighborhoods of equilibrium
are eventually reached automatically. The distinction is exact equality, not
slow convergence, complicated spectra, or full-state erasure.

The immediate next mathematical and engineering tasks are explicit universal
instantiation, state-versus-height optimization, and formal certification of
the lift. The note supplies the proofs and a reproducible rational reference
implementation needed to begin those tasks without conflating their remaining
costs with the results already established here.

\part{Coercive Green operators: finite support and rational response in well-conditioned networks (sources 12 and 13)}\label{ste:part:cg}

\noindent\wtag\ Part~V prints two batch-79 manuscripts that prove one theorem by two routes. They were written independently from the same repository snapshot \texttt{44983ed7e} (their 8-gram text overlap is at most 0.009), arrived together with the archives of commit \texttt{ef2fc7990} and were placed by commit \texttt{bbaf322e5}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{source~12} (batch-79 manuscript~10, file prefix \texttt{12-coercive-green-}): \emph{Coercive Linear Algebra as a Universal Computational Substrate: Canonical Quasi-Diophantine Certificates, Rational Green Functions, and Uniformly Shunted Resistor Networks}, ``Research report prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov'' (no assistant named), 2~October 2026. Labels \texttt{ste:cg:}; printed first, as the base (Part~V.A, Sections~55--69, Appendices~\ref{ste:cg:app:spec} and~\ref{ste:cg:app:manifest});
\item \emph{source~13} (batch-79 manuscript~14, file prefix \texttt{13-well-conditioned-}): \emph{Well-Conditioned Diophantine Computation: Connected Resistor Networks, Compact Support, and a Prime-Localization Barrier}, ``Research manuscript prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov; Mathematical development and reference implementation: ChatGPT'' (PDF metadata author ``ChatGPT; prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov''), 2~October 2026. Labels \texttt{ste:wc:}; printed second, as a second route with its further results (Part~V.B, Sections~70--81, Appendices~\ref{ste:wc:app:algorithm} and~\ref{ste:wc:app:archive}).

The source numbers are this report's file prefixes. \emph{Source~12 is batch-79 manuscript~10}; it is unrelated to source~10 of Part~\ref{ste:part:ec} (batch-78 manuscript~10).

\wtag\ \emph{The common theorem.} A branch-history lift of counter machines makes every component of the configuration graph a rooted finite path or a rooted ray. The grounded operator, $\alpha I-A$ in source~12 and $mI-J$ in source~13, is uniformly coercive, and its Green column at an empty-history source has finite support, a rational root value and a primitive continuant certificate exactly when the computation halts; the resistance is rational or a fixed quadratic irrational, and no computable function bounds support, charge or time. Statement by statement:

\noindent\wtag\ Source~12's abstract, keywords and reading guide follow.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract (source 12).} We construct a fixed, finitely described, sparse self-adjoint operator whose
inverse is uniformly bounded and efficiently approximable, but whose exact
arithmetic behavior represents universal computation. For every integer
$\alpha\ge3$, the operator has the form $L_\alpha=\alpha I-A$, where $A$ is
the adjacency operator of a computable disjoint union of finite paths and
one-sided infinite rays. Its condition number is at most
$(\alpha+2)/(\alpha-2)$; after normalization it is within $2/\alpha$ of the
identity. Nevertheless, for a computable family of roots $s$, the conditions
that $L_\alpha^{-1}e_s$ has finite support and that its diagonal Green
function is rational are both equivalent to halting.

An explicit branch-history lift of two-counter machines gives the graph by
fixed guarded affine rules on $Q\times\N^3$. Halting has a unique normalized
integer certificate $L_\alpha u=c e_s$, $H(u)=1$, with $u$ of finite support.
If the run has $n$ configurations, then $c=D_n$ and its coefficients are
$D_{n-1},\ldots,D_0$, where $D_{-1}=0$, $D_0=1$, and
$D_{j+1}=\alpha D_j-D_{j-1}$. The Green function is $D_{n-1}/D_n$ for a
halting run and $(\alpha-\sqrt{\alpha^2-4})/2$ otherwise. We prove a complete
Pell-type classification of the rational output spectrum, a polynomial-time
algorithm for each specified rational level, and a polynomial-time
approximation algorithm despite undecidable rationality.

The same construction yields a passive resistor-network interpretation and
a uniformly killed random walk with bounded mean lifetime. We give a linear
polynomial-functional presentation, exact quadratic finite-support slices,
and a positive-characteristic obstruction. The accompanying standard-library
implementation passes 110,522 exact checks. The contribution is a proved
structural normal form and its consequences, not a new ordinary universal
integer-polynomial bound, a finite physical halting oracle, or an established
claim of literature priority.

\textbf{Keywords.} Diophantine representation; counter machines;
coercive operators; finite support; effective resistance; computable real
numbers; continued fractions; certificate uniqueness.
\end{quote}
\noindent\textbf{Reading guide.}
The representation theorem is developed in Sections~\ref{ste:cg:sec:history}--\ref{ste:cg:sec:certificate}.
The exact observable and its computability boundary are in
Sections~\ref{ste:cg:sec:green}--\ref{ste:cg:sec:approximation}. Sections~\ref{ste:cg:sec:polynomial}
and~\ref{ste:cg:sec:slices} specify the algebraic language and the ordinary
Diophantine interface. A fully numerical example appears in
Section~\ref{ste:cg:sec:example}. Sections~\ref{ste:cg:sec:physical}--\ref{ste:cg:sec:research}
cover other substrates, limitations, validation, and further research.

\section{Question, contribution, and source boundary}\label{ste:cg:sec:scope}
\subsection{The structural question}
A broad Diophantine representation of the halting relation is already
provided by the Davis--Putnam--Robinson--Matiyasevich theorem
\cite{matiyasevich1970}. A new
programming-language name, followed only by an appeal to that theorem, does
not normally constitute a new result about representation. We instead ask:
\begin{quote}
Can universal computation have a canonical \emph{linear} finite-support
certificate even when its associated infinite-dimensional operator is
self-adjoint, strictly positive, uniformly invertible, and arbitrarily
well-conditioned? Can an exact arithmetic observable remain undecidable
when that observable has a uniform fast approximation algorithm?
\end{quote}
The answer to both questions is yes. The construction below separates three
issues which are easy to conflate: existence of an analytic solution,
finite support of an exact algebraic solution, and decidability of the exact
arithmetic type of a computable real number.

The underlying combinatorics is unusually simple: every component is a
path. The difficulty is not hidden in enormous vertex degrees, a closing
spectral gap, a noncomputable matrix entry, or a variable operator chosen
after observing a computation. It lies in whether an effectively presented
path ever ends. A finite table of guarded affine rules generates all rows
of the matrix, and a source configuration chooses the component.

\subsection{Repository audit and relation to previous work}
The public repository was inspected at commit
\begin{center}
\texttt{44983ed7ebfd545de55bfdb50e040c82f3d24295}.
\end{center}
The Hilbert-tenth-problem directory contains corrected historical articles,
formal material, and a large, separately qualified research corpus
\cite{repoH10}. Its README reports established arithmetic counts of 75
operations for a complete certificate and 87 for a universal polynomial.
It also distinguishes finite-horizon substrate compilers from fixed-arity,
unbounded-time representations. Those distinctions are retained here.
Neither number is improved by this article.

\wtag\ Both figures are still those of the project README at this write; the source's pin is kept as provenance.

An earlier project report, \emph{Linear Quasi-Diophantine Universality}
\cite{linearreport}, gives a clocked boundary-transport equation with unique
binary polynomial certificates over every nonzero commutative coefficient
ring. Its Hilbert-space consequence is a dense, nonclosed range and an exact
finite-support integrality-gap phenomenon. The present construction does
\emph{not} repackage that range-membership result: its operator is onto,
its range is closed, its inverse is bounded, and it may be arbitrarily close
to the identity after scaling. Its main exact decision problems concern
finite support and rationality. Its coefficients are continuants rather
than binary digits, and its characteristic-independent validity fails.

\wtag\ \emph{Linear Quasi-Diophantine Universality} is batch-79 manuscript~01 (archive \path{Linear_Boundary_Transport_Research.zip}, arrival \texttt{060e08a07}). Batch~79 placed it in the collection's report \emph{polynomial-witness-histories}~\cite{pwh} (commit \texttt{224ca41df}), where it is to be printed as a Part with label prefix \texttt{pwh:bt:}; that report had not yet been written at this write. Source~13 cites the same manuscript (Section~\ref{ste:wc:sec:scope}).

The classical ingredients are also explicit. Suitable two-counter
instruction sets are universal; importantly, changing the available jump
instructions can change decidability \cite{dudenhefner}. Recording enough
history to make a computation injective is a classical reversible-computing
idea \cite{bennett}. The connection between resistor networks and random
walks is classical \cite{doyle}. We prove the particular lift, certificates,
operator bounds, observable formulas, and computational consequences used
here, rather than importing unproved properties of a different machine
model.

\subsection{Status of the claims}
The theorems in this manuscript have mathematical proofs. The executable
checks provide additional finite validation, including independent
implementations of key identities; they are not a replacement for those
proofs. No Lean or Coq formalization of the new construction is supplied.
The bibliography and repository comparison establish relevant antecedents,
not an exhaustive priority search. In particular, no claim is made that
undecidable rationality of computable reals, reversible simulation, or path
continued fractions are individually new. The proposed contribution is
the combined, explicit, coercive normal form with a canonical integer
witness and the precise interfaces proved below.

\section{A finite affine history lift}\label{ste:cg:sec:history}
\subsection{Machine semantics}
Fix a finite state set $Q$. A configuration of the base machine is
$(r,a,b)\in Q\times\N^2$. Every state has exactly one instruction of one of
the following forms:
\begin{align*}
\INC(1,p)&:\quad (r,a,b)\longmapsto(p,a+1,b),\\
\INC(2,p)&:\quad (r,a,b)\longmapsto(p,a,b+1),\\
\TEST(1,p_+,p_0)&:\quad
(r,a,b)\longmapsto
\begin{cases}(p_+,a-1,b),&a>0,\\(p_0,0,b),&a=0,
\end{cases}\\
\TEST(2,p_+,p_0)&:\quad
(r,a,b)\longmapsto
\begin{cases}(p_+,a,b-1),&b>0,\\(p_0,a,0),&b=0,
\end{cases}\\
\HALT&:\quad\text{no successor.}
\end{align*}
Destinations are arbitrary states; no fall-through restriction is imposed.
