The two branches of a test are regarded as distinct branches even when they
have the same destination. This is an ordinary deterministic counter
model containing the universal instruction patterns discussed in
\cite{dudenhefner}. We do not assert universality for the more restricted
reversible two-counter model considered there.

Let $\Sigma$ be the finite set of all nonhalting branches, and give its
members distinct tags $d_\sigma\in\{1,\ldots,m\}$, where $m=|\Sigma|$.
Put $B=\max(2,m+1)$. The lifted vertex set is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:vertices}
 V=Q\times\N^3,
 \qquad v=(r,a,b,h).
\end{equation}
The extra natural number $h$ records a branch history. If branch $\sigma$
is enabled at $(r,a,b)$, define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:lift}
 F(r,a,b,h)=(p,a+\delta_1,b+\delta_2,Bh+d_\sigma),
\end{equation}
where $(\delta_1,\delta_2)$ is the corresponding counter update. Leave
$F$ undefined at halting states. Each individual branch is a fixed affine
map restricted by one elementary counter guard.

\begin{lemma}[Effective injectivity]\label{ste:cg:lem:injective}
The partial map $F$ is injective. Its partial inverse is computable from a
vertex by one division with remainder by the fixed base $B$, one table
lookup, and a counter-guard check. Moreover, $h(F(v))>h(v)$ whenever $F(v)$
is defined.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The last base-$B$ digit of the target history is exactly $d_\sigma$ and
therefore determines the branch. The quotient determines the preceding
history. For an increment branch, subtract one from the affected target
counter and require that counter to be positive. For a decrement branch,
add one. For a zero branch, require the affected target counter to be zero
and leave it unchanged. Check also that the target state is the branch's
destination. These conditions reconstruct at most one predecessor, and a
forward application verifies it. Finally,
$Bh+d_\sigma>h$ because $B\ge2$ and $d_\sigma\ge1$.
\end{proof}

This inverse is defined on \emph{all} natural-number labels, not just on
histories known in advance to arise from an input. Labels with inadmissible
last digits, mismatched target states, or failing inverse guards simply
have no predecessor. Such labels do not create spurious branching.

\begin{theorem}[Path normal form]\label{ste:cg:thm:paths}
Join $v$ to $F(v)$ whenever the latter is defined. The resulting undirected
graph $G$ is a disjoint union of finite paths and one-sided infinite rays.
Its degree is at most two. Every vertex with $h=0$ is the initial endpoint
of its component. The component of $s=(r,a,b,0)$ is finite precisely when
the base-machine run from $(r,a,b)$ halts. If it halts after $T$ transitions,
that component has $n=T+1$ vertices.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Determinism gives at most one successor and Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:injective} gives
at most one predecessor. A directed cycle would strictly increase $h$ and
return to its starting value, which is impossible. Nor can there be an
infinite chain of predecessors, since $h$ would be a strictly decreasing
sequence of natural numbers. Thus every component has a root and is a
finite path or a ray; no bi-infinite component occurs. A history-zero vertex
cannot be a target of~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:lift}. Its forward path projects, step for
step, to the specified base-machine run. The only vertices without a
successor are those in halting states. This proves both the equivalence
and the vertex count.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fixing the universal graph}
Choose once and for all a universal counter program in the stated
instruction set. More explicitly, simulate a fixed universal Turing
machine by a fixed finite-register machine and use the standard effective
two-counter simulation. A computable encoding $x\mapsto s(x)$ maps each
program/input pair to an empty-history source of this one counter program.
This use of universality is external; the subsequent graph and algebra
constructions are explicit for \emph{every} finite instruction table.
A numerical smallest universal table is neither required by the theorem
nor included in the implementation. Complexity bounds below are measured
in the binary size of the \emph{counter configuration supplied}, not in a
hypothetically efficient implementation of every possible source-language
encoding.

For the fixed universal program, $G$ is a fixed graph. Computing its
neighbors uses only the finite table and the vertex label, with polynomial
bit complexity. In particular, the graph does not contain matrix entries
whose definitions require deciding whether an arbitrary computation halts.
Only the unbounded question of which paths have terminal endpoints is hard.

\section{A uniformly coercive operator}\label{ste:cg:sec:operator}
On the Hilbert space $\ell^2(V)$, with standard basis $(e_v)_{v\in V}$, set
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:operators}
 Se_v=\begin{cases}e_{F(v)},&F(v)\text{ exists},\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
 \qquad A=S+S^*,\qquad L_\alpha=\alpha I-A,
 \quad\alpha\in\Z,\ \alpha\ge3.
\end{equation}
Injectivity makes $S$ a partial isometry: distinct nonzero images of basis
vectors are distinct basis vectors. Thus $\norm S\le1$, and $A$ is exactly
the adjacency operator of the graph in Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:paths}.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform invertibility]\label{ste:cg:thm:coercive}
The operator $L_\alpha$ is bounded and self-adjoint, with
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:coercivity}
 (\alpha-2)I\ \le\ L_\alpha\ \le\ (\alpha+2)I.
\end{equation}
It is bijective on $\ell^2(V)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:condition}
 \norm{L_\alpha^{-1}}\le\frac1{\alpha-2},\qquad
 \kappa(L_\alpha)\le\frac{\alpha+2}{\alpha-2},\qquad
 \norm{\alpha^{-1}L_\alpha-I}\le\frac2\alpha.
\end{equation}
All rows contain at most three nonzero entries, drawn from
$\{\alpha,-1\}$. The inverse is given by the norm-convergent series
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:neumann}
 L_\alpha^{-1}=\frac1\alpha\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(A/\alpha)^k,
\end{equation}
whose tail after $k=K$ has norm at most
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:tail}
 \frac{(2/\alpha)^{K+1}}{\alpha-2}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The bound $\norm A\le\norm S+\norm{S^*}\le2$ gives
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:coercivity}. Since $\norm{A/\alpha}<1$, the geometric operator
series~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:neumann} converges and is a two-sided inverse. Summing its
norm bounds gives the inverse bound and~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:tail}; the remaining
claims follow from $L_\alpha=\alpha I-A$ and the degree bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Effective inversion on sparse rational data]\label{ste:cg:prop:effectiveinverse}
For fixed program and fixed $\alpha$, a finitely supported rational vector
$z$ and a precision $b$ determine, in polynomial bit time, a finitely
supported rational vector approximating $L_\alpha^{-1}z$ within $2^{-b}$
in $\ell^2$ norm. The complexity is polynomial in $b$ and the full sparse
binary description of $z$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Use the partial sum in~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:neumann}. Choose $K$ so that the tail
bound~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:tail} multiplied by the computable rational upper bound
$\norm z_1$ for $\norm z_2$ is at most $2^{-b}$. For fixed $\alpha$, one
can choose $K=O(b+\mathrm{size}(z)+1)$. The radius-$K$ neighborhood of a
set of $r$ vertices in a path forest contains at most $r(2K+1)$ vertices.
All successive partial products can therefore be stored sparsely on a
polynomial-size set. The guarded affine updates increase label bit sizes
by at most $O(K)$, and the rational coefficients have polynomial bit size:
common input denominators, powers of the fixed $\alpha$, and walk counts
bounded by $2^K$ suffice. Exact sparse iteration computes the claimed
partial sum in polynomial time. If $z=0$, return zero directly.
\end{proof}

For any prescribed $K_0>1$, an integer
$\alpha\ge 2(K_0+1)/(K_0-1)$ guarantees
$\kappa(L_\alpha)\le K_0$. The eventual undecidability result therefore
persists as close to condition number one as desired. This statement
concerns a fixed choice of $\alpha$ for each target condition bound; it
does not take a limit in which an algorithm is permitted to discard the
nonzero off-diagonal entries.

\begin{lemma}[Support of the inverse column]\label{ste:cg:lem:positive}
Every entry of $L_\alpha^{-1}$ is nonnegative. Its $(v,w)$ entry is strictly
positive if and only if $v,w$ belong to the same component. Consequently,
for an empty-history source $s$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:finiteinverse}
 \supp(L_\alpha^{-1}e_s)\text{ is finite}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{the source computation halts}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The $(v,w)$ entry of $A^k$ counts length-$k$ walks from $w$ to $v$ and is
nonnegative. It is positive for some $k$ exactly when the vertices are
connected. Apply~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:neumann} and Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:paths}.
\end{proof}

The analytic equation $L_\alpha u=e_s$ has a unique $\ell^2$ solution for
\emph{every} source. Thus~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:finiteinverse} is not undecidable
analytic solvability, and it is not a range-closure pathology.

\section{Canonical normalized integer certificates}\label{ste:cg:sec:certificate}
Let $R^{(V)}$ denote finite-support arrays with coefficients in a ring $R$.
The terminal functional
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:terminal}
 H(u)=\sum_{v:\,\text{state}(v)\in\Halt}u_v
\end{equation}
is well-defined on this algebraic direct sum. It is not asserted to be a
bounded functional on the full Hilbert space. The certificate uses only
finite support.

Define integers $D_j=D_j(\alpha)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:continuants}
 D_{-1}=0,\qquad D_0=1,\qquad
 D_{j+1}=\alpha D_j-D_{j-1}\quad(j\ge0).
\end{equation}
Let $L_{\alpha,n}$ be the $n\times n$ tridiagonal matrix with $\alpha$ on the
diagonal and $-1$ immediately above and below it.

\begin{lemma}[Continuants and endpoint normalization]\label{ste:cg:lem:continuants}
For $n\ge1$, $\det L_{\alpha,n}=D_n>0$. Furthermore,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:column}
 L_{\alpha,n}
 \begin{pmatrix}D_{n-1}\\D_{n-2}\\\vdots\\D_0\end{pmatrix}
 =D_n e_0.
\end{equation}
For every $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:growth}
 D_n>(\alpha-1)D_{n-1}>0.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expansion along an endpoint gives the determinant recurrence
$\det L_{\alpha,n}=\alpha\det L_{\alpha,n-1}-\det L_{\alpha,n-2}$,
with initial values $1$ for the empty matrix and $\alpha$ for $n=1$.
For~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:column}, the first row is the defining recurrence for $D_n$,
each interior row vanishes by the same recurrence, and the last row
vanishes because $D_1=\alpha D_0$. When $n=1$, the single row is simply
$\alpha D_0=D_1$. Finally, $D_1=\alpha>\alpha-1$; induction using
$D_{n-2}<D_{n-1}$ proves~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:growth}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical certificate]\label{ste:cg:thm:canonical}
For an empty-history source $s$, consider
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:certificate}
 L_\alpha u=c e_s,\qquad H(u)=1,
 \qquad c\in\Z,\quad u\in\Z^{(V)}.
\end{equation}
This system has a solution if and only if the computation from $s$ halts.
When it has a solution, the entire pair $(c,u)$ is unique. If the run is
$v_0=s,v_1,\ldots,v_{n-1}$, ending in a halting configuration, then
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:canonical}
 c=D_n,\qquad u_{v_j}=D_{n-1-j}\ (0\le j<n),\qquad
 u_v=0\text{ off the run}.
\end{equation}
In particular all occupied coefficients and $c$ are positive integers,
and the terminal coefficient is exactly one.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every finite-support solution is in $\ell^2(V)$, so invertibility gives
$u=cL_\alpha^{-1}e_s$. If $c=0$, then $u=0$, contradicting $H(u)=1$.
By Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:positive}, a nonzero inverse column has finite support
only when the source component is a finite path. Hence any solution
implies halting.

Suppose the component has $n$ vertices. Equation~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:column} gives
the finite-support solution~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:canonical}; there is exactly one
halting vertex in that component, and its coefficient is $D_0=1$, so it
satisfies the normalization. Finally, any other solution is a scalar
multiple of the same inverse column. Its terminal coordinate fixes that
scalar, proving uniqueness. This argument also covers an already halted
source: $n=1$, $c=\alpha$, and $u=e_s$.
\end{proof}

There is no positivity condition hidden in the quantifiers of
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:certificate}. Positivity is a consequence of its equations.
Nor is the scalar $c$ a freely chosen scaling slack: the terminal
normalization fixes it to the path determinant.

\begin{proposition}[Torsion-free coefficient extension]\label{ste:cg:prop:torsionfree}
Let $R$ be a nonzero commutative unital ring whose additive group is
$\Z$-torsion-free. Replacing $\Z$ by $R$ in~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:certificate} preserves
existence and uniqueness, with the integer solution~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:canonical}
interpreted through the canonical map $\Z\to R$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
On a finite path not containing the source, the adjugate identity implies
$D_n u=0$ for a homogeneous solution, hence $u=0$ by torsion-freeness.
On a ray, a finite-support homogeneous solution is zero: at the vertex
just beyond its last possibly occupied position, the equation is the
negative of that last coefficient, and downward induction finishes.
These arguments eliminate all components not containing the source.

If the source component is a ray, the same exterior-row induction first
eliminates every positive-index coefficient, then the coefficient at the
root, and finally $c$. The normalization is impossible. If it is a finite
path, its sole terminal coefficient is one. Reading the terminal row and
then successive rows backwards yields $1,\alpha,D_2,\ldots,D_{n-1}$.
The root row then gives $c=D_n$. This is both existence and uniqueness.
\end{proof}

In particular the certificate is unchanged over $\Q$, $\R$, or $\C$ when
finite support is retained. The torsion-free hypothesis is stronger than
characteristic zero. Section~\ref{ste:cg:sec:characteristic} explains why this
qualification matters.

\section{Exact Green functions and universal rationality}\label{ste:cg:sec:green}
For a source $s$, define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:green}
 g_\alpha(s)=\ip{e_s}{L_\alpha^{-1}e_s},\qquad
 \rho_\alpha=\frac{\alpha-\sqrt{\alpha^2-4}}2.
\end{equation}
Then $0<\rho_\alpha<1/2$ and
$\rho_\alpha^2-\alpha\rho_\alpha+1=0$.

\begin{theorem}[Rational--quadratic dichotomy]\label{ste:cg:thm:green}
For every empty-history source,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:dichotomy}
 g_\alpha(s)=
 \begin{cases}
 D_{n-1}/D_n,&\text{if the source run has $n$ configurations},\\[2pt]
 \rho_\alpha,&\text{if the source run never halts}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The first value is rational and strictly smaller than $\rho_\alpha$; the
second is a quadratic irrational. Thus all source values lie in the fixed
quadratic field $\Q(\sqrt{\alpha^2-4})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
On a finite path, divide~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:column} by $D_n$ and read the first
coordinate. On a ray with coordinates indexed by $j\ge0$, put
$w_j=\rho_\alpha^{j+1}$. This sequence is in $\ell^2$. Its root equation is
$\alpha w_0-w_1=\alpha\rho_\alpha-\rho_\alpha^2=1$, and its remaining
rows vanish by the same quadratic identity. Uniqueness of the inverse
therefore gives $g_\alpha(s)=w_0=\rho_\alpha$.

For irrationality, $(\alpha-1)^2<\alpha^2-4<\alpha^2$ for integer
$\alpha\ge3$, so the discriminant is not a square. The strict inequality
for the finite value follows from Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:gap} below.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Exact convergence gap]\label{ste:cg:lem:gap}
Writing $\rho=\rho_\alpha$ and $\lambda=\rho^{-1}$ gives
\begin{align}
 D_n&=\frac{\lambda^{n+1}-\rho^{n+1}}{\lambda-\rho},\label{ste:cg:eq:closedD}\\
 g_n:=\frac{D_{n-1}}{D_n}
 &=\rho\frac{1-\rho^{2n}}{1-\rho^{2n+2}},\label{ste:cg:eq:closedg}\\
 \rho-g_n
 &=\frac{\rho^{2n+1}(1-\rho^2)}{1-\rho^{2n+2}}
 \le\rho^{2n+1}.\label{ste:cg:eq:gap}
\end{align}
The sequence $g_n$ is strictly increasing and converges to $\rho$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The expression in~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:closedD} has the initial values and recurrence
in~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:continuants}, since $\lambda,\rho$ are the two roots of
$t^2-\alpha t+1$. Division gives~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:closedg}, and subtraction
gives~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:gap}. The last denominator is at least $1-\rho^2$.
Alternatively, monotonicity follows from $g_1=1/\alpha$ and
$g_{n+1}=1/(\alpha-g_n)$: this increasing map carries every point of
$[0,\rho)$ to a strictly larger point below $\rho$. The displayed gap
then gives convergence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Fixed-operator completeness]\label{ste:cg:cor:complete}
For the fixed graph obtained from a universal machine, each of the following
sets of encoded empty-history sources is computably enumerable complete
under computable many-one reductions:
\[
 \{s:g_\alpha(s)\in\Q\},\qquad
 \{s:g_\alpha(s)<\rho_\alpha\},\qquad
 \{s:\supp(L_\alpha^{-1}e_s)\text{ is finite}\}.
\]
The set $\{s:g_\alpha(s)=\rho_\alpha\}$ is co-computably-enumerable
complete. Deciding whether $g_\alpha(s)$ has algebraic degree one or two
is therefore impossible in general.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each of the first three conditions is exactly halting by
Theorems~\ref{ste:cg:thm:canonical} and~\ref{ste:cg:thm:green}. Simulating the fixed
counter program semidecides them. The fixed universal input encoding
reduces the usual halting set to each one. The equality condition is their
complement, and the degree statement follows from~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:dichotomy}.
\end{proof}

For $\alpha=3$, the recurrence is especially concrete:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:fibonacci}
 D_n=F_{2n+2},\qquad g_n=\frac{F_{2n}}{F_{2n+2}},\qquad
 \rho_3=\frac{3-\sqrt5}{2}.
\end{equation}
Here $F_0=0,F_1=1$ are the Fibonacci numbers. The subsequence
$F_{2n+2}$ has the initial values $1,3$ and satisfies
$D_{n+1}=3D_n-D_{n-1}$, which proves the identity. The first values are
$1/3,3/8,8/21,21/55,55/144,\ldots$.

\section{The rational output spectrum is a Pell curve}\label{ste:cg:sec:pell}
The rational values in the preceding theorem are not arbitrary. Their
numerators and denominators obey a single quadratic equation, and its
positive branch admits an effective descent.

\begin{theorem}[Complete spectrum classification]\label{ste:cg:thm:pell}
For positive integers $p<q$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:pell}
 q^2+p^2-\alpha pq=1;
 \qquad
 (q,p)=(D_n,D_{n-1})\text{ for a unique }n\ge1.
\end{equation}
All such pairs are coprime. The index $n$ is recovered by repeatedly
replacing
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:descent}
 (q,p)\longmapsto(p,\alpha p-q)
\end{equation}
until reaching $(1,0)$. Exactly $n$ replacements occur.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The transformation $(q,p)\mapsto(\alpha q-p,q)$ preserves
$q^2+p^2-\alpha pq$. Starting with $(1,0)$ therefore proves the forward
identity for all consecutive continuants. Any common divisor of $q,p$
has its square dividing one, so the pair is coprime.

Conversely, suppose $q>p>0$ satisfies the equation. For
$r=\alpha p-q$, rearrangement gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:descentbound}
 qr=p^2-1.
\end{equation}
Thus $r\ge0$ and $r<p$, because $q>p$. The pair $(p,r)$ satisfies the
same equation. Descent strictly decreases its positive second coordinate,
so it reaches a pair $(q',0)$; the equation then forces $q'=1$.
Reversing the uniquely specified steps recovers exactly the continuant
sequence, proving existence and uniqueness of $n$.
\end{proof}

This is an ordinary Diophantine description of the \emph{possible output
fractions}; it does not by itself bind a fraction to a particular source
computation. Dropping that binding would replace a universal-computation
question by a decidable recurrence membership problem.

\begin{theorem}[Decidability of every specified rational level]\label{ste:cg:thm:level}
For fixed program and fixed $\alpha\ge3$, the predicate
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:rationallevel}
 g_\alpha(s)=r,
 \qquad s\text{ an empty-history source},\quad r\in\Q,
\end{equation}
is uniformly decidable. With binary integer input, it is decidable in
polynomial time in the bit sizes of $s$ and $r$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Reduce $r=p/q$ to lowest terms with $q>0$. Reject unless $0<p<q$ and
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:pell} holds. In the remaining case, run the descent in
Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:pell} to obtain its index $n$. Simulate the source for
$n-1$ transitions. Accept exactly when its first halt occurs at that time,
including time zero when $n=1$. Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:green} proves correctness:
an earlier halt gives a strictly smaller rational level, a later halt gives
a larger one, and an infinite run gives the irrational limit.

By~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:growth}, $D_n>2^n$, so $n<\log_2 q$. Euclidean reduction,
quadratic arithmetic, and the descent use integers of polynomial bit size.
During $O(\log q)$ simulated steps, each counter changes by at most one
per step and the history gains only a constant number of bits per step.
With a fixed instruction table, this is a polynomial-time computation.
\end{proof}

Consequently there is a uniformly decidable family of level sets whose
union, the rationality set, is computably enumerable complete. There is no
contradiction: deciding the countable union would require a computable
bound on which denominator, or which index $n$, must be checked.

\section{Fast approximation and unbounded exact separation}\label{ste:cg:sec:approximation}
\subsection{A rational interval algorithm}
Put $f(t)=1/(\alpha-t)$. On $[0,1/2]$, this map is increasing, maps the
interval into itself, and has derivative bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:lipschitz}
 \sup_{0\le t\le1/2}f'(t)=\frac1{(\alpha-1/2)^2}\le\frac4{25}<\frac14.
\end{equation}
Its unique fixed point in that interval is $\rho_\alpha$.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform polynomial-time approximation]\label{ste:cg:thm:approx}
For fixed program and $\alpha$, a rational interval containing
$g_\alpha(s)$ and of width at most $2^{-b}$ can be computed in time
polynomial in $b$ and the binary size of the source $s$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Set $n=\max(1,\lceil b/2\rceil)$ and simulate $n-1$ transitions. If a halt
is reached, compute its exact continuant fraction and return the degenerate
interval consisting of that fraction. Otherwise return
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:interval}
 I_n=[f^n(0),f^n(1/2)].
\end{equation}
The lower endpoint is $g_n$, while the upper endpoint is above $\rho$.
If the run later halts, its number of configurations is greater than $n$,
so its Green value lies between $g_n$ and $\rho$; a nonhalting run has value
$\rho$. Therefore the returned interval always contains the answer.
By~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:lipschitz},
\[
 |I_n|\le\tfrac12(4/25)^n<2^{-(2n+1)}\le2^{-b}.
\]
Each endpoint is obtained by $n$ rational fractional-linear updates; its
numerator and denominator have $O(n\log\alpha)$ bits. The bit-size and
simulation estimates from Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:level} now give the stated
polynomial bound.
\end{proof}

This algorithm does not attempt to recognize a nonhalting run. It uses the
same certified interval in every case in which no halt has yet been seen.
The conclusion is stronger than mere computability of the real: arbitrarily
fine approximation is compatible with the exact undecidability in
Corollary~\ref{ste:cg:cor:complete}. The separation rests on the absence of a
uniform computable gap from the irrational threshold, not on slow numerical
convergence.

\subsection{Certificate height and the absence of a cutoff}
For fixed $\alpha$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:height}
 \log_2 D_n=\Theta(n\log\alpha).
\end{equation}
This follows either from~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:closedD} or from
$(\alpha-1)D_{n-1}<D_n<\alpha D_{n-1}$. The denominator of $g_n$ is
exactly $D_n$, because Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:pell} gives coprimality. The unique
certificate has $n$ occupied configurations and maximum coefficient
$D_{n-1}$. Along an $n$-configuration run, history labels use $O(n\log B)$
bits and counter labels use the source bit length plus $O(\log n)$ bits.
A literal sparse certificate therefore has size
$O(n^2+n\,\mathrm{size}(s))$ for fixed program and $\alpha$.
This is a size bound in terms of actual runtime, not a polynomial bound in
source length.

\begin{theorem}[No computable uniform exact cutoff]\label{ste:cg:thm:nocutoff}
For a fixed universal program, there is no total computable function of
source bit length bounding, on all halting sources, any of the following:
the halting time; the number of occupied coefficients in the canonical
certificate; or its scalar $c$. There is also no total computable positive
rational function $\gamma(m)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:nogap}
 \rho_\alpha-g_\alpha(s)\ge\gamma(m)
\end{equation}
for every halting source of bit length at most $m$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A halting-time bound would decide halting by simulation. The support size
is the halting time plus one, so a support bound would give such a time
bound. A bound on $c=D_n$ also bounds $n$, since $D_n>2^n$.

Suppose a function $\gamma$ satisfying~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:nogap} existed. Since
$\rho<1/2$, equation~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:gap} gives
$\rho-g_n<2^{-(2n+1)}$. Compute an integer $N$ for which
$2^{-(2N+1)}<\gamma(m)$. No halting source of length at most $m$ could
have $n\ge N$. This again gives an effective time bound and decides
halting, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ This is the bounded-search argument of \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound} in~\cite{cdc}. Source~13 proves the same for support and charge and adds the largest prime factor of the charge (Corollary~\ref{ste:wc:cor:nobound}).


Equivalently, the maximum finite runtime among sources of bounded size
has no computable upper bound, and the smallest positive separation from
the threshold along those halting sources has no computable positive
lower bound. These statements do not require an unqualified claim about
which busy-beaver function eventually dominates every computable function
under every possible encoding.

\section{An explicit polynomial-functional presentation}\label{ste:cg:sec:polynomial}
\subsection{The sorts and operations}
Identify an array of finite support on $V$ with a vector
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:polyencoding}
 U=(U_r)_{r\in Q}\in\Z[X,Y,Z]^Q,
 \qquad e_{(r,a,b,h)}\longleftrightarrow e_r X^aY^bZ^h.
\end{equation}
The variables $X,Y,Z$ respectively encode the two counters and the history.
All polynomials have finite support; their degrees are not bounded in
advance.

Define the counter operators
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:counterops}
 M_XP=XP,\qquad E_XP=P(0,Y,Z),\qquad
 D_XP=\frac{P-E_XP}{X},
\end{equation}
and define $M_Y,E_Y,D_Y$ similarly. The quotient in this notation is an
exact polynomial because its numerator is divisible by $X$. On coefficient
arrays, $M_X^*=D_X$, $D_X^*=M_X$, and $E_X^*=E_X$.
For each digit $0\le d<B$, define
\begin{align}
 J_dP&=Z^d P(X,Y,Z^B),\label{ste:cg:eq:append}\\
 C_d\!\left(\sum_{a,b,h}p_{abh}X^aY^bZ^h\right)
 &=\sum_{a,b,k}p_{ab,Bk+d}X^aY^bZ^k.\label{ste:cg:eq:extract}
\end{align}
Thus $J_d$ appends a base-$B$ digit to each history exponent, and $C_d$
extracts the coefficients in that residue class while dividing the
remaining history exponent by $B$. Directly on monomials, $J_d^*=C_d$.
These are coefficient-linear maps. They are not multiplication by fixed
polynomials in the ordinary polynomial-ring language.

Let $E_{p,r}$ denote the state matrix unit carrying the $r$ component to
the $p$ component. For a branch $\sigma$ from $r$ to $p$, set
\[
 T_\sigma=
 \begin{cases}
 M_X\text{ or }M_Y,&\text{increment},\\
 D_X\text{ or }D_Y,&\text{positive decrement branch},\\
 E_X\text{ or }E_Y,&\text{zero branch}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then the two transport directions are explicitly
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:Scompiler}
 S=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}E_{p,r}J_{d_\sigma}T_\sigma,
 \qquad
 S^*=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}E_{r,p}T_\sigma^*C_{d_\sigma}.
\end{equation}
The counter and history operations commute because they act on different
exponents. Formula~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:Scompiler} agrees with the graph definition
on every monomial, including labels that are not reachable from any
chosen input, and hence on every polynomial by linearity.

\begin{theorem}[Linear quasi-Diophantine representation]\label{ste:cg:thm:poly}
For a finite program and an empty-history source $s=(r_0,a_0,b_0,0)$,
its halting relation is represented by the following fixed set of linear
polynomial-functional identities:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:polycertificate}
 \begin{split}
 (\alpha I-S-S^*)U&=c\,e_{r_0}X^{a_0}Y^{b_0},\\
 \sum_{r\in\Halt}U_r(1,1,1)&=1,
 \qquad U\in\Z[X,Y,Z]^Q,\quad c\in\Z.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Its witness is unique when it exists. The same assertion holds over every
nonzero torsion-free commutative $\Z$-algebra.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The identification~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:polyencoding} intertwines every monomial term
of~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:Scompiler} with the corresponding graph transport. Evaluation
at $(1,1,1)$ sums polynomial coefficients, so the second identity is
exactly~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:terminal}. Apply Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:canonical} and
Proposition~\ref{ste:cg:prop:torsionfree}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Making the extra operations explicit}
The counter boundary maps can be described by uniquely typed polynomial
splits. For example, $P=P_0+XD$ with $P_0\in\Z[Y,Z]$ uniquely determines
$P_0=E_XP$ and $D=D_XP$. Likewise every polynomial has a unique expansion
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:residuesplit}
 P(X,Y,Z)=\sum_{d=0}^{B-1}Z^d P_d(X,Y,Z^B),
 \qquad P_d\in\Z[X,Y,Z],
\end{equation}
and $P_d=C_dP$. Uniqueness follows by disjointness of the history-exponent
residue classes. Thus the functional notation can be replaced by a finite
list of linear identities with explicit sort restrictions and the fixed
substitution $Z\mapsto Z^B$.

The substitution is part of the language; it has not been eliminated into
ordinary integer polynomial arithmetic. Similarly, a source exponent such
as $a_0$ is a monomial input parameter, not a counted constant-size integer
loader. The representation is therefore \emph{quasi-Diophantine} in a
specified algebraic language. It is not an assertion that an ordinary
linear system in a fixed finite tuple of integer unknowns has an
undecidable existence problem.

\section{Ordinary quadratic slices with complete boundary rows}\label{ste:cg:sec:slices}
Let $E\subset V$ be a finite set of supplied configuration labels. A
candidate vector is described by coordinates $(u_v)_{v\in E}$ and is set
to zero outside $E$. Define the finite row set
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:rowset}
 W=E\cup N(E)\cup\{s\},
\end{equation}
where $N(E)$ contains all graph neighbors of vertices in $E$. Write
$u_w=0$ when $w\notin E$. Define the ordinary integer polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:slice}
 P_{s,E}(c,(u_v)_{v\in E})=
 \sum_{w\in W}
 \left(\alpha u_w-\sum_{\substack{v\in E\\v\sim w}}u_v
       -c\,\mathbf1_{w=s}\right)^2
 +\left(\sum_{v\in E\cap\Halt}u_v-1\right)^2.
\end{equation}
Here $E\cap\Halt$ denotes the vertices in $E$ whose state is halting.
The coefficients and index set of this polynomial are effectively computed
from the \emph{supplied} labels and program table.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-slice compiler]\label{ste:cg:thm:slice}
The polynomial $P_{s,E}$ has degree at most two in $|E|+1$ integer
variables. Its displayed sum has at most $3|E|+2$ residual squares.
It has an integer zero if and only if the source halts and its entire
history-lifted run is contained in $E$. In that case the zero is unique:
it is~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:canonical}, with zero coordinates at every extra label.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each residual is affine linear. Since graph degree is at most two,
$|W|\le3|E|+1$. An integer sum of squares is zero precisely when every
residual is zero. Rows outside $W$ vanish automatically: they have no
candidate coefficient, no candidate neighbor, and no source term.
Therefore a zero of~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:slice} is exactly a finite-support solution
of~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:certificate}. Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:canonical} shows that this
solution exists precisely under the stated condition and fixes every
coefficient uniquely.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why exterior rows cannot be omitted}
Suppose a nonhalting ray is truncated after its first $n$ vertices. The
finite tridiagonal system on those vertices alone accepts the continuant
column from~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:column}. But the next, omitted vertex has residual
$-1$, because it is adjacent to the last occupied coefficient $D_0=1$.
That exterior equation is part of the infinite operator even though its
own candidate coefficient is zero. Keeping only rows with labels in $E$
would silently change the problem to an artificially terminated path.

The verifier therefore generates the whole set~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:rowset}. It also
receives the source as an independent argument; a certificate is not
allowed to substitute an easier source for the one being verified.
These two requirements are important for a source-bound certificate,
regardless of how attractive a smaller residual count might appear.

\subsection{The fixed-arity boundary}
For each fixed $E$, the problem is just a finite system of integer linear
equations, written as a quadratic sum of squares. It is decidable.
The unbounded computation enters through existential choice of an
arbitrary finite support with arbitrary natural-number labels. Thus the
collection of all slices is not one fixed finite-variable quadratic
polynomial. Encoding those labels and their unbounded list into a fixed
integer tuple is a further arithmetic compilation problem.

MRDP supplies existence of an ordinary Diophantine representation for the
resulting computably enumerable source relation \cite{matiyasevich1970}. It does not imply that
this presentation directly yields a small, unique, or finite-fold ordinary
integer witness. A unique polynomial or finite-support witness can have
many integer codes or many auxiliary arithmetic certificates. No
finite-fold Diophantine conjecture is resolved here.

\section{A complete numerical certificate}\label{ste:cg:sec:example}
Consider the two-state program
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:exampleprogram}
 0:\ \TEST(1,0,1),\qquad 1:\ \HALT.
\end{equation}
The positive branch has tag one, the zero branch tag two, and $B=3$.
Start at $(0,2,0,0)$ and take $\alpha=3$. The complete lifted run is
\begin{align*}
 v_0&=(0,2,0,0),&v_1&=(0,1,0,1),\\
 v_2&=(0,0,0,4),&v_3&=(1,0,0,14).
\end{align*}
There are three transitions and four configurations. The continuants are
$D_0=1,D_1=3,D_2=8,D_3=21,D_4=55$, so the unique certificate is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:examplecertificate}
 c=55,\qquad (u_{v_0},u_{v_1},u_{v_2},u_{v_3})=(21,8,3,1),
 \qquad g_3(v_0)=\frac{21}{55}.
\end{equation}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,every node/.style={font=\small}]
\node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (v0) at (0,0) {$v_0$};
\node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (v1) at (3,0) {$v_1$};
\node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (v2) at (6,0) {$v_2$};
\node[draw,circle,minimum size=9mm] (v3) at (9,0) {$v_3$};
\draw[->] (v0) -- node[above] {tag $1$} (v1);
\draw[->] (v1) -- node[above] {tag $1$} (v2);
\draw[->] (v2) -- node[above] {tag $2$} (v3);
\node[below=4mm of v0] {$u=21$};
\node[below=4mm of v1] {$u=8$};
\node[below=4mm of v2] {$u=3$};
\node[below=4mm of v3] {$u=1$};
\node[above=8mm of v0] {source};
\node[above=8mm of v3] {halt};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A source-bound history path. The operator uses undirected edges;
the arrows display only the computational orientation.}
\label{ste:cg:fig:example}
\end{figure}

For direct row verification,
\begin{align*}
 3\cdot21-8&=55,&3\cdot8-21-3&=0,\\
 3\cdot3-8-1&=0,&3\cdot1-3&=0.
\end{align*}
The polynomial vectors are
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:examplepoly}
 U_0=21X^2+8XZ+3Z^4,\qquad U_1=Z^{14}.
\end{equation}
Formula~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:Scompiler} gives
\begin{align*}
 (SU)_0&=21XZ+8Z^4,&(SU)_1&=3Z^{14},\\
 (S^*U)_0&=8X^2+3XZ+Z^4,&(S^*U)_1&=0.
\end{align*}
Hence $(3I-S-S^*)U=(55X^2,0)$ and $U_1(1,1,1)=1$.
This checks both directions of the polynomial transport, not just the
forward computation.

For the supplied support $E=\{v_0,v_1,v_2,v_3\}$, the complete ordinary
quadratic is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:examplequadratic}
 \begin{split}
 &(3u_0-u_1-c)^2+(3u_1-u_0-u_2)^2\\
 &\qquad +(3u_2-u_1-u_3)^2+(3u_3-u_2)^2+(u_3-1)^2=0.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Its sole integer zero is $(u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3,c)=(21,8,3,1,55)$.
Finally, its output fraction satisfies
$55^2+21^2-3\cdot55\cdot21=1$; the descent is
\[
 (55,21)\to(21,8)\to(8,3)\to(3,1)\to(1,0),
\]
recovering the exact number of configurations.

\section{Passive electrical and stochastic substrates}\label{ste:cg:sec:physical}
\subsection{A countable passive resistor network}
Place conductance one on every graph edge. At every vertex $v$, add a
shunt to a common grounded boundary with conductance
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:shunt}
 a_v=\alpha-\deg_G(v)\in\{\alpha,\alpha-1,\alpha-2\}.
\end{equation}
All shunts are strictly positive. They can be realized as $a_v$ parallel
unit-conductance resistors, so no irrational component value is needed.
The common ground is a Dirichlet boundary; its possible infinite degree
does not alter the bounded degree of the non-ground configuration graph.

For a voltage vector $w$ and injected currents $j$, Kirchhoff's law at
$v$ is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:kirchhoff}
 \sum_{z\sim v}(w_v-w_z)+a_vw_v=j_v,
\end{equation}
which is precisely $L_\alpha w=j$. The energy identity is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:energy}
 \ip{w}{L_\alpha w}=
 \sum_{\{v,z\}\in E(G)}|w_v-w_z|^2+
 \sum_v a_v|w_v|^2.
\end{equation}
It holds first for finite-support vectors and then for $\ell^2$ vectors
by continuity and nonnegative convergence. Since $a_v\ge\alpha-2$, finite
energy implies membership in $\ell^2$; conversely, bounded degree makes
every $\ell^2$ vector finite-energy. There is therefore no unspecified
boundary choice at infinity in the finite-energy solution.

\begin{theorem}[Exact resistance represents halting]\label{ste:cg:thm:resistance}
In this fixed countable resistor network, inject unit current at an
empty-history source $s$ and remove it at ground. The unique finite-energy
voltage vector is $L_\alpha^{-1}e_s$, and the source-to-ground effective
resistance is $g_\alpha(s)$. Its rationality is equivalent to halting.
This remains true with all individual resistors having unit resistance
and with condition number as close to one as prescribed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:coercive} gives the unique solution of
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:kirchhoff}, and~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:energy} identifies the appropriate
finite-energy class. With unit source current, effective resistance is
the source voltage, the diagonal Green entry. Apply
Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:green}. Parallel unit shunts realize~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:shunt}
exactly; the condition-number statement is~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:condition}.
\end{proof}

The resistance/Green-function interpretation is consistent with the
classical framework of~\cite{doyle}; the particular arithmetic classification
is established above. This is \emph{static exact-observable universality}.
A finite resistor circuit is not being claimed to execute an arbitrary
unbounded computation, and a physical measurement is not being claimed to
decide exact rationality. The network is countable and finitely specified,
and the observable is defined exactly. The exponentially vanishing gap
in~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:gap} explains why robust finite-precision detection does not
provide a halting oracle.

\subsection{A uniformly killed random walk}
At each non-ground vertex, move to each graph neighbor with probability
$1/\alpha$, and otherwise move to a cemetery state and stop. The
substochastic transition operator is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:markov}
 P=A/\alpha.
\end{equation}
Its row sums are at most $2/\alpha<1$. Let $N$ be the number of visits to
non-cemetery vertices before death, counting the initial visit, and let
$N_s$ count visits to the starting vertex $s$.

\begin{theorem}[Undecidable exact arithmetic with bounded mean lifetime]
\label{ste:cg:thm:killed}
The walk dies almost surely. Uniformly in the source,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:lifetime}
 \Prob(N>t)\le(2/\alpha)^t\quad(t\in\N),\qquad
 \E N\le\frac\alpha{\alpha-2}.
\end{equation}
Nevertheless,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:cg:eq:returns}
 \E_s N_s=\alpha g_\alpha(s)
\end{equation}
has undecidable rationality for the universal source family. Given any
$\varepsilon>0$, a fixed integer $\alpha$ can make the uniform mean-lifetime
bound at most $1+\varepsilon$ without changing that conclusion.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At each step, conditional survival probability is at most $2/\alpha$.
Induction gives the tail bound, and summing tails gives the expectation
bound and almost-sure death. The expected number of visits to the start
is the sum of its return probabilities:
\[
 \E_s N_s=\sum_{k\ge0}(P^k)_{s,s}
 =\alpha(L_\alpha^{-1})_{s,s}.
\]
The series converges absolutely by the geometric bound. Multiplication
by the nonzero integer $\alpha$ preserves rationality, so
Corollary~\ref{ste:cg:cor:complete} applies. Finally,
$\alpha/(\alpha-2)=1+2/(\alpha-2)$ tends to one.
\end{proof}

The undecidable predicate is not almost-sure termination, which is
uniformly proved here, nor is it failure of an expected-time bound. It is
an exact arithmetic property of a well-behaved expectation. The transient
kernel is symmetric with respect to counting measure; no claim of a
finite stationary probability distribution on the non-ground state space
is needed.

\wtag\ This is the report's spine (Section~\ref{ste:sec:spine}) for a substochastic chain: a uniform geometric lifetime bound and a computable expectation whose exact arithmetic type is undecidable. Part~V of~\cite{pqc} (source~17 there) has the analogous statement for the stationary laws of reversible chains with a uniform spectral gap.

\section{Why positive characteristic breaks the certificate}\label{ste:cg:sec:characteristic}
Analytic coercivity is an ordered-field fact. Its integer consequences
extend to torsion-free coefficient rings, but not to every characteristic.
The obstruction can be made uniform and completely explicit.

Add a disjoint dummy sector to the program: one state decrements counter
one until zero and then enters a halting state. Its empty-history roots
supply finite path components of every length $n\ge2$, and an already
halted root supplies length one. This augmentation preserves the universal
sector. A second, unused counter supplies arbitrarily many disjoint copies
of each dummy path length.

\begin{lemma}[Singular continuants in every modulus]\label{ste:cg:lem:modular}
For every integer modulus $m\ge2$ there exists $n\ge1$ with
$D_n\equiv0\pmod m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The matrix
\[
 M=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}
\]
has determinant one, so it has finite order $r$ in
$\mathrm{GL}_2(\Z/m\Z)$. Since
$M^j(1,0)^{\mathsf T}=(D_j,D_{j-1})^{\mathsf T}$ and
$M^{-1}(1,0)^{\mathsf T}=(0,-1)^{\mathsf T}$, the index $n=r-1$
satisfies the assertion. The order is not one, since the lower-left entry
of $M$ is one modulo $m$, so $n\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A complete positive-characteristic obstruction]
\label{ste:cg:thm:modular}
For a program containing the dummy sector, and over every nonzero unital
commutative ring of positive characteristic, system~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:certificate}
can have a finite-support solution for a nonhalting source. It therefore
fails as a uniform halting representation in positive characteristic.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let the characteristic be $m\ge2$. Choose a dummy path of length $n$ with
$D_n=0$ in the ring, using Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:modular}. On that path place the
continuant vector $(D_{n-1},\ldots,D_0)$ and put zero elsewhere. Equation
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:column} now says $L_\alpha u=0$, while the terminal coefficient
still equals one, so $H(u)=1$. Set $c=0$. This pair satisfies
\eqref{ste:cg:eq:certificate} for any independently supplied nonhalting source,
even one in the universal sector.
\end{proof}

For $\alpha=3$ modulo two, length two already suffices:
$D_2=8=0$ and $(D_1,D_0)=(3,1)=(1,1)$.
The length-two matrix becomes
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
whose product with $(1,1)^{\mathsf T}$ is zero. The terminal coefficient
is still one. The archive contains this source-bound false positive and
verifies that it fails over the integers.

Adding $c\ne0$ is not a general repair: the valid normalized certificate
for a genuine halting path of singular length has $c=D_n=0$ in that same
ring. Such a restriction would also remove legitimate computations.

Characteristic zero alone does not ensure uniqueness. For example,
$R=\Z\times\F_2$ has characteristic zero but has additive torsion. At
$\alpha=3$, place the preceding modulo-two homogeneous vector in each
of two disjoint dummy length-two paths, using zero in the $\Z$ component.
Their terminal sums add to zero in $\F_2$. Adding this nonzero vector to
any valid certificate leaves both equations unchanged. This supplies an
explicit reason for using torsion-freeness in
Proposition~\ref{ste:cg:prop:torsionfree}, rather than merely characteristic zero.

\section{Implementation, validation, and integration}\label{ste:cg:sec:implementation}
\subsection{Executable contract}
The companion module \texttt{code/green\_machine.py} is a standard-library
Python implementation. It accepts immutable, validated finite instruction
tables and exact natural-number configuration labels. Boolean values and
floating-point values are not silently accepted as integer inputs.
The constructor freezes nested input tables so later caller mutation does
not change a compiled program.

Its main interfaces are \texttt{step} and \texttt{predecessor}; sparse
\texttt{apply\_l}; an independently implemented
\texttt{polynomial\_transport}; bounded \texttt{run}; canonical
\texttt{certificate} and \texttt{check\_certificate}; the ordinary
quadratic-row compiler \texttt{slice\_rows}; Pell descent and exact
rational-level testing; and the rational interval algorithm
\texttt{green\_interval}. A failure to find a halt within a supplied
horizon raises a horizon-exhaustion exception. It is never reported as a
proof of nonhalting.

The certificate checker evaluates all operator rows induced by the sparse
support and all exterior neighbors. It takes the source separately and
checks the terminal normalization. It does not trust a stored execution
trace, positivity claim, or a purported terminal designation. The finite
instruction table determines the semantics.

\subsection{What was actually checked}
The reproducible suite \texttt{code/verify.py} completed with
\textbf{110,522 exact checks in 43 categories}, using seed 20261002 and
Python 3.13.5. Counts are assertion counts, not counts of independently
proved theorems. Selected categories are summarized below; the JSON report
contains the complete ledger.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.72\linewidth}r}
\toprule
Check category & Checks\\
\midrule
Exhaustive small rational-spectrum membership & 30,300\\
Successor/predecessor round trips (two directions) & 18,792\\
Graph degree bound & 9,396\\
Independent polynomial transport versus graph transport & 3,600\\
Pell descent and continuant identity (combined) & 5,600\\
Independent exact Gaussian-elimination inverse entries & 546\\
Exhaustive small quadratic zero set & 1,056\\
Source-bound halting certificates & 825\\
Exact irrational enclosure checks on nonhalting increment rays & 450\\
Positive-characteristic false positives & 258\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The tests cover 30 explicit program tables, 28 generated by the fixed seed;
all increment, positive-test, and zero-test branches; history labels that
need not be reachable; signed sparse coefficient arrays; and a range of
$\alpha$ values and moduli. Independent rational Gaussian elimination is
compared with the continuant inverse formula. The finite quadratic
interface is checked with extra disconnected labels, exterior-row tests,
source substitutions, and coefficient tampering. A small zero set is
exhausted over all 1,056 assignments in its declared box. Invalid scalar
and constructor inputs are also tested.

These checks do not enumerate all counter programs, prove the analytic
statements, or validate a numerical universal-machine table. The general
claims rest on the proofs in this article. The implementation gives a
replayable artifact for the exact local construction and examples.

\wtag\ In this report the two programs are \path{code/12-coercive-green-green_machine.py} and \path{code/12-coercive-green-verify.py}; the examples are \path{data/12-coercive-green-countdown.json} and \path{data/12-coercive-green-modular_false_positive.json}; \path{validation/results.json} is \path{data/12-coercive-green-results.json}; \path{SOURCE_AUDIT.md} is \path{12-coercive-green-SOURCE_AUDIT.md}. Not shipped: the source's PDF and README, and \path{validation/test_output.txt}, a byte copy of \path{results.json}. The delivered README notes that the code numbers the two registers 0 and~1 (1 and~2 in the article) and calls the scalar $c$ \texttt{q}. \path{verify.py} imports \texttt{green\_machine} by its delivered name and rewrites \path{examples/} and \path{validation/results.json} under its parent directory, so it must run on a copy (README of this report). At placement it passed on a copy with 110,522 checks and reproduced both examples after line-ending normalization; the research programme's review~\cite{reviewCG} replayed it too and found no defect in this package.

\subsection{A proposed ProveIt interface}
A natural integration target is a separate research package under the
Hilbert-tenth-problem computational-substrate materials, not a replacement
for the existing universal-polynomial compiler. The logical dependency
chain should be kept explicit:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{0.29\linewidth}p{0.61\linewidth}}
\toprule
Layer & Obligation\\
\midrule
Counter frontend & Exact finite instruction semantics and input binding.\\
History lift & Injectivity, computable inverse, and strict history growth.\\
Finite algebra & Path continuants, exterior-row completeness, normalization.\\
Hilbert analysis & Bounded adjacency, coercivity, inverse positivity.\\
Observable & Finite-path ratio, ray root, Pell descent, gap formula.\\
Arithmetic backend & Separate encoding of arbitrary sparse supports, with
all auxiliary witnesses and input-loading costs accounted for.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The first three layers can be formalized without infinite-dimensional
analysis. One can prove the canonical finite-support representation
combinatorially, using the torsion-free argument. The fourth layer is
needed for the electrical and Green-function interpretations, and the
fifth connects exact values with computation. The final layer is future
work; this package does not silently charge its cost to an external
uncounted decoder.

\section{Further research questions}\label{ste:cg:sec:research}
The following questions concern extensions not proved by the present
construction. They are ordered roughly from directly implementable
formalization work to more speculative structural goals.

\subsection{A certified compiler with a frozen universal frontend}\label{ste:cg:q:frontend}
Implement a proof-producing version of the finite branch-table compiler
and attach an explicitly listed universal counter program. A useful
milestone is a formal theorem that emitted polynomial-functional rows
intertwine both graph transport directions for every monomial, including
unreachable labels. The current code checks this independently on many
examples, but a machine-checked all-input theorem and a frozen universal
input-loader would remove the remaining implementation-level universality
interface. Optimize measured state count only after this full contract is
fixed.

\subsection{Eliminating history substitution without losing coercivity}\label{ste:cg:q:history}
Can the operator be expressed using fewer polynomial coordinates or using
only typed polynomial subrings, without $Z\mapsto Z^B$, while retaining a
uniform positive lower bound and canonical finite-support witnesses? The
history coordinate enforces injectivity and a root for each component.
Any replacement must preserve both properties on all labels, not merely
on valid executions. A directed clock is sufficient for other
representations, but symmetrization can reveal predecessor collisions
that were harmless in the directed setting.

\subsection{Geometrically local realizations}\label{ste:cg:q:local}
The present graph is abstractly a path forest, but its effective vertex
labels are not a fixed nearest-neighbor lattice embedding. Can the same
exact rationality classification be realized by a fixed local rule in a
bounded-dimensional grid, with a simple computable source embedding and
uniform shunts? Merely drawing a path after simulating its length does
not answer this question. A successful construction must make adjacency
geometrically local before the halting behavior is known, and must analyze
the effect of wire subdivision on the observable and its condition bound.

\subsection{A connected network with the same exact classification}\label{ste:cg:q:connected}
The grounded electrical network is connected through its boundary, but
the non-ground configuration graph is a forest of separate components.
Can one connect those components by a fixed, computable, bounded-degree
non-ground structure while preserving a rational-versus-fixed-irrational
observable? Generic weak coupling destroys the exact finite-component
formula. This calls for exact decoupling identities or algebraically
controlled tails, not merely a perturbative estimate.

\wtag\ \emph{Batch~79, 2~October 2026: answered by source~13.} Part~V.B connects the components in the way asked for. Two copies of the path forest (rails), one coupling hub per configuration and a backbone ray give a fixed connected computable graph of maximum degree three, and the rail-swap involution together with a dipole source cancels every hub and backbone voltage exactly (Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:antisym}). The finite-support, rationality and least-charge classification survive, with resistance $2D_{L-1}/D_L$ on halting inputs and $m-\sqrt{m^2-4}$ otherwise (Theorems~\ref{ste:wc:thm:main} and~\ref{ste:wc:thm:resistance}). The answer uses a signed two-terminal source; Proposition~\ref{ste:wc:prop:positive} shows that a nonnegative finitely supported source cannot have a finitely supported response on any infinite connected graph of this kind.

\subsection{Other algebraic thresholds and output degrees}\label{ste:cg:q:thresholds}
Periodic Jacobi tails have algebraic response functions obtained from
fractional-linear fixed-point equations. Which algebraic thresholds can
replace $\rho_\alpha$ while retaining a finite alphabet of rational
coefficients and a uniform spectral lower bound? Can a similarly explicit
construction distinguish more than two prescribed algebraic degrees?
The present theorem already gives degree one versus two, but it does not
classify attainable higher-degree tails or canonical normalization in
those cases.

\subsection{Exact off-diagonal and multi-source observables}\label{ste:cg:q:offdiagonal}
For vertices on one finite path, inverse entries also have continuant
formulas. Determine which source-bound reachability and relative-time
relations can be captured by rationality, vanishing, or specified algebraic
identities among a fixed number of off-diagonal Green entries. A source
pair in different components gives zero automatically; a useful universal
relation must control that alternative explicitly. Multi-source versions
may expose additional information while retaining positive definiteness.

\subsection{Sharp decidable boundaries for effective path families}\label{ste:cg:q:decidable}
Find natural restrictions on the finite affine rule table under which
rationality of the root response becomes decidable. Candidates include
one-counter frontends, eventually periodic symbolic descriptions, or
bounded-history variations. The theorem rules out a criterion based only
on degree two, coefficient alphabet, self-adjointness, coercivity, or fast
approximation. A useful positive criterion must constrain the effective
presentation of endpoint reachability itself.

\subsection{Characteristic-independent symmetrized certificates}\label{ste:cg:q:characteristic}
Can one add a finite collection of typed constraints that prevents the
modular dummy-component kernels while keeping a useful self-adjoint
interpretation? The obstruction in Section~\ref{ste:cg:sec:characteristic} shows
that the current terminal normalization cannot work over all rings, and
simply requiring a nonzero source scalar loses genuine halting paths.
A repair would need a different normalization, a changed coefficient
module, or a proof that the extra constraints exclude every unwanted
homogeneous component without excluding valid computations.

\subsection{Support coding and ordinary witness multiplicity}\label{ste:cg:q:coding}
Translate the canonical sparse witness into a fixed-arity ordinary
Diophantine certificate with an explicit operation ledger. Track the
fibers of every encoding map: canonical polynomial support does not by
itself give a single-fold integer representation. A realistic intermediate
goal is a source-bound, unbounded-time ordinary compiler with a proved
surjection and fully accounted input conversion. Any reduction in the
repository's established arithmetic counts must be measured at that
complete interface.

\subsection{Robust promise problems and quantitative restrictions}\label{ste:cg:q:promise}
Under what computable restrictions on a source family can one prove a
positive separation from $\rho_\alpha$ and thereby turn approximation into
an exact classifier? For example, a proved runtime bound gives an explicit
gap by~\eqref{ste:cg:eq:gap}. Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:nocutoff} excludes a uniform solution
for all universal inputs, but leaves meaningful subclasses and promise
problems. The challenge is to derive such bounds from structural program
properties rather than assume the answer in the promise.

\subsection{Uniform killing with additional stochastic constraints}\label{ste:cg:q:killing}
The present walk has geometric killing and a symmetric transient kernel.
Can comparable exact-arithmetic universality coexist with a connected,
positive-recurrent chain carrying an explicitly computable stationary
probability measure and a uniform mixing bound? That question is not
settled by the killed construction: a cemetery state changes the invariant
measure and makes almost-sure termination trivial. Any extension must
separately establish its recurrence and stationary-measure claims.

\wtag\ Part~V of~\cite{pqc} (source~17 there) supplies reversible chains with a uniform spectral gap and an undecidable exact stationary equality; whether they can carry this Part's finite-support certificates is addressed by neither source.

\subsection{Realizing intermediate computability degrees}\label{ste:cg:q:degrees}
Replacing the universal recognizer by a fixed recognizer for another
computably enumerable set transfers that set to the source-bound
rationality predicate, subject to the chosen effective source encoding.
Develop an exact classification of which degrees can be obtained under
additional syntactic restrictions on the frontend and under uniform
bounds on source-loading complexity. The unrestricted transfer is immediate
from the construction; the interesting problem is the tradeoff with a
restricted, explicitly specified local rule set.

\section{Conclusion}
A fixed coercive linear operator can retain the entire halting problem in
the exact arithmetic structure of its inverse. There is no ill-posed
analytic equation: the inverse exists everywhere, has a known norm bound,
and is numerically approximable with a geometric modulus. Yet its
source column has finite support exactly on halting inputs, and its root
Green value is rational exactly on those same inputs.

The canonical certificate is particularly concrete. A finite computation
of length $n$ produces the continuant column
$(D_{n-1},\ldots,D_0)$ and source scalar $D_n$. The terminal value one
fixes the whole witness. At $\alpha=3$, these are even-index Fibonacci
numbers, and the nonhalting resistance is $(3-\sqrt5)/2$.
The quadratic Pell identity classifies the possible rational outputs,
while exact source binding still requires the computation.

The result supplies three linked substrates: a linear
polynomial-functional certificate system, a passive uniformly shunted
network, and a uniformly killed random walk. Their shared lesson is a
precise separation between numerical stability and exact arithmetic
decidability. It is this separation, rather than a claim of physical
hypercomputation or a new small ordinary universal polynomial, that the
construction establishes.

\section*{Part V.B: the second route (source 13)}
\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part V.B: the second route (source 13)}

\noindent\wtag\ Sections~70--81 print source~13 (batch-79 manuscript~14) in full; its two appendices are Appendices~\ref{ste:wc:app:algorithm} and~\ref{ste:wc:app:archive}. Its statements shared with source~12 carry bracketed pointers to Part~V.A, and their proofs are kept as second routes. Its abstract and keywords follow.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Abstract (source 13).} Can an exactly solvable, uniformly well-conditioned linear system still be a
universal quasi-Diophantine substrate? We construct a fixed computably presented,
connected graph of maximum degree three whose grounded conductance operator
$A=5I-J$ satisfies $2I\leq A\leq8I$. It has integer coefficients in
$\{0,-1,5\}$ and at most four nonzero entries per row. A computable family of
two-terminal sources $b_x$ satisfies
\[
 U(x)\text{ halts}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists q\in\N_{>0}\ \exists z\in\Z^{(W)}:\ Az=qb_x.
\]
Here the superscript denotes finite support, not a fixed witness dimension.
Every analytic equation $Au=b_x$ nevertheless has a unique, uniformly
computable $\ell^2$ solution, with an explicit geometric approximation rate.

The arithmetic certificate has an exact denominator law. A run of $T$ steps
has $L=T+1$ configurations; its least positive integral source charge is
$D_L$, where $D_0=1$, $D_1=5$, and $D_{j+1}=5D_j-D_{j-1}$. The primitive
finite-support certificate is unique. Effective resistance is rational exactly
on halting inputs and otherwise equals $5-\sqrt{21}$. An elementary rank and
valuation argument gives a contrasting decidability theorem: for every finite
set of primes $S$, membership of the solution in
$\Z[S^{-1}]^{(W)}$ is decidable, uniformly in $S$ and the input.

We also prove a sharp connected row-sparsity threshold, explain why a positive
one-terminal source cannot work, and give a single quadratic polynomial over
natural numbers for each external horizon, with a unique complete witness.
The package includes full proofs, an exact compiler, a 32-witness worked
example, and 38,524 reproducible checks. These are proposed structural
contributions, not a claim to solve a named classical open problem, establish
historical priority, improve a fixed universal-polynomial operation bound, or
settle finite-fold Diophantine representation.

\textbf{Keywords.} Diophantine representation; computable graphs;
resistor networks; finite support; positive-definite operators; Lucas sequences;
prime localization; counter machines.
\end{quote}

\section{The research question and the boundary of the contribution}
\label{ste:wc:sec:scope}

\subsection{From existence to structure}
The MRDP theorem already implies that every computably enumerable acceptance
relation has an ordinary integer-polynomial representation. ProveIt's
\texttt{Diophantine.mrdp} and \texttt{Diophantine.mrdp\_iff} expose precisely
such a fixed-polynomial, fixed-finite-witness statement, including input
zero~\cite{repo-mrdp}. Applying that interface to one more universal language
would not, by itself, reveal much about the language.

\wtag\ These three theorems are at lines 33, 42 and~26 of \path{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Lean/Diophantine/MRDP.lean}, unchanged from the source's pin to this write.

This manuscript asks more specific questions. Does universality of a linear
finite-support certificate require a singular or poorly conditioned analytic
operator? Can a connected passive network carry the same arithmetic? How
sparse can the operator be? And is its use of unrestricted rational
denominators essential?

The answers obtained here are: a uniformly coercive operator suffices; a
connected degree-three grounded network suffices; four nonzeros per row are
optimal in the fixed connected scalar class specified below; and, for this
construction, every finite-prime localization is decidable even though the
rational finite-support problem is universal. The prime-localization theorem
is not merely a restatement of MRDP: its proof identifies the exact
computation-dependent denominator and gives a finite, effective stopping
bound.

\subsection{Repository and prior-manuscript audit}
The repository reference point is the commit
\begin{center}\small\texttt{\pin}.\end{center}
The inspected Hilbert-tenth-problem README separates formalized results,
research reports, complete arithmetic ledgers, and externally bounded
substrate compilers~\cite{repoH10}. Its stated 87-operation universal
polynomial bound is not affected by this work. We have inspected source
interfaces, not rebuilt the repository or independently verified all of its
research claims.

Two supplied earlier manuscripts are particularly relevant. \emph{Linear
Quasi-Diophantine Universality} constructs unique linear polynomial
certificates and a bounded operator with dense nonclosed
range~\cite{linearreport}. The present operator instead has a bounded inverse
and full closed range. \emph{Arithmetic of Rapidly Mixing Reversible
Computation} separates effective approximation from exact stationary
arithmetic in uniformly gapped Markov chains~\cite{priorMixing}. The present
network gives a different invariant, exact finite support, together with
an explicit localization theorem and a row-sparsity lower boundary.
These earlier research manuscripts are antecedents, not independently
established literature results imported as axioms. Every new mathematical
assertion needed here is proved below, apart from standard computability and
Hilbert-space background.

\wtag\ \emph{Linear Quasi-Diophantine Universality} is batch-79 manuscript~01, placed in the report \emph{polynomial-witness-histories}~\cite{pwh} (note in Section~\ref{ste:cg:sec:scope}). \emph{Arithmetic of Rapidly Mixing Reversible Computation} is source~17 of~\cite{pqc}, printed in its Part~V. Source~12 of this report, written from the same snapshot on the same day, proves the disconnected form of this manuscript's main theorem; neither of sources~12 and~13 cites the other.

The history mechanism belongs to the classical reversible-computation
tradition~\cite{bennett}. Exponential inverse decay is also a classical
phenomenon~\cite{Demko}; our relevant bounds follow directly from a Neumann
series. We do not claim novelty for either ingredient. The contribution
under examination is the combined compiler and its exact algebraic,
connectedness, sparsity, and denominator consequences.

\subsection{Conventions and effectivity}
We use $\N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. For a countable set $W$ and a ring $R$,
\[
 R^{(W)}=\{u:W\to R:\supp(u)\text{ is finite}\}
\]
is the algebraic direct sum. On a discrete locally finite graph, finite
support is the intended meaning of compact support. An element is represented
by a finite list of vertex--coefficient pairs.

A \emph{computable presentation} includes a coding of vertices by natural
numbers and an algorithm returning the complete finite neighbor list of each
vertex. This is stronger than merely deciding whether a given pair forms an
edge. All our coefficients are exact integers. Computable real or Hilbert
space vectors are understood through effective rational approximation with
an explicit error bound.

A set is \emph{$\Sigma^0_1$-complete} when it is computably enumerable and every
computably enumerable set reduces to it by a computable many-one map. Fix a
universal halting set $K$. Universality statements below concern a single
fixed operator and a computable input-to-source map. The operator is not
rechosen after inspecting whether the input halts.

\section{A finite arithmetic compiler for reversible rooted paths}
\label{ste:wc:sec:history}

\subsection{The counter-machine interface}
A program has a finite state set $Q=\{0,\ldots,Q_0-1\}$, $d\geq1$ counters
in $\N$, a start state $q_*$, and exactly one halting state $H$. Every other
state has one instruction of either form
\[
 \inc(j,q')\qquad\text{or}\qquad\test(j,q_0,q_+).
\]
The first increments counter $j$ and changes state to $q'$. The second goes
to $q_0$ without changing counters when counter $j$ is zero, and otherwise
decrements it and goes to $q_+$. The halting state has no successor. We count
transitions, so an initially halting configuration has halting time zero.

This finite-counter interface supports a fixed universal program when a
sufficient fixed number of counters is permitted. One standard construction
stores the two halves of a Turing tape as base-$\beta$ stack integers, with
nonzero digits and zero for the empty stack. Fixed-base multiplication,
addition of a digit, quotient, and remainder are implemented by decrement
loops, finite-control remainders, and a finite supply of scratch counters.
The same loops convert an ordinary input counter to a binary input stack.
A fixed interpreter then gives a fixed universal program. No two-counter
ordinary-input minimality claim is needed. The repository contains a
verified finite-Turing-machine interface and counter simulation pipeline
relevant to this step~\cite{repoTM}.

For the mathematical construction we can take any program $P$. For the
completeness results choose one fixed $U$ with
\[
 x\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 U\text{ halts from }(q_*,x,0,\ldots,0).
\]
The reference implementation compiles arbitrary programs in the stated
interface; its small demonstration programs are not advertised as universal.

\subsection{Branch tags and a one-coordinate history}
Split each test into its zero and positive branches. Number all branches
$r=1,\ldots,R$. A branch specifies its source $s_r$, target $t_r$, update
$\Delta_r\in\{-1,0,1\}^d$, and either no guard, a zero guard, or a positive
guard. Set $B=R+1$; in the degenerate $R=0$ case use $B=2$.

Let
\[
 V=Q\times\N^d\times\N_{>0}.
\]
A vertex $v=(q,\bm c,h)$ has the edge
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:history}
 (s_r,\bm c,h)\longrightarrow
 (t_r,\bm c+\Delta_r,Bh+r)
\end{equation}
when branch $r$ is enabled. The natural number $h$ stores the complete
branch history as appended base-$B$ digits. Its initial value is $1$.

\begin{lemma}[Exact predecessor recovery]\label{ste:wc:lem:predecessor}
Every vertex has at most one predecessor and at most one successor. Both
can be computed without simulating an unbounded future. Every predecessor
has strictly smaller history coordinate.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Determinism gives the successor. To recover a predecessor of
$(q',\bm c',h')$, perform Euclidean division $h'=Bh+r$. Reject unless
$h\geq1$ and $1\leq r\leq R$. The tag determines the only possible branch.
Check $q'=t_r$, form $\bm c=\bm c'-\Delta_r$, check nonnegativity and its
branch guard, and verify \eqref{ste:wc:eq:history}. There is at most one candidate.
Since $B\geq2$ and $r\geq1$, a forward edge strictly increases $h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rooted-path compiler]\label{ste:wc:thm:paths}
The underlying undirected graph $G_P$ is a computably presented disjoint
union of finite rooted paths and one-sided infinite rooted paths. The
component of
\[
 s_x=(q_*,(x,0,\ldots,0),1)
\]
is a path of $L=T+1$ vertices if the computation first halts after $T$
transitions, and is a ray otherwise.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Degrees are at most two by Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:predecessor}. Backward iteration must
terminate because the positive integer $h$ strictly decreases. There are
no directed cycles, and an undirected cycle in a graph with both indegree
and outdegree at most one would necessarily be directed. Each component
therefore has a root and no branches. Nonhalting states always have a
successor, so a finite path ends precisely at a halting state. The vertex
$s_x$ has no predecessor because its history is $1$. Its forward trajectory
is exactly the original computation with its branch history appended.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ [The same as Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:injective} and Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:paths} of source~12, with $d$ counters and histories starting at~$1$ rather than~$0$; source~13's proof is kept as a second route.]


A computable bijection $\nu:\N\to V$ can be obtained by iterating Cantor's
pairing function on the counters and $h-1$, and then adjoining the finite
state coordinate. The code gives this bijection and its inverse explicitly.
Thus the entire graph is fixed once $P$ is fixed. In particular, one does
not decide in advance whether to build a finite path or an infinite one.

\section{A path resolvent and its exact integral denominator}
\label{ste:wc:sec:resolvent}

Fix an integer $m\geq3$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:continuant}
 D_0=1,\qquad D_1=m,\qquad D_{j+1}=mD_j-D_{j-1}\quad(j\geq1).
\end{equation}
Also put $U_0=0$, $U_1=1$, and $U_{n+1}=mU_n-U_{n-1}$, so that
$D_j=U_{j+1}$. These symbols always refer to the displayed fixed value of $m$.

Let $J_P$ be adjacency on $\ell^2(V)$ and set $A^{\rm path}_m=mI-J_P$.
For a graph of maximum degree two, the inequality
$2|ab|\leq |a|^2+|b|^2$ on each edge gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:pathbound}
 (m-2)I\leq A^{\rm path}_m\leq(m+2)I.
\end{equation}
This proves boundedness, self-adjointness, and a bounded inverse.

\begin{lemma}[Finite and infinite Green columns]\label{ste:wc:lem:green}
Let the source be the root of a component. If that component has $L$ vertices
numbered $0,\ldots,L-1$, the unique solution of
$A^{\rm path}_m v=\delta_0$ is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:finitegreen}
 v_i=\frac{D_{L-i-1}}{D_L}\quad(0\leq i<L),
\end{equation}
and is zero on all other components. If the component is a ray, then
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:raygreen}
 v_i=\rho^{i+1},\qquad
 \rho=\frac{m-\sqrt{m^2-4}}2\in(0,1).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The finite equations are $mv_0-v_1=1$, followed by
$mv_i-v_{i-1}-v_{i+1}=0$, with omitted exterior values zero. Substituting
\eqref{ste:wc:eq:finitegreen} verifies the first equation by
$mD_{L-1}-D_{L-2}=D_L$, the interior ones by the recurrence, and the last
by $D_1=mD_0$. The case $L=1$ reads $v_0=1/m$ separately.
The positive recurrence implies all numerators and denominators are
positive. For a ray, $m\rho-\rho^2=1$ and
$m\rho^{i+1}-\rho^i-\rho^{i+2}=0$; the sequence is in $\ell^2$.
Invertibility gives uniqueness in both cases and zero on other components.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ [The same as Lemma~\ref{ste:cg:lem:continuants} with Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:green} of source~12, with $m$ for~$\alpha$ and $L$ for~$n$; source~13's proof is kept as a second route.]


\begin{proposition}[Complete integral fiber]\label{ste:wc:prop:denominator}
For a root $s$ and $q\in\N_{>0}$, the equation
$A^{\rm path}_m z=q\delta_s$ has a finite-support integral solution exactly
when the root component is finite, of length $L$, and $D_L\mid q$.
Every such pair is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:integerfiber}
 q=kD_L,\qquad z_i=kD_{L-i-1},\qquad k\in\N_{>0}.
\end{equation}
The pair with greatest common divisor one across $q$ and all nonzero
coordinates is unique and corresponds to $k=1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every finite-support solution is in $\ell^2$, so Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:green} determines
it. A ray solution has infinitely many nonzero entries. On a finite path
the last entry is $q/D_L$, which proves necessity of divisibility; the other
entries then give sufficiency. The last coordinate in the primitive pair
is $1$, and the common divisor of the scaled pair is exactly $k$.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ [The same as Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:canonical} of source~12, which fixes the scale by the terminal normalization $H(u)=1$ instead of listing the whole fibre; source~13's proof is kept as a second route.]


The determinant of the $L$-vertex tridiagonal matrix is $D_L$, by expansion
along an end row. Also
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:gcd}
 \gcd(D_j,D_{j-1})=1
\end{equation}
by the Euclidean algorithm applied to \eqref{ste:wc:eq:continuant}. Thus the root
fraction $D_{L-1}/D_L$ is already reduced. At $m=3$, the even Fibonacci
subsequence satisfies the same recurrence, and
\[
 D_L=F_{2L+2},\qquad q_{\min}=F_{2T+4}.
\]
This Fibonacci specialization concerns the disconnected path operator;
our main connected integer network will use $m=5$.

\section{Connecting every component without losing its computation}
\label{ste:wc:sec:connected}

\subsection{The two-rail construction}
For every $v=\nu(n)$ make two vertices $v^+$ and $v^-$. Copy each edge of
$G_P$ within each rail. Add two further vertices $c_n,d_n$ and edges
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:connect}
 \{v^+,c_n\},\quad\{v^-,c_n\},\quad\{c_n,d_n\},\quad
 \{d_n,d_{n+1}\}\quad(n\geq0).
\end{equation}
Call the resulting graph $\mathcal G_P$, with vertex set $W$ and adjacency
$J$. It is connected because the $d_n$ form a backbone ray and every rail
vertex is attached to that ray. Each rail vertex has degree at most three,
each $c_n$ has degree three, and each $d_n$ has degree two or three.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1, every node/.style={font=\small},
 dot/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=1.8pt}]
 \node[dot,label=above:$v^+$] (p) at (0,1.1) {};
 \node[dot,label=below:$v^-$] (m) at (0,-1.1) {};
 \node[dot,label=above:$c_n$] (c) at (1.7,0) {};
 \node[dot,label=right:$d_n$] (d) at (3.6,0) {};
 \node[dot,label=right:$d_{n+1}$] (e) at (3.6,1.65) {};
 \node[dot,label=right:$d_{n-1}$] (f) at (3.6,-1.65) {};
 \draw (p)--(c)--(m) (c)--(d) (e)--(d)--(f);
 \draw[dashed] (p)--(-1.3,1.1) (p)--(-0.7,2.0);
 \draw[dashed] (m)--(-1.3,-1.1) (m)--(-0.7,-2.0);
 \draw[dashed] (e)--(3.6,2.15) (f)--(3.6,-2.15);
 \node[align=center] at (-2.1,0) {computational\\rail neighbors};
 \node[align=center] at (1.75,-2.0) {unit conductance\\on every edge};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One attachment cell, with $v=\nu(n)$. The dashed rail edges
represent at most two computational neighbors, not a prescribed geometric
embedding. At $n=0$ the lower backbone edge is omitted. Ground shunts are
not shown.}
\label{ste:wc:fig:cell}
\end{figure}

Let $S$ swap $v^+$ with $v^-$ and fix every $c_n,d_n$. This involution is
a graph automorphism. Fix now $m\geq5$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:connectedA}
 A_m=mI-J,\qquad b_x=\delta_{s_x^+}-\delta_{s_x^-}.
\end{equation}
(The localization theorem will additionally use odd $m$.) The same edge
estimate as before gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:connectedbound}
 (m-3)I\leq A_m\leq(m+3)I.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Antisymmetric decoupling]\label{ste:wc:lem:antisym}
The unique solution $u=A_m^{-1}b_x$ satisfies
\[
 u(v^-)=-u(v^+),\qquad u(c_n)=u(d_n)=0.
\]
Its restriction to the positive rail solves
$A^{\rm path}_m u_+=\delta_{s_x}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
