The involution commutes with $A_m$ and sends $b_x$ to $-b_x$.
Consequently both $Su$ and $-u$ solve $Aw=-b_x$, so uniqueness gives
$Su=-u$. At fixed vertices this forces zero. The equation at $v^+$ is
therefore the original path equation; at $c_n$ the opposite rail values
cancel, and at $d_n$ all values are zero. Conversely, lifting a path
solution with opposite signs on the rails and zero on the backbone solves
every equation, including all attachment equations.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Connected, coercive quasi-Diophantine universality]
\label{ste:wc:thm:main}
There is a fixed computably presented connected graph of maximum degree
three, with conductance operator $A=5I-J$, and a computable family of
sources $b_x$ having exactly two nonzero entries $+1,-1$, such that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:mainiff}
 x\in K\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A^{-1}b_x\in\Q^{(W)}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists q\in\N_{>0}\ \exists z\in\Z^{(W)}:\ Az=qb_x.
\end{equation}
The operator has at most four nonzeros per row, entries in $\{0,-1,5\}$,
and $2I\leq A\leq8I$. If $x$ halts after $T$ transitions, let $L=T+1$.
The least possible charge is $D_L$, the nonzero support has exactly $2L$
vertices, and the complete integral fiber consists of the positive
multiples of a unique primitive certificate.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply the construction to the fixed universal program $U$.
Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:paths}, Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:antisym} and Proposition~\ref{ste:wc:prop:denominator} prove every stated equivalence,
the support size, and the complete fiber description. Rational finite
support is equivalent to clearing finitely many denominators. The graph
and its complete neighbor algorithm were specified independently of the
input. The bounds and coefficient count follow from maximum degree three.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ Together with Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:antisym}, this answers source~12's research question of Section~\ref{ste:cg:q:connected} (dated note there).


The displayed quantifier over $\Z^{(W)}$ is genuinely algebraic but is not
an ordinary finite-dimensional Diophantine quantifier. Checking a proposed
finite-support witness is effective: evaluate $Az-qb_x$ on the union of
its support, the neighbors of that support, and the source vertices. All
other rows vanish automatically. This also directly proves the
computable-enumerability side of the assertion.

\subsection{A passive circuit, not an analog oracle}
Give every graph edge conductance $1$. At a vertex $w$, add a conductance
$m-\deg(w)$ to a common zero-potential ground. Kirchhoff's nodal equation is
\[
 \sum_{v\sim w}(u_w-u_v)+(m-\deg(w))u_w=b_w,
\]
which is exactly $A_mu=b$. At $m=5$ the shunts belong to the finite set
$\{2,3,4\}$. The degree-three claim refers to the connected graph before
adding the common ground reference; including that ground as a vertex
would give it infinite degree.

The signal is a two-terminal current injection and withdrawal. No active
or negative conductance is used. What is computationally universal is the
\emph{exact finite-support or arithmetic question} for an infinite
computably specified network. Neither a finite physical device nor finite
precision measurement is asserted to decide a halting problem.

\subsection{Arbitrarily close to the identity}
\begin{corollary}\label{ste:wc:cor:nearidentity}
For arbitrarily large fixed odd $m$, the same construction satisfies
\[
 \norm{I-A_m/m}\leq\frac3m,
 \qquad \kappa_2(A_m)\leq\frac{m+3}{m-3},
\]
while retaining \eqref{ste:wc:eq:mainiff} with the corresponding $D_L$.
Thus the normalized operators can be arbitrarily close to the identity,
and the condition-number upper bound can be arbitrarily close to one.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use $\norm J\leq3$ and \eqref{ste:wc:eq:connectedbound}. The path resolvent and its
finite-support dichotomy hold for each fixed $m\geq5$.
\end{proof}

\section{Fast analytic approximation and undecidable exact arithmetic}
\label{ste:wc:sec:analysis}

\subsection{The inverse is uniformly computable}
For every $m\geq5$, the operator-norm Neumann expansion gives
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:neumann}
 A_m^{-1}=\frac1m\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(J/m)^k,
 \qquad
 \norm{A_m^{-1}-\frac1m\sum_{k=0}^{N}(J/m)^k}
 \leq\frac{(3/m)^{N+1}}{m-3}.
\end{equation}
In \eqref{ste:wc:eq:neumann}, the norm bars denote the ordinary operator norm;
the bound is just the tail of a geometric series. For a finite rational
source, each partial sum has finite rational support and is computed from
a finite neighborhood. Multiplying the right side by the source norm
bounds the vector approximation error. For $b_x$ that norm is $\sqrt2$.

This proves considerably more than existence of an analytic solution:
there is one uniform algorithm producing arbitrarily accurate rational
finite-support approximations for every input, including nonhalting
inputs. The neighbor exploration cost is not asserted to be polynomial.
The distinction is not computable inversion versus noncomputable inversion;
it is computable approximation versus an undecidable exact support test.

\wtag\ [Compare Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:coercive} and Proposition~\ref{ste:cg:prop:effectiveinverse} of source~12, where the path-forest neighbourhoods also give a polynomial bit-time bound.]

\subsection{The exact resistance dichotomy}
Write
\[
 g_L=\frac{D_{L-1}}{D_L},\qquad
 \mathcal R_x=\ip{b_x}{A_m^{-1}b_x}.
\]
The latter is the voltage difference under a unit two-terminal current, or
effective resistance in the grounded network.

\begin{theorem}[Rational versus fixed quadratic resistance]
\label{ste:wc:thm:resistance}
For the fixed universal network and $m\geq5$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:resistance}
 \mathcal R_x=
 \begin{cases}
  2D_{L-1}/D_L,&\text{if }U(x)\text{ halts with }L=T+1,\\
  m-\sqrt{m^2-4},&\text{if }U(x)\text{ does not halt}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Consequently rationality of $\mathcal R_x$ is $\Sigma^0_1$-complete on this
computable family; equality to the displayed irrational constant is
$\Pi^0_1$-complete. At $m=5$ the nonhalting value is $5-\sqrt{21}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The source pairs with the two opposite root values, so the answer is twice
the root value in Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:green}. The finite values are rational. For
integer $m\geq3$, the integer $m^2-4$ lies strictly between $(m-1)^2$ and
$m^2$, hence is not a square. This makes the nonhalting value irrational.
The assertions of completeness now follow by equality of the corresponding
input sets with $K$ and its complement. No claim is made about rationality
tests for arbitrary computable real numbers outside this family.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ [The same as Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:resistance} of source~12 for a dipole source: the value is twice the root value $g_L$, and the nonhalting value $m-\sqrt{m^2-4}$ is $2\rho$. The closed forms \eqref{ste:wc:eq:binet}--\eqref{ste:wc:eq:distance} below are \eqref{ste:cg:eq:closedD}--\eqref{ste:cg:eq:gap} of source~12; here $\alpha$ denotes $\rho^{-1}$, source~12's $\lambda$, not a diagonal parameter. Source~13's proof is kept as a second route.]


For precise quantitative separation, let $\alpha=\rho^{-1}$ and
$\delta=\sqrt{m^2-4}=\alpha-\rho$. Solving the recurrence gives
\begin{align}
 D_j&=\frac{\alpha^{j+1}-\rho^{j+1}}{\delta},\label{ste:wc:eq:binet}\\
 g_L&=\rho\frac{1-\rho^{2L}}{1-\rho^{2L+2}},\label{ste:wc:eq:gclosed}\\
 \rho-g_L&=\frac{\delta\rho^{2L+2}}{1-\rho^{2L+2}}>0.
 \label{ste:wc:eq:distance}
\end{align}
The last expression strictly decreases with $L$, proving that the rational
values $g_L$ strictly increase to $\rho$. If the first $N\geq1$ configuration
vertices have been inspected and the run has not yet halted, its eventual
root value lies between $g_N$ and $\rho$. If it has halted, the exact
fraction is known. Equation~\eqref{ste:wc:eq:distance} is an exponential error
bound for this scalar procedure. Thus a number of simulated transitions
linear in the requested number of accuracy bits suffices, with a constant
depending only on fixed $m$. This does not give a bound on when the
procedure will discover exact rationality.

\subsection{A uniformly strongly convex energy}
Define on $\ell^2(W;\R)$
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:energy}
 E_x(u)=\tfrac12\ip{u}{A_mu}-\ip{b_x}{u}.
\end{equation}
The unique minimizer is $u_x=A_m^{-1}b_x$, and completion of the square
shows
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:energygap}
 E_x(u)-E_x(u_x)=\tfrac12\ip{u-u_x}{A_m(u-u_x)}.
\end{equation}
For $m=5$ this yields
\[
 \norm{u-u_x}^2\leq E_x(u)-E_x(u_x)\leq4\norm{u-u_x}^2.
\]

\begin{corollary}[Undecidable attainment on an algebraic domain]
\label{ste:wc:cor:attainment}
The restriction of $E_x$ to $\Q^{(W)}$ has an infimum equal to its Hilbert
space minimum. That infimum is attained exactly when $x\in K$. The same
attainment equivalence holds on $\R^{(W)}$. Moreover,
\[
 \min_{\ell^2} E_x=-\tfrac12\mathcal R_x
\]
is rational exactly on halting inputs.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Finite rational arrays are dense in $\ell^2$, and the energy is continuous.
Attainment on either subdomain forces equality with the unique Hilbert
space minimizer by \eqref{ste:wc:eq:energygap}. That minimizer has finite support
exactly in the finite-path case, where it is rational. The formula for the
minimum follows by substituting $A_mu_x=b_x$.
\end{proof}

Here coercivity, strict convexity, invertibility, and an explicit analytic
error bound all hold. The algebraic finite-support domain is not closed
in the Hilbert topology. It is this domain restriction, rather than a
nonclosed operator range, that carries the obstruction.

\section{A finite-prime localization restores decidability}
\label{ste:wc:sec:localization}

\subsection{The fixed-amplitude question}
For a finite set of primes $S$, put
\[
 R_S=\Z[S^{-1}]
 =\{a/b\in\Q:\text{all prime divisors of }b\text{ belong to }S\}.
\]
We now ask whether the \emph{unit-source} solution $A_m^{-1}b_x$ belongs to
$R_S^{(W)}$. The free charge $q$ in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:mainiff} must not be silently
reintroduced: allowing arbitrary $q$ already makes integral certificates
universal. Equivalently, the localized question permits a scaled integral
certificate only when $q$ itself has no prime factors outside $S$.

By the final coordinate $1/D_L$ in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:finitegreen},
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:localcriterion}
 A_m^{-1}b_x\in R_S^{(W)}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \bigl[U(x)\text{ halts at length }L
 \text{ and }D_L\text{ has no prime factor outside }S\bigr].
\end{equation}
To make this a decision procedure we need an effective upper bound on
such $L$. We give an elementary proof rather than invoke a primitive-divisor
theorem or a general effective $S$-unit theorem.

\subsection{Ranks of apparition}
Throughout this section $m\geq3$ is odd. For a prime $p$, let
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:rank}
 z_p=\min\{n\geq1:p\mid U_n\}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Effective rank and exact divisibility]\label{ste:wc:lem:rank}
The integer $z_p$ exists, can be found by a finite modular recurrence, and
satisfies $1\leq z_p\leq p^2-1$. For every $n\geq1$,
\[
 p\mid U_n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad z_p\mid n.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The recurrence map $(a,b)\mapsto(b,mb-a)$ is invertible over $\F_p$. Its
orbit from $(0,1)$ stays among the $p^2-1$ nonzero pairs and returns to
$(0,1)$; at a return time its first coordinate is zero. This proves existence
and the search bound.

For the divisibility assertion use
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:matrixLucas}
 M=\begin{pmatrix}m&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 M^n=U_nM-U_{n-1}I\quad(n\geq1).
\end{equation}
The identity follows by induction, or from $M^2=mM-I$. Modulo $p$, the
matrix $M^n$ is scalar exactly when $U_n=0$, because its lower-left entry
is $U_n$. The integers $n\in\Z$ for which the power of the invertible
matrix $M$ is scalar form a subgroup of $\Z$. Its least positive element
is $z_p$, proving the claim.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A valuation-lifting proof with the prime two included}
The recurrence and $U_1=1$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:coprimeU}
 \gcd(U_n,U_{n-1})=1\qquad(n\geq2).
\end{equation}
For $n=1$ the corresponding assertion with $U_0=0$ is also true.

\begin{lemma}[Exact valuation on rank multiples]\label{ste:wc:lem:valuation}
For odd $m\geq3$, every prime $p$, and every $k\geq1$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:valuation}
 v_p(U_{z_pk})=v_p(U_{z_p})+v_p(k).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $z=z_p$, $a=-U_{z-1}$, $b=U_z$, and $v=v_p(b)\geq1$.
Equation~\eqref{ste:wc:eq:coprimeU} gives $p\nmid a$. From
$M^z=aI+bM$ and the binomial theorem, taking lower-left entries yields
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:binomialU}
 U_{zk}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\binom{k}{j}a^{k-j}b^j U_j.
\end{equation}
The $j=1$ term has valuation $v+v_p(k)$. The identity
$j\binom{k}{j}=k\binom{k-1}{j-1}$ gives
\[
 v_p\!\binom{k}{j}\geq v_p(k)-v_p(j).
\]
Consequently, for $j\geq2$, the $j$th term of \eqref{ste:wc:eq:binomialU} has
valuation at least
\[
 v+v_p(k)+(j-1)v-v_p(j).
\]
For odd $p$, $(j-1)v-v_p(j)\geq1$: indeed $v\geq1$ and
$v_p(j)\leq j-2$ for $j\geq2$. All these terms therefore have strictly
larger valuation than the first.

It remains to check $p=2$ rather than import an odd-prime argument
unchanged. Since $m$ is odd, $U_n$ modulo two has the repeating pattern
$0,1,1$, so $z_2=3$. Moreover $U_3=m^2-1$ is divisible by eight. Thus
$v\geq3$; in particular $v\geq2$, which makes
$(j-1)v-v_2(j)\geq1$ for every $j\geq2$. Again the first term is the
unique term of lowest valuation. The valuation of the sum is its
valuation, proving \eqref{ste:wc:eq:valuation} for every prime.
\end{proof}

No estimate here is asymptotic. The small-rank values, including the
exceptional behavior at two, enter an exact computable constant.

\begin{theorem}[An explicit finite-prime length bound]\label{ste:wc:thm:Sbound}
For a finite prime set $S$ define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:CS}
 c_p=\max\{0,v_p(U_{z_p})-v_p(z_p)\},\qquad
 C_S=\prod_{p\in S}p^{c_p},
\end{equation}
with the empty product equal to $1$. Let $B_S$ be the first integer
$L\geq1$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:BS}
 (m-1)^L>C_S(L+1).
\end{equation}
Then $B_S$ is effectively computable. If $D_L$ has no prime factor outside
$S$, then $L<B_S$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Lemmas~\ref{ste:wc:lem:rank} and~\ref{ste:wc:lem:valuation}, for every $n\geq1$ and prime $p$,
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:majorant}
 v_p(U_n)\leq c_p+v_p(n).
\end{equation}
When $z_p\nmid n$ the left side is zero. Otherwise the exact valuation is
$v_p(U_{z_p})+v_p(n)-v_p(z_p)$, proving the inequality.
If $U_n$ has all its prime factors in $S$, it follows that
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:smoothmajorant}
 U_n\leq C_S\prod_{p\in S}p^{v_p(n)}\leq C_S n.
\end{equation}
On the other hand $U_1=1$, $U_2=m$, and the positive increasing recurrence
implies $U_{n+1}\geq(m-1)U_n$, so
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:expgrowth}
 U_n\geq(m-1)^{n-1}\qquad(n\geq1).
\end{equation}
Put $n=L+1$. Smoothness forces $(m-1)^L\leq C_S(L+1)$.
The ratio $(m-1)^L/(L+1)$ is strictly increasing for $L\geq1$, because
its successive ratio is $(m-1)(L+1)/(L+2)>1$; it tends to infinity.
The first failure can be found by integer arithmetic, and every later
length fails as well. All constants in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:CS} are computable by the
finite rank search and exact integer valuations.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Uniform localized decidability]\label{ste:wc:cor:localdecide}
For each fixed odd $m\geq5$, the predicate
\[
 (x,S)\longmapsto [A_m^{-1}b_x\in\Z[S^{-1}]^{(W)}]
\]
is decidable for finite prime sets $S$. More generally the same algorithm
works uniformly for any finite counter program supplied to the compiler.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Compute $B_S$. If $B_S\leq1$, reject. Otherwise simulate through transition
number $B_S-2$, including the initial configuration. If a halt is found
at length $L<B_S$, compute $D_L$ and repeatedly divide out primes in $S$.
Accept exactly when the remaining integer is $1$. If no halt has occurred,
reject. Equation~\eqref{ste:wc:eq:localcriterion} and Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:Sbound} prove both
directions, including inputs which eventually halt after this finite bound.
\end{proof}

The theorem is family-specific. It is not a decision theorem for arbitrary
Diophantine equations over $\Z[S^{-1}]$, nor a claim that those rings cannot
support other universal representations. In fact our own unrestricted
charge equation $Az=qb_x$ remains universal with integral $z$.

\begin{example}[A small explicit bound]
At $m=3$ and $S=\{2,3\}$, the ranks are $z_2=3$ and $z_3=2$.
The valuations give $c_2=3$, $c_3=1$, and $C_S=24$. The first $L$ with
$2^L>24(L+1)$ is $L=8$, so every admissible length is below eight.
Checking those seven values gives precisely $L=1,2,5$, with denominators
$3,8,144$. This example illustrates the disconnected Fibonacci case;
the same algorithm, with $m=5$, applies to the connected network.
\end{example}

\subsection{Noncomputable certificate resources}
\begin{corollary}\label{ste:wc:cor:nobound}
For a fixed universal program, none of the following resources on its
halting inputs has a total computable upper bound as a function of input:
the finite-support size, the least charge $D_L$, or the largest prime factor
of $D_L$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A support bound gives a halting-time bound because support has size $2L$.
A charge bound gives a length bound from $(m-1)^L\leq D_L$.
Either would decide halting by bounded simulation.

For the last assertion, suppose $f(x)$ bounds every prime factor of $D_L$
whenever $x$ halts. Compute the finite set of primes at most $f(x)$ and
apply Corollary~\ref{ste:wc:cor:localdecide}. Under the assumed bound it accepts every
halting input and no nonhalting input, again deciding $K$.
\end{proof}

\wtag\ [The support and charge clauses are the same as Theorem~\ref{ste:cg:thm:nocutoff} of source~12; all three clauses are the bounded-search argument of \texttt{cdc:bd:prop:nobound} in~\cite{cdc}. The prime-factor clause is new with source~13.]


Thus the decidable finite-prime fragments form an effectively indexed
cover of the rational finite-support halting relation, but no computable
input-dependent prime cutoff captures all its positive instances.

\section{Sharp sparsity boundaries and the necessity of a dipole}
\label{ste:wc:sec:sparsity}

\subsection{Connected scalar operators with at most three entries per row}
We make the lower-bound class explicit. An operator has a computably
presented countable vertex set, rational entries, a nonzero diagonal,
symmetric off-diagonal support, and complete finite neighbor lists. The
support graph records nonzero off-diagonal entries. In particular,
positive-definite scalar conductance matrices belong to this class.

\begin{theorem}[A fixed connected row-three decision boundary]
\label{ste:wc:thm:row3}
For every fixed connected operator in the preceding class with at most
three nonzero entries per row, finite-support rational solvability of
$Au=b$ is decidable as a function of the supplied finite rational source
$b$. Consequently no such fixed connected operator can realize
\eqref{ste:wc:eq:mainiff}. Four entries per row are sufficient by
Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:main}, and hence are optimal in this specified class.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The nonzero diagonal leaves at most two off-diagonal neighbors. A connected
simple graph of maximum degree two is a finite path, a finite cycle, a
one-sided ray, or a two-sided infinite path. A single vertex is included
in the finite case.

For a finite graph, solve its finite rational system. For a two-sided path,
let $K_b$ be the finite interval spanned by the source support. If a
finite-support solution had a nonzero coordinate beyond that interval,
choose the outermost such coordinate on that side. At the next vertex,
the source is zero, the solution value and the value farther out are zero,
but the inward neighbor is nonzero. That row reads a nonzero edge
coefficient times a nonzero solution value equal to zero, a contradiction.
Thus every finite-support solution is contained in $K_b$.

For a ray, include its endpoint together with the source support when
forming $K_b$. The same outermost-coordinate argument beyond the far end
bounds every finite-support solution by this interval. For a zero source,
the zero solution is immediately sufficient; otherwise these constructions
apply directly.

The needed finite paths can be found by exhaustive search in the
computable connected neighbor graph. Restrict unknowns to $K_b$, set
exterior values to zero, and include equations at $K_b$ and all its
neighbors. This is a finite rational linear system, decidable by Gaussian
elimination. Including the neighbor rows is essential for sufficiency.
\end{proof}

There is a quantifier qualification in this theorem. The decision algorithm
exists for \emph{each fixed} connected graph. In the ray case it may hardcode
that graph's unique endpoint. We do not assert a uniform algorithm which,
from an arbitrary graph description, decides whether it is a ray or a
two-sided path, or finds an endpoint when one exists. This nonuniformity
in the fixed operator does not weaken the claimed exclusion of a single
fixed universal row-three operator.

If symmetry and positive definiteness are additionally assumed, analytic
uniqueness is automatic; the finite-support decision proof does not need
that stronger hypothesis. The lower bound does not concern block-valued
vertices, hidden auxiliary sorts, or a representation in which the
operator itself is allowed to vary with the input.

\subsection{The disconnected threshold is three}
Without connectedness, two nonzeros per row and a nonzero diagonal leave
maximum off-diagonal degree one. Components have one or two vertices. For
a fixed rational positive-definite operator, every finite rational source
therefore has a finite rational solution obtained by solving finitely
many blocks. The disconnected path construction in Section~\ref{ste:wc:sec:resolvent}
uses three nonzeros per row and already realizes the universal
finite-support problem. Thus connectedness increases the sharp scalar
threshold from three to four under these conventions.

\subsection{Why a single positive source cannot work}
\begin{proposition}[Positivity obstruction]\label{ste:wc:prop:positive}
Let $G$ be an infinite connected graph of maximum degree $\Delta$, and
$m>\Delta$. Then every coordinate of $(mI-J_G)^{-1}\delta_s$ is strictly
positive. In particular it never has finite support.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The Neumann series has nonnegative entries. For any vertex $v$, connectivity
provides a finite walk from $s$ to $v$, of some length $k$, so the
$(v,s)$ entry of $J_G^k$ is positive. The corresponding positive term
occurs in the inverse series.
\end{proof}

The same argument works for a nonzero finitely supported nonnegative
source. The two-terminal dipole is not cosmetic: its opposite signs let
an exact graph symmetry cancel all backbone voltages while preserving
a nontrivial computation in the antisymmetric sector. The network is
passive, but its imposed current source is signed.

\section{One quadratic polynomial at every external horizon}
\label{ste:wc:sec:quadratic}

The linear module equation is the unbounded-support representation. This
section supplies a separate, completely finite arithmetic interface, with
all configuration and circuit data paid for. Its arity depends on a supplied
horizon $T$. The construction is useful for exact checking and formalization;
it is not the main source of universality or a new fixed-arity quadratic
representation of all halting computations.

\subsection{All variables and all linear residuals}
Fix a program $P$, dimension $d$, branch count $R$, and number $Z$ of test
instructions. Input coordinates $x_0,\ldots,x_{d-1}$ are parameters, not
witnesses. For $0\leq t\leq T$, introduce the state $q_t$, counters
$c_{t,j}$, and history $h_t$. For $0\leq t<T$ and $1\leq r\leq R$,
introduce a natural selector $b_{t,r}$ and routed counters $a_{t,r,j}$.
For every positive test branch also introduce a natural slack $s_{t,r}$.
Finally introduce $u_0,\ldots,u_T$ and a charge $Q_c$. All these unknowns
range over $\N$.

The initial and terminal residuals are
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:initres}
 q_0-q_*,\quad h_0-1,\quad c_{0,j}-x_j\ (0\leq j<d),\quad q_T-H.
\end{equation}
At each $t<T$, include the following linear residuals:
\begin{align}
 &\sum_r b_{t,r}-1,\label{ste:wc:eq:selectorres}\\
 &q_t-\sum_r s_r b_{t,r},\qquad
 q_{t+1}-\sum_r t_r b_{t,r},\label{ste:wc:eq:stateres}\\
 &c_{t,j}-\sum_r a_{t,r,j}\quad(0\leq j<d),\label{ste:wc:eq:routeres}\\
 &c_{t+1,j}-c_{t,j}-\sum_r\Delta_{r,j}b_{t,r}
       \quad(0\leq j<d),\label{ste:wc:eq:updateres}\\
 &h_{t+1}-Bh_t-\sum_r r b_{t,r}.\label{ste:wc:eq:histres}
\end{align}
Here $s_r$ in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:stateres} denotes the fixed \emph{source state} of
branch $r$, whereas $s_{t,r}$ below is a witness slack; their indices
separate the two uses. For a zero-test branch testing counter $j_r$, include
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:zerores}
 a_{t,r,j_r};
\end{equation}
for a positive-test branch, include
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:posres}
 a_{t,r,j_r}-b_{t,r}-s_{t,r}.
\end{equation}
There are $Z$ branches of each of these two kinds.

For the electrical layer, set the omitted symbols $u_{-1}=u_{T+1}=0$,
without introducing them as variables, and include
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:circuitres}
 m u_i-u_{i-1}-u_{i+1}-Q_c\mathbf1_{i=0}\quad(0\leq i\leq T),
 \qquad u_T-1.
\end{equation}
These are equations on a path of $T+1$ configuration vertices. They will
be lifted to the actual connected graph only after the machine layer
has certified that the last vertex is a genuine halt.

\subsection{Natural nonnegativity replaces squared routing gates}
The selectors are automatically zero or one when they are natural and
sum to one. Instead of squaring quadratic routing equalities, use the
nonnegative polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:cross}
 C_t=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}\ \sum_{r\ne s} b_{t,s}a_{t,r,j}.
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal L_T$ be the complete list of linear residuals
\eqref{ste:wc:eq:initres}--\eqref{ste:wc:eq:circuitres}. Define
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:PT}
 P_T(\bm x,\bm w)=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L_T}\ell^2+
                  \sum_{t=0}^{T-1} C_t.
\end{equation}
This is a single integer polynomial of total degree at most two. On natural
coordinates every summand is nonnegative. The construction does not assert
nonnegativity on arbitrary signed real assignments.

\begin{lemma}[Exact routing]\label{ste:wc:lem:routing}
Suppose $\bm b,\bm a$ are natural, $\sum_r b_r=1$, and
$\sum_{j,r\ne s}b_s a_{r,j}=0$. There is a unique selected branch $r_*$
with $b_{r_*}=1$, and $a_{r,j}=0$ for every $r\ne r_*$. If additionally
$c_j=\sum_r a_{r,j}$, then $a_{r_*,j}=c_j$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The sum-one equation on natural coordinates selects exactly one index.
For each $r\ne r_*$, the nonnegative sum contains
$b_{r_*}a_{r,j}=a_{r,j}$, so it forces all those entries to zero.
The remaining assertion follows from the counter sum.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A quadratic, complete, unique finite certificate]
\label{ste:wc:thm:quadratic}
For every fixed program $P$, integer $m\geq3$, horizon $T\geq0$, and
natural input vector $\bm x$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:quadraticiff}
 \exists\bm w\in\N^{N_T}: P_T(\bm x,\bm w)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 P\text{ first halts after exactly }T\text{ transitions}.
\end{equation}
Whenever a zero exists, its \emph{entire} witness tuple is unique. It has
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:quadcounts}
 N_T=(d+3)(T+1)+1+T\bigl((d+1)R+Z\bigr)
\end{equation}
natural witnesses, excluding the $d$ input parameters. The displayed
compiler uses
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:rescounts}
 d+5+T(5+2d+2Z)
\end{equation}
linear residuals and $dR(R-1)T$ unsquared cross-term occurrences.
Its electrical part is
\begin{equation}\label{ste:wc:eq:canonicalelectrical}
 u_i=D_{T-i},\qquad Q_c=D_{T+1}.
\end{equation}
For $m\geq5$ this tuple reconstructs the primitive connected-network
certificate of Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:main}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At a natural zero every squared residual and every nonnegative cross term
vanishes. At each step Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:routing} selects exactly one branch and
makes its routed counters the actual counters. The state residuals give
that branch's correct source and target. A selected zero branch is
permitted only when the tested counter vanishes. A selected positive
branch has tested counter $1+s_{t,r}\geq1$. The update residuals implement
its actual counter change. Inactive positive-branch slacks are forced to
zero because their routed counter and selector are both zero.

Starting from \eqref{ste:wc:eq:initres}, induction therefore recovers exactly the
deterministic computation, including every state, counter, selector,
routed counter, slack, and history value. If a halt occurred before $T$,
there would be no branch with that source state to satisfy the next
sum-one and state equations. The terminal equation forces a halt at $T$.
Thus the halting time is exact, not merely bounded.

The final equation $u_T=1$, followed by the circuit equations in reverse
order, forces successively $u_{T-1}=m$, $u_{T-2}=m^2-1$, and in general
$u_i=D_{T-i}$. The first row then forces $Q_c=D_{T+1}$. This also proves
positivity of the charge without an extra positive slack and proves
uniqueness of the remaining witnesses. For $T=0$, the two circuit rows
are simply $mu_0-Q_c=0$ and $u_0-1=0$.

Conversely, supply an actual first-halting trace. Give its selected branch
value one, all other selectors zero, put the actual counters in its routed
coordinates, and set selected positive slacks to the tested counter minus
one and other slacks to zero. Append the prescribed history and the values
\eqref{ste:wc:eq:canonicalelectrical}. Every residual and cross term then
vanishes. All witnesses are natural, proving existence and the stated
bijection.

For the count, there are $(d+2)(T+1)$ configuration coordinates,
$(d+1)RT$ selectors and routed counters, $ZT$ positive slacks, and $T+2$
electrical coordinates. There are $d+3$ initial/terminal rows,
$4+2d+2Z$ transition rows per step, and $T+2$ electrical rows. These are
exactly \eqref{ste:wc:eq:quadcounts} and \eqref{ste:wc:eq:rescounts}.
Finally, place $u_i$ on the positive rail of the $i$th recovered history
vertex, $-u_i$ on its negative rail, and zero elsewhere. Its final vertex
is a genuine halt; Lemma~\ref{ste:wc:lem:antisym} verifies all graph equations, not
just a finite principal block.
\end{proof}

These counts describe the literal reference compiler, not an optimal
quadratic representation. For example, the positive slacks and their
rows can be projected away: on the selected positive branch, natural
successor counters in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:updateres} already force a positive tested
counter. Their unique restoration is the tested counter minus one, or
zero on an inactive branch. We retain them to expose the branch guards
explicitly and keep the code, residual list, and witness count identical.

\wtag\ The transition layer of this compiler (natural one-hot selectors, routed counters with the unsquared cross terms $b_{t,s}a_{t,r,j}$, guard slacks) is a variant of the degree-two counter-machine compilers with unsquared natural products in the report \emph{liveness-beyond-halting}~\cite{lbh} (\texttt{lbh:qd:thm:compiler} and \texttt{lbh:ql:thm:compiler}, compared in \texttt{lbh:sec:threecompilers}) and of the quadratic bounded counter execution of~\cite{cdc} (display \texttt{cdc:eq:bigM}). It is printed with source~13's proof and without a novelty claim. New is the electrical layer \eqref{ste:wc:eq:circuitres}, which forces $u_i=D_{T-i}$ and $Q_c=D_{T+1}$ and lifts to the connected network. The research programme's review~\cite{reviewCG} remarked that the routing cross sum could be factored by sharing the selector and routed-counter sums. The programme has since emitted complete paid straight-line evaluators of this unchanged polynomial~\cite{slpCG} (commit \texttt{9bc875b6a}): with $B=\sum_rb_{t,r}$ and $A^*_r=\sum_ja_{t,r,j}$, the identity $C_t=\sum_r(B-b_{t,r})A^*_r$ holds over all integer tuples, and the complete cost of evaluating $P_T$ for a two-counter transfer program at horizon~3 falls from 245 to 206 operations. These are comparisons of explicitly emitted evaluators, not lower bounds or a fixed-arity universal equation. The slack projection above remains the author's observation and is not implemented.

\subsection{Why this is not a finite-fold MRDP breakthrough}
The parameter $T$ in \eqref{ste:wc:eq:quadraticiff} chooses a different polynomial
and witness dimension. It is not just another variable of one fixed
polynomial. Taking the union over $T$ gives the intended halting relation,
but does not exhibit a fixed finite-dimensional polynomial whose complete
natural witness is unique. Encoding all these finite tuples and applying
MRDP may introduce further auxiliary witnesses with no preserved
uniqueness theorem. We therefore make no claim about single-fold or
finite-fold ordinary Diophantine representation.

Similarly, the total degree two here concerns a horizon-indexed family over
natural coordinates. Replacing natural coordinates by unrestricted signed
integers invalidates the nonnegativity argument for \eqref{ste:wc:eq:cross}.
One cannot infer an analogous signed quadratic theorem merely by
reusing the same formal polynomial.

\section{A complete numerical example and adversarial boundary checks}
\label{ste:wc:sec:example}

\subsection{Countdown on one counter}
Take states $0,1$, with $1$ halting, and instruction
\[
 0:\ \test(0,1,0).
\]
Tag its zero branch by $1$ and its positive branch by $2$, so $B=3$.
For input $x=2$ the exact history is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}crrrl@{}}
\toprule
$t$ & state $q_t$ & counter $c_t$ & history $h_t$ & selected branch\\
\midrule
0&0&2&1&positive, tag 2\\
1&0&1&5&positive, tag 2\\
2&0&0&17&zero, tag 1\\
3&1&0&52&halt\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
There are $T=3$ transitions and $L=4$ configuration vertices. At $m=5$,
\[
 D_0,D_1,D_2,D_3,D_4=1,5,24,115,551.
\]
The primitive positive-rail potentials are $(115,24,5,1)$, with charge
$551$; negative-rail potentials are their negatives. The circuit equations
are visibly
\[
 5(115)-24=551,\quad5(24)-115-5=0,\quad
 5(5)-24-1=0,\quad5(1)-5=0.
\]
The unit-source effective resistance is
\[
 \mathcal R=\frac{230}{551},\qquad551=19\cdot29.
\]
This particular unit-source solution belongs to
$\Z[1/19,1/29]^{(W)}$, but not to $\Z[1/2,1/3,1/5]^{(W)}$.
It nevertheless has an integral scaled certificate with charge $551$,
illustrating the fixed-amplitude qualification in Section~\ref{ste:wc:sec:localization}.

For completeness the routed data are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}crrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$t$&$b_{t,1}$&$b_{t,2}$&$a_{t,1,0}$&$a_{t,2,0}$&$s_{t,2}$\\
\midrule
0&0&1&0&2&1\\
1&0&1&0&1&0\\
2&1&0&0&0&0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These tables and the electrical values specify all 32 natural witnesses.
There are 33 linear residuals and six cross terms. Expansion and collection
produce 105 distinct nonzero monomials, of degree at most two, counting the
input variable as part of the polynomial. The complete polynomial and its
entire witness are exported as JSON; no term is omitted from that artifact.

Using the code's explicit vertex enumeration, the nonzero primitive network
array is
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}r r r r@{}}
\toprule
positive vertex&value&negative vertex&value\\
\midrule
24&115&25&$-115$\\
152&24&153&$-24$\\
1216&5&1217&$-5$\\
11020&1&11021&$-1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Applying the full connected operator gives value $551$ at vertex $24$,
value $-551$ at vertex $25$, and zero at every other row, including all
attachment hubs and every exterior neighbor.

\subsection{The finite-principal-block trap}
Suppose instead that the program increments forever. Truncate the first
$L$ configurations and assign the same formal path values
$D_{L-i-1}$ to them. Every equation inside that principal block is satisfied
with charge $D_L$. But the actual next positive-rail vertex sees the
residual $-1$, because the last retained value is $1$; its negative partner
sees $+1$. The finite array is not a solution of the infinite system.

The verifier deliberately tests this failure for $1\leq L\leq19$.
All four nonzero residuals are checked: the two source values and the two
leakage values. A correct verifier must check exterior rows. A correct
finite arithmetic compiler must also pay for the terminal-halting test;
an invertible truncated circuit alone proves nothing about halting.

\subsection{Other exact boundary checks}
The tests include the initially halting case $T=0$, both sides of zero
branches, unused history digits, invalid inverse history candidates,
multiple positive scalings of primitive certificates, and computations
which never halt within the test horizon. They also check every single
natural-coordinate perturbation by $+1$ and every permitted perturbation
by $-1$ of the complete valid witnesses, including input parameters.
These checks catch unbound input coordinates, free private witnesses,
and several common boundary omissions. They are not substitutes for the
inductive soundness and uniqueness proofs.

\section{Reproducibility and a precise integration plan}
\label{ste:wc:sec:verification}

\subsection{What was executed}
The implementation uses Python's standard library, immutable program and
configuration records, arbitrary-precision integers, exact rational
arithmetic, and deterministic random seeds. Running
\begin{lstlisting}
python verify.py
\end{lstlisting}
from the unpacked directory regenerates the examples and
\texttt{verification.json}. The recorded run passed 38,524 assertions,
distributed among 26 test categories. Important groups are shown below;
the receipt contains the full list and exact counts.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Check group&Assertions\\
\midrule
Connected neighbor symmetry, degree, and rail involution&7,500\\
Lucas rank divisibility over five odd parameters and 25 primes&15,000\\
Exact prime-valuation lifting&1,867\\
Continuant equations and coprimality&2,475\\
Single-coordinate natural witness mutations&3,698\\
Signed polynomial expansion identities&600\\
Exhaustive small natural routing gates&819\\
All categories combined&38,524\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The polynomial identity tests allow signed assignments to compare two
algebraic expressions; the zero-set and nonnegativity tests use natural
assignments. This distinction is intentional. Determinant tests use an
independent rational Gaussian-elimination calculation, rather than merely
repeating the continuant recurrence. Full network evaluations compute all
neighbor rows, not just rows listed in the certificate.

The tests do not instantiate a particular published small universal
machine. They verify the generic compiler on several finite programs,
including one- and two-counter examples, initially halting programs,
unconditional divergence, and a program with both halting and divergent
inputs. The fixed-universal existence theorem is a mathematical composition
with a standard universal finite counter program, not an empirical
universality claim about these toy examples.

\wtag\ \emph{Review and shipped files.} The research programme's review~\cite{reviewCG} (commit \texttt{899391bde}) replayed this suite (38,524 checks) and found two defects in the reference API, not in the mathematics. The frozen \texttt{Certificate} keeps mutable row dictionaries and \texttt{export} returns the same objects, so mutating an export erases the live input-binding row; the review's fixture changes a wrong-input energy from 9409 to~0 (delivered \path{src/substrate.py}, lines 343--348 and 373--377). Prime deduplication by \texttt{set(primes)} runs before the primality checks, so \texttt{localization\_bound(5,[2,2.0])} accepts an inexact scalar (lines 285 and~306). Its tested repair is \path{well_conditioned_certificate_snapshots.patch} (SHA-256 \texttt{cdd6307d\ldots72f2a9}) in the research programme; it keeps every formula, passes the unchanged 38,524-check suite and reproduces all exports. The shipped \path{code/13-well-conditioned-substrate.py} is the unpatched original. In this report \path{verify.py} and \path{build.sh} are \path{code/13-well-conditioned-verify.py} and \path{code/13-well-conditioned-build.sh}, \path{src/substrate.py} is \path{code/13-well-conditioned-substrate.py}, the three examples and \path{verification.json} are \path{data/13-well-conditioned-*.json}, and \path{sources.md} is \path{13-well-conditioned-sources.md}; the PDF, the README and \path{SHA256SUMS} (11 of 11 verified at placement) are not shipped. \path{verify.py} imports \texttt{substrate} from a \path{src/} directory beside it and rewrites \path{examples/} and \path{verification.json} in its own directory, so it must run on a copy (README of this report).

\subsection{What remains unformalized}
No Lean or Rocq proof of this manuscript has been built. A suitable integration
sequence would first formalize the branch-history bijection and
finite-support module identities, then add the analytic and arithmetic
layers. The following proposed modules describe interfaces, not existing
completed source files:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
\toprule
Proposed module&Primary mathematical obligation\\
\midrule
\texttt{ReversibleHistory}&Injective step map and finite-rooted-path theorem.\\
\texttt{PathResolvent}&Continuant recurrence, exact Green column, integral fiber.\\
\texttt{ConnectedDipole}&Neighbor presentation, involution, antisymmetric lifting.\\
\texttt{CoerciveSupport}&Norm bounds, inverse, approximation, support equivalence.\\
\texttt{LucasLocalization}&Ranks, valuation lifting including two, effective bound.\\
\texttt{QuadraticCertificate}&Literal compiler, soundness, completeness, uniqueness.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The first, second, third, and last layers are largely finitary and can be
proved before analytic inversion. The analytic proof then shows why the
same finite certificate coexists with a uniformly well-posed continuous
problem. The existing MRDP interface can be applied to the final halting
predicate, but it should not be used to bypass the structural proofs or
claim preservation of primitive-witness uniqueness.

\subsection{Source and trust boundaries}
The archive includes the authored manuscript and code, not copies of the
repository, third-party articles, or font files. Repository citations are
pinned to the stated commit; the two earlier manuscript citations identify
the supplied research documents by title and date. The executable checks
and this manuscript's proofs have been reviewed within this work, but
have not received independent peer review or a proof-assistant check.
A literature search located classical antecedents but does not establish
priority for the combined theorem. This is a substantive proposed research
contribution with explicit claims and limitations, not a declaration that
a recognized longstanding conjecture has been settled.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{ste:wc:sec:questions}

The following questions go beyond the proved statements. They are proposed
research directions, not an assertion that every formulation already has
a recognized open-problem status in the literature.

\begin{question}[Finite geometric presentation]\label{ste:wc:q:geometric}
Can the connected degree-three construction be realized by a fixed
finite-range rule on a geometric lattice, with an explicitly paid ordinary
input loader, while retaining exact compact-support equivalence and a
uniform coercivity bound?
\end{question}
The current graph has an effective neighbor algorithm, but the vertex
pairing and backbone are not a translation-invariant local geometric
layout. An embedding must pay for wire routing, crossings, and source
placement; simply renaming a computable graph as a lattice would not answer
the question.

\begin{question}[Planarity with exact antisymmetric isolation]\label{ste:wc:q:planar}
Can one retain a connected planar graph, bounded degree, a finite integer
conductance alphabet, and the same two-terminal denominator law?
\end{question}
The two rails and their many backbone attachments do not come with a planar
embedding theorem. A useful approach would be a planar symmetry-preserving
crossing or isolation gadget whose Schur complement is exact and whose
introduced denominators are controlled.

\begin{question}[Infinite recursive prime localizations]\label{ste:wc:q:infinite}
For which computable infinite prime sets $S$ is
$\{x:A_m^{-1}b_x\in\Z[S^{-1}]^{(W)}\}$ decidable, complete, or of intermediate
computably enumerable degree?
\end{question}
The exact criterion is now concrete: it depends only on which continuants
$D_L$ have all prime factors in $S$. The finite-set proof supplies an
obstruction and a benchmark. Extending it requires control of infinitely
many rank constants, not merely a formal union of finite decision procedures.

\begin{question}[Prime-restricted charge without losing universality]\label{ste:wc:q:primecharge}
Can another coercive connected compiler realize universal finite-support
certificates while forcing every primitive charge to be a power of one
fixed prime?
\end{question}
Our Lucas-denominator family cannot do so by Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:Sbound}. A positive
answer would require a different recurrence, a different gadget, or another
way to normalize a primitive certificate. The question concerns this
structural design objective, not general Diophantine definability over a
localization.

\begin{question}[Exact constants at the optimal row threshold]\label{ste:wc:q:constants}
With four nonzeros per row and a fixed small integer coefficient alphabet,
what are the best simultaneous coercivity, upper spectral, and denominator
growth bounds for a connected universal compact-support compiler?
\end{question}
Increasing $m$ makes the normalized operator nearly the identity but changes
the alphabet and increases the charge growth. The tradeoff is not addressed
by the row-sparsity theorem alone. It would be useful to optimize it within
a rigorously specified gadget class before claiming a global optimum.

\begin{question}[Symmetry-breaking and robust replacements for exact support]\label{ste:wc:q:robust}
Which quantified perturbations preserve a computable reduction, even when
literal antisymmetry is lost? Can exact support be replaced by a precisely
specified arithmetic invariant that survives a wider class of perturbations?
\end{question}
Our backbone cancellation is exact. Generic perturbation need not preserve
it, and good analytic conditioning alone does not protect a zero-support
predicate. A theorem here must state the permitted perturbations and the
new exact decision problem rather than identify small numerical tails with
zero.

\begin{question}[A finite-dimensional uniqueness-preserving compression]\label{ste:wc:q:compression}
Can the canonical path charge and primitive finite-support certificate be
compressed into a fixed finite tuple of integer witnesses without introducing
uncontrolled multiplicities?
\end{question}
The horizon-indexed quadratic construction makes every finite witness
explicit but does not answer this question. Any successful compression
should distinguish the unique run, the unique primitive network array,
and auxiliary arithmetic witnesses introduced by sequence coding. Its
relation to finite-fold representation would have to be proved, not assumed.

\begin{question}[Other computable graphs and algebraic Green values]\label{ste:wc:q:algebraic}
Can one obtain a prescribed finite menu of algebraic nonhalting resistance
values, or higher arithmetical classifications of exact resistance properties,
while retaining a single fixed connected coercive graph?
\end{question}
The present path resolvent produces one rational-versus-quadratic dichotomy.
More elaborate computational subgraphs may produce richer algebraic behavior,
but their exact integral fibers and localization properties should be
analyzed alongside the real-valued observable.

\begin{question}[Effective versus nonuniform sparsity boundaries]\label{ste:wc:q:uniform}
What is the exact computational complexity of finite-support solvability
when a degree-two connected operator is supplied as part of the input,
rather than fixed in advance?
\end{question}
Theorem~\ref{ste:wc:thm:row3} deliberately allows an endpoint to be hardcoded for each
fixed ray. A uniform classification must pay for discovering endpoints or
certifying their absence. Separating these quantifier orders may lead to a
useful hierarchy of sparse computational presentations.

\begin{question}[Kernel-checked end-to-end extraction]\label{ste:wc:q:extraction}
Can ProveIt extract the complete natural quadratic polynomial, its witness
restoration map, and the connected-graph residual checker from a single
verified program specification?
\end{question}
The reference implementation already gives a concrete target, including
input zero, exact first-halting time, history inversion, prime two, and
exterior rows. An initial formal milestone could be the full countdown
example, followed by a parametric proof for the counter instruction type;
only then should a universal table and operation ledger be instantiated.

\section{Conclusion}
The central separation is between analytic well-posedness and algebraic
support. A single fixed connected grounded network can have a uniformly
computable bounded inverse, an explicit spectral gap from zero, and a
strongly convex energy, while its finite-support rational solutions encode
halting. The input is only a two-terminal dipole. The graph's maximum degree
is three, and the associated four-entry row bound is sharp in the fixed
connected scalar class considered here.

The exact primitive certificate exposes more than undecidability: its
charge is a specific Lucas continuant. That formula leads to a contrasting
effective theorem over every finite-prime localization, and to the absence
of any computable prime cutoff covering all halting certificates. A complete
horizon-indexed quadratic polynomial connects these infinite-network
statements to explicit finite natural arithmetic without disguising an
external horizon as a fixed witness dimension.

These results give a concrete, proof-oriented extension of the repository's
Diophantine-substrate program. Their next tests are independent mathematical
review, stronger geometric realizations, and formal extraction of the
compiler and its exact witness correspondences.

\appendix
\section{A fully instantiated horizon-two polynomial schema}
\label{ste:ee:app:tinyquartic}

\noindent\wtag\ Appendix~A of source~02.


For the three-state alphabet \eqref{ste:ee:eq:tiny}, let $e_{t,0},e_{t,R},e_{t,L}$ be the
natural selectors at time $t=1,2$. Its compressed information matrix is
\[
 A(e_t)=e_{t,0}H_0+e_{t,R}H_R+e_{t,L}H_L
 =3\begin{pmatrix}
 e_{t,R}+e_{t,L}&e_{t,R}\\
 e_{t,L}&e_{t,R}+e_{t,L}
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Let $U=\one_2\one_2\transpose$, $Y_0=I_2+U$, $p_0=1$, $p_1=39$, and
$p_2=1521$. The following is one explicit integer polynomial in fourteen natural
variables (six selectors and eight entries of $Y_1,Y_2$):
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal I_2={}&
 \sum_{t=1}^2(e_{t,0}+e_{t,R}+e_{t,L}-1)^2\\
 &+\sum_{t=1}^2\sum_{a,b=1}^2
 \left((Y_t)_{ab}-p_t-
       \bigl[A(e_t)(Y_{t-1}-p_{t-1}U)\bigr]_{ab}\right)^2\\
 &+\sum_{a,b=1}^2((Y_2)_{ab}-1521)^2.
\end{align*}
All finite sums, coefficients, dimensions, and constant matrices are specified;
expanding the squares is optional and does not change the polynomial. The word
$(0,L)$ gives selectors $(1,0,0)$ and $(0,0,1)$, with $Y_1=39U$ and $Y_2=1521U$.
Every displayed residual is then visibly zero. A word containing only $R,L$
produces an invertible transverse product and cannot satisfy the terminal
conditions.

This example illustrates the natural-domain uniqueness directly. A selector
assignment such as $(2,-1,0)$ is inadmissible, even though its entries sum to one;
using arbitrary integer selectors without additional conditions would invalidate
the one-hot proof. The checker enforces the natural domain exactly.

\section{A compact implementation contract}\label{ste:ec:app:contract}

\noindent\wtag\ Appendix~B of source~10.


\texttt{Program} validates a finite source table and natural initial
counters. \texttt{counter\_circuit} emits the absorbing or erasing map.
\texttt{layer\_circuit} inserts shared delay copies and pads all outputs.
\texttt{compile\_program} emits paired-channel actions, normalization
constants, and optional common reset and reward. \texttt{initialize}
emits the complete dyadic payoff vector.
(\wtag\ The review~\cite{review808} found that an explicitly supplied \emph{integer} scale in \texttt{Program.encode} is divided in floating point, so that a counter value $1075$ becomes $0.0$ instead of $2^{-1075}$; the default rational scale, used by every shipped loader and export, is exact. Its patch \path{exact_convergence_integer_scale.patch} normalizes integer or rational scales and rejects floating-point and Boolean ones. The shipped \path{code/10-exact-convergence-bellman_diophantine.py} is the unpatched original.)

\texttt{certificate} accepts the game, initial numerator vector and positive
denominator, a natural horizon, and either a scalar pair, consensus test,
or rational constant full-vector target. It emits every squared linear
form and complementary product, including all reward forcing coefficients.
Its metadata reports the exact witness count, scaling convention, and
endpoint-square count.

A JSON linear form is a list of integer pairs $(j,a_j)$; index $-1$ denotes
the constant monomial, and all other indices refer to the supplied variable
label array. A complementarity term is an index pair $(u,v)$ and means
$W_uW_v$, not a residual that still needs squaring. The complete polynomial
is the sum of the squares of all listed linear forms plus all listed
complementarity terms. This specifies an ordinary integer polynomial
without auxiliary syntax such as ``run,'' ``minimum,'' or ``there exists
a schedule.''

The independent checker evaluates that polynomial and, optionally,
reconstructs the game trajectory from the serialized stochastic rows.
Unsupported domains and malformed instructions are rejected. The implementation
is intended as a transparent research reference, not as a hardened service
for untrusted, resource-exhausting inputs.

The arithmetic compiler and interval routines themselves use only the Python standard library. The manuscript can be rebuilt with a conventional LaTeX installation containing the packages listed in its preamble:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
\wtag\ The delivered \path{build.sh} (shipped as \path{code/03-thermal-arithmetic-build.sh}) runs \emph{three} pdflatex passes, not the two shown. File names in the table above are the delivered ones; their shipped names are listed in Section~\ref{ste:ta:sec:package}. \path{verify.py} imports \texttt{compiler} by its delivered name and rewrites \path{examples.json} and \path{verification.json} in its own directory, so it must be run on a copy (README of this report); \path{article.tex} is printed as Part~\ref{ste:part:ta}, and the source's \path{README.md}, \path{article.pdf} and \path{SHA256SUMS.txt} are not shipped.

The verification receipt is generated by the delivered code. In particular, the optional accelerated ground-series path used for the near-boundary examples is identified in the implementation; it is not substituted for the general computability proof.

\section{Compiler and verifier specifications}\label{ste:cg:app:spec}

\noindent\wtag\ Appendix~A of source~12 (batch-79 manuscript~10).

\subsection{Local predecessor procedure}
For a target $(r,a,b,h)$, compute $(h',d)=\operatorname{divmod}(h,B)$.
If $d$ is not a branch tag, return ``no predecessor.'' Otherwise retrieve
its unique branch. Reject a target-state mismatch. Invert the counter
update and check its guard: an inverse increment requires a positive
target counter, an inverse zero branch requires a zero counter, and an
inverse decrement adds one. Return the resulting source only after its
forward step is checked to equal the target. Every rejected case is a
root candidate, not an error in the ambient vertex set.

\subsection{Sparse verification}
A verifier receives a validated program, $\alpha$, a source $s$, an integer
$c$, and a finite mapping $u$. It accumulates $\alpha u_v$ at each occupied
vertex, subtracts $u_v$ at each of its graph neighbors, and subtracts $c$
at $s$. It accepts exactly when every accumulated residual is zero and
the sum of coefficients at halting states is one. Because each occupied
vertex has at most two neighbors, only finitely many rows are generated,
including every row that can be nonzero. This procedure is polynomial in
the complete sparse binary input size.

\Needspace{17\baselineskip}
\subsection{Exact interval procedure}
\begin{lstlisting}
Input: fixed program, root source s, integer alpha >= 3, bits b >= 0
n := max(1, ceil(b/2))
Simulate n-1 transitions, testing for a halt at the final configuration.
If a halt occurs with k configurations:
    compute D_0,...,D_k and return [D_(k-1)/D_k, D_(k-1)/D_k]
Otherwise:
    lo := 0; hi := 1/2
    repeat n times:
        lo := 1/(alpha-lo)
        hi := 1/(alpha-hi)
    return [lo, hi]
\end{lstlisting}
All arithmetic in the supplied implementation is integer or rational.
The irrational endpoint is used only in the proof, never as a
floating-point oracle in the algorithm.

\section{The modular bound as executable pseudocode}
\label{ste:wc:app:algorithm}
\noindent\wtag\ Appendix~A of source~13 (batch-79 manuscript~14).

The proof of Corollary~\ref{ste:wc:cor:localdecide} is implemented by the following finite
procedure. Here \texttt{U(m,n)} is evaluated by the recurrence, not by
floating-point exponentiation.
\begin{lstlisting}
localization_bound(m, S):
    require m odd, m >= 3; require S a finite set of primes
    C = 1
    for p in S:
        find the first z >= 1 with U(m,z) = 0 mod p
        # An invertible pair recurrence guarantees z <= p*p - 1.
        c = max(0, valuation_p(U(m,z)) - valuation_p(z))
        C = C * p**c
    L = 1
    while (m-1)**L <= C*(L+1):
        L = L + 1
    return L

localized_unit_solution(P, input, m, S):
    B = localization_bound(m, S)
    if B <= 1: return false
    simulate P through transition number B-2
    if no halt occurred: return false
    L = number of configurations in that first-halting trace
    D = U(m,L+1)
    divide out every prime in S from D
    return (D == 1)
\end{lstlisting}
For the connected-network interpretation use odd $m\geq5$. The bound
itself is also valid for the disconnected $m=3$ Fibonacci specialization.

\section{Archive contents and rebuilding}\label{ste:wc:app:archive}

\noindent\wtag\ Appendix~B of source~13.

The archive is self-contained for the examples and verification. The
principal files are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}lX@{}}
\toprule
File&Purpose\\
\midrule
\texttt{article.tex}, \texttt{article.pdf}&This manuscript and its compiled version.\\
\texttt{src/substrate.py}&Counter compiler, connected graph, arithmetic and polynomial APIs.\\
\texttt{verify.py}&Deterministic exact verification and example regeneration.\\
\texttt{verification.json}&Actual test counts and worked-example metadata.\\
\texttt{examples/}&Full polynomial, full witness, and finite-prime bounds.\\
\texttt{README.md}&Commands, scope, and interpretation of the artifacts.\\
\texttt{sources.md}&Pinned source references and audit boundaries.\\
\texttt{build.sh}&Python verification followed by LaTeX compilation.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
A standard pdfLaTeX installation with the packages listed in the preamble
is sufficient. Run pdfLaTeX twice, or use \texttt{latexmk -pdf article.tex}.
The verification code requires no downloaded dependencies and makes no
network requests. The PDF is rendered and visually inspected separately
from mathematical verification.

\wtag\ Shipped names: see the note in Section~\ref{ste:wc:sec:verification}.
